<span style="color:#800000">ftA45</span> *Blarney* renders it “adversity.” That the word sometimes means that, is true, but most commonly wickedness; and this is the sense required by the context: it must be that which corresponds in character with the word that follows-apostasy, or turning aside. “ Wickedness” is the meaning sanctioned by all the early versions, as well as modern.-*Ed*.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#800000">ftA46</span> The word is singular in all the early versions. It is rendered “apostasy-</p>

<span style="color:#0000d4">ἀποστασία</span>,” by the *Septuagint,* and, “turning aside-aversio,” by the *Vulgate*. Though there is no MS. in favor of the singular, yet the verb connected with it is in that number. The true reading no doubt is according to the versions, confirmed as it is by the number of the verb.*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA47</span> The verse literally is as follows,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">19.Chastise thee shall thy wickedness, And thy apostasy, it</span> <span style="color:#000080">shall correct thee; Know then and see, That evil and bitter shall</span> <span style="color:#000080">be Thy forsaking of Jehovah thy God; And my fear is not in</span> <span style="color:#000080">thee, Saith the Lord, Jehovah of hosts.</span></p>

The future is spoken of. They were warned; they were to know and see, or consider, that the forsaking of God, “the apostasy,” would be afflictive and bitter: and then the cause of the “wickedness” first mentioned is stated, no “fear” of God. How “wickedness” was to chastise them, and “apostasy” to correct them, is signified,-they would turn out to be “evil”-affiictive-hurtful, and “bitter”-grievous-painfully distressing. Hence *Grotfius’s* exposition cannot be right-”Thy wickedness shall be a proof that thou art justly punished.” The reference is to the very evils and miseries to which their “wickedness” and “apostasy” would inevitably lead them. Their foreign alliances were eventually the means of their degradation and misery; and in seeking them, they forsook God as their protector; and by adopting idols, they forsook him as the object of their worship.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA48</span> On the authority of the *Septuagint* and of the *Vulgate, Blarney* has rendered this verb and the following in the second person, “thou hast broken, “ etc. There is no MS. that has this reading except one, and that as to the first verb only. The *Targum* and the *Syriac* retain the first person; but the *Arabic* the second. There is no necessity of a change, as *Blarney* intimates, arising from the *usus loquendi;* see Jeremiah 28:2; Isaiah 9:4. *Jerome* followed the

<!-- PAGE 1093 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">*Septuagint;* but all modern versions have adopted the Hebrew text. *Horsley* gives this version,-</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">20. Verily of old time I broke thy yoke, I burst thy bands</span> <span style="color:#000080">asunder; Yet thou saidst, I will not obey: Verily, upon every</span> <span style="color:#000080">high hill, And under every green tree, Thou layest thyself along,</span> <span style="color:#000080">playing the strumpet.</span></p>

*Blarney* having proposed to amend the last line, the Bishop justly says, “The text wants no correction.” The verb <span style="color:#008080">h[x</span> found only here, and in Jeremiah 48:12, and in Isaiah 51:14; Isaiah 63:1, means, according *to Buxtorff* and *Leigh,* to wander, to ramble, to travel up and down, and in a transitive sense, to cause to travel, or to migrate; but, according to *Parkhurst,* to stretch out, to lie along, and transitively to cause to be stretched out, that is, to throw down. The first meaning is more suitable to the passages referred to above. It is here a participle, preceded by a pronoun, “thou,” the way in which a present act is commonly expressed in Hebrew. The line may then be thus rendered,<span style="color:#000080">Thou ramblest, playing the strumpet.</span>

The *Targum* gives the meaning, though not the right tense, “Thou didst worship idols.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA49</span> The received Text has <span style="color:#008080">db[</span>, to serve, and the *Keri,* <span style="color:#008080">rb[</span>, to transgress. In favor of the latter there are about 30 MSS., while the rest of those examined by Kennicott (in all 198, 71 examined throughout, and 127 on particular parts) retain the former verb, and also all the early versions, the *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* the *Arabic,* and the *Vulgate*. The *Targum* only has the latter. *Piscator, Jun*. and *Trem*.*, Capellus, Blarney,* and *Horsley* decide with *Calvin* in favor of the former; while *Munster* and *Gataker* side with our version and that of *Geneva,* in which the latter has been adopted. Clearly the former has the weight of authority: and the contrast, too, is striking, “I have broken thy bonds of slavery; but thou hast refused to serve or obey me.” The former part of this verse is of the same purport with  Jeremiah 2:6, and the latter with Jeremiah 2:25. The verse begins with <span style="color:#008080">yk</span>, rendered “for” in our version, by *Calvin,* and many others, but “surely” by

<!-- PAGE 1094 -->

*Blarney,* and “verily” by *Horsley*. It is omitted in the *Vulgate*. Were it rendered “though,” the meaning would be more evident,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Though from old time I had broken thy yoke, I had burst thy</span> <span style="color:#000080">bands asunder; Yet thou hast said, “I will not obey:” For on</span> <span style="color:#000080">every high hill and under every green tree Thou ramblest,</span> <span style="color:#000080">playing the strumpet.-</span><span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA50</span> The word means not only the seed of vegetables, but whatever forms that from which anything grows. It is applied as a verb to the planting of shoots or cuttings in Isaiah 17:10. The proper rendering here would be,<span style="color:#000080">The whole of it a genuine plant (or shoot).</span>

What is rendered “choice vine,” <span style="color:#008080">qrwç</span> is the yellow vine; the best was so called, because it produced wine of that color.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA51</span> Much difference exists as to the literal meaning of this clause, though the general meaning is quite evident. None of the early versions are the same. The word <span style="color:#008080">yrws</span> is rendered, “into bitterness-<span style="color:#0000d4">eijv πικρίαν</span>,” by the *Septuagint;* “thou hast rebelled,” by the *Syriac;* “into what is corrupt-in pravurn,” by the *Vulgate;* “thou hast declined from my fear,” by the *Targum*. *Blarney* takes it as a verb in the imperative mood, and renders the two lines thus,<span style="color:#000080">Yet how I find thee changed! Depart, O vine of spurious growth.</span>

But there is a harshness and incongruity in this version that renders it inadmissible. Besides “vine of spurious growth” is not the meaning of the words used, for it is “a foreign vine,” that is, a heathen vine; which contains an allusion to the idolatry which had been imported from heathen nations.

It is most probable that <span style="color:#008080">yrws</span>, or in full, <span style="color:#008080">µyrws</span>, means degenerate shoots or branches, as *Parkhurst* thinks. To turn aside, to decline, to degenerate, seems to be the most common meaning of the verb. There would in this case be a congruity in the whole verse,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And I myself had planted thee a choice vine, The whole of it a</span> <span style="color:#000080">genuine plant; How then art thou become to me The degenerate</span> <span style="color:#000080">shoots of a foreign vine?</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1095 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The plant was of the best kind, but the shoots or the branches had become degenerated, such as a foreign or heathen vine produced.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA52</span> What we call “nitre” is different from the “nitron” here mentioned. The verb, from which the noun is derived, means to loosen, to set free: and hence the article called nitron dissolves in water, and loosens and washes away spots and stains. *Borith* was an herb, which, being burnt, and its ashes dissolved in water, had a strong cleansing power.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA53</span> The verb rendered “stamped” is only found here in Niphel, but, as a participial noun, it seems to mean gold stamped or marked to shew its genuineness. See Psalm 45:9; Proverbs 25:12. A stain or spot is not what it signifies, as given by the *Septuagint* and the *Vulgate,* nor “blot,” according to Blarney; but it refers to the stamp or mark imprinted on a hard metal, such as gold: and this idea alone corresponds with the other parts of the verse. A stain, a spot, or a blot, might be cleansed by abstergents, but not a mark stamped on a metal,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">But thou washest thyself with nitron, And multipliest for</span> <span style="color:#000080">thyself fuller’s ashes: Stamped is thine iniquity before me,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Saith the Lord Jehovah.-Ed.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA54</span> “The Jews, it seems,” says *Loath,* “had found out distinctions, whereby to reconcile the worship of the true God with those religious rites which they paid to the deities of the heathen, called here Baalim. These, they pretended, were only inferior demons or spirits, or the souls of men departed, and might be worshipped in subordination to the supreme God.” *Scott* adds to this quotation this just remark, “This, and nothing better, can the Papists urge in excuse of their manifest idolatry in worshipping saints and angels” -*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA55</span> The grammatical anomalies at the beginning of this verse are satisfactorily removed by *Parkhurst,* and what he has proposed is approved by *Horsley*. He considers <span style="color:#008080">hrp</span> to be the female dromedary, he derives <span style="color:#008080">dml</span> from <span style="color:#008080">dm</span>, measure, or extent, with a <span style="color:#008080">l</span> prefixed, and regards <span style="color:#008080">hçpn</span> as the true reading, being that of the *Keri,* and of the largest number of MSS. This verse and the preceding are to be thus connected,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">23. How canst thou say, “ I have not been polluted, After</span> <span style="color:#000080">Baalim have I not walked!” See thy way in the valley, Know</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1096 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">what thou hast done,- Like a swift dromedary which winds</span> <span style="color:#000080">about her courses,-</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">24.A female which, in the wide space of the wilderness,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Through the desire of her natural instinct, Snuffs up the wind</span> <span style="color:#000080">she meets with: Who can turn her back? All who seek her, Let</span> <span style="color:#000080">them not weary themselves; In her month they shall find her.</span></p>

By “winding about her courses,” or tracks, or ways, is meant running in this and in that direction, and not in a straight course. The word, as a noun, denotes the string or latchet by which the ancients fastened their sandals, and which they *twined* round the feet. “The wind she meets with,” is literally, “the wind of her meeting.” The *Septuagint* and the early versions have departed widely from the original; the *Vulgate* comes nearest to it; nor is the *Targum* far off-*Ed*.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#800000">ftA56</span> That the word means to be barefooted, or without shoes, is clear from</p>

Isaiah 20:2-4, and also from 2 Samuel 15:30: and it is nowhere else found except here. It being here a noun, it signifies literally barefootedness. They are here exhorted not to travel for aid to foreign lands, so as to wear out their shoes and thus become barefooted. This was said in contempt, in order to pour ridicule on their folly in seeking foreign aid.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA57</span> It has been disputed whether the negative “no,” refers to the advice given at the beginning of the verse, or to the immediately preceding word. The latter is the most natural. The word <span style="color:#008080">çawn</span> is a participle, as in Job 6:26. The verse may be thus rendered,<span style="color:#000080">25. Keep thy foot from being bare And thy throat from thirst; But</span> <span style="color:#000080">thou hast said, “ Hopeless! No; For I have loved strangers, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">after them will I go.”</span>

The first part implies that they were pursuing a useless course. The insolent answer was, “Is it hopeless? By no means.” The *Septuagint* omit the negative, and have only “<span style="color:#0000d4">ἀνδριοῦμαι</span>-I will act manfully;” and this version has been followed by the *Syriac* and *Arabic*. The *Vulgate* has, “desperavi, nequaquam faciam-I have despaired, I will by no means do *so*.” The most literal rendering is given above, and affords the best and the most suitable meaning.

<!-- PAGE 1097 -->

To confess that it was a hopeless thing to attempt to reform them, is not so appropriate, as to deny it to be hopeless to have recourse to foreign alliances: which seems to be the import of the passage. This is the view which *Gataker* seemed most inclined to take; and he mentions this rendering, “Should I despair? No.” To the same purpose is the version of *Jun*. and *Trem*. But *Grotius, Henry,* and *Adam Clarke,* agree with the explanation of *Calvin*.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA58</span> The verb rendered “is ashamed,” is in the past tense in Huphal, and means “made ashamed,” or, “confounded,” as rendered, by the *Targum* and the *Vulgate*. The *Septuagint* have converted it into the future tense, and so have the *Syriac* and the *Arabic,* which have been followed by most modern versions, and by commentators. If we rightly view the whole passage, we shall see reason to take this verb as we find it, in the past tense. The verse is an answer, as it were, to what is contained in the latter part of the previous verse, by a reference to what had already taken place as to the people of Judah; and the 30th verse (Jeremiah 2:30) countenances the past tense. This and the following verse may be thus rendered,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">26.As a thief is ashamed when he is found out, So made</span> <span style="color:#000080">ashamed have been the house of Israel, They, their kings, their</span> <span style="color:#000080">princes, Their priests and their prophets;</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">27.Who have said to the wood, “My father art thou,” And to</span> <span style="color:#000080">the stone, “Thou hast begotten me.” Though they have turned</span> <span style="color:#000080">to me the back and not the face; Yet in the time of their</span> <span style="color:#000080">calamity, They say, “Arise and save us.”</span></p>

The participles in Hebrew are regulated as to their tense by the verbs in the passage. Hence <span style="color:#008080">µyrma</span> in Jeremiah 2:27, is to be in the same tense with the previous verb. The future in the last line is to be in the present, as it expresses what was commonly done. Then what was usually said to them is mentioned in the following verse,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">28.But where are thy gods, which thou hast made for thyself?</span> <span style="color:#000080">Let them arise, if they can save thee In the time of thy</span> <span style="color:#000080">calamity: For according to the number of thy cities Have been</span> <span style="color:#000080">thy gods, O Judah.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1098 -->

*Blayney* has kept to the past tense as to the last line, and also as to the beginning of Jeremiah 2:26.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA59</span> The words employed by *Calvin* are the technical terms, *latria* and *dulia,* the fictions of the Papists. The first means specifically worship, and the second, service, obedience. The verb <span style="color:#0000d4">δουλεύω</span> in the New Testament is never used in the sense of worshipping or adoring, but of serving and obeying: but to bow to images or to kiss them, is an act of adoration, and not of service.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA60</span> The “neck” here means evidently the hinder part, for it is in contrast with “ face;” and the word generally means the hinder part. Hence it is properly rendered here “back” in our version and by *Blayney,* and so by the *Targum* and the early versions, except the *Syriac,* which retains the hinder part of the neck. We have no single word, except it be nape, which denotes the back part of the neck. There is one in *Welsh,* “ gwegil, “ and so in *Latin,* “ cervix, “ and in *Greek,* <span style="color:#0000d4">ἀυχὴν</span>. But the *Septuagint* have adopted here “<span style="color:#0000d4">νῶτα</span>-backs.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA61</span> The verb rendered “plead” in our version, is followed by <span style="color:#008080">la</span>, against or in opposition to. There are two other instances, Judges 21:22; Job 33:13. Our version in Job is, “Why dost thou strive against him?” The most suitable rendering of this passage is, <span style="color:#000080">Why should ye contend against (or, with) me?</span>

Then follows a fact sufficient to put an end to all contention,<span style="color:#000080">All of you have rebelled against me, Saith Jehovah.</span>

The primary idea of <span style="color:#008080">[çp</span> is, to go, to pass, to march on. See Isaiah 28:4. Its meaning depends on the preposition which follows it. Followed by <span style="color:#008080">l[</span>, over, it means to transgress, it being a *going* or *passing over* the limits set by the law, Hosea 8:1,-by <span style="color:#008080">m</span>, to go from, to revolt, to apostatize, 2 Kings 8:22,-and by <span style="color:#008080">b</span>, to go against, to rebel, as in this passage. Hence the noun has attained various meanings-transgression, apostasy, and rebellion. Its precise meaning in any case is to be determined by the context. *Gataker* and *Blayhey* render the verb here the same,<span style="color:#000080">All of you have rebelled against me, saith Jehovah.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1099 -->

The early versions vary. The *Septuagint* have “<span style="color:#0000d4">ἠσεβήσατε</span>-ye have acted impiously,” the *Syriac,* “ye have denied me,”-the *Arabic*, “ye have sinned against me,”-and the *Vulgate*, “ye have forsaken me.” The general idea is the same, but the specific one is that of rebelling against God.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA62</span> *Blayney* renders the word “instruction.” The *Septuagint* have “<span style="color:#0000d4">παιδείαν</span>-discipline: “the *Syriac, Vulgate,* and the *Targum* are the same; but the *Arabic* has “instruction-eruditionem.” The strict meaning of the word <span style="color:#008080">rswm</span>, is restraint, check, discipline, correction. Not to receive restraint or correction, is not to be thereby improved or reformed, but to proceed in the same course, see Jeremiah 5:3. The word has also a secondary meaning, instruction, as the effect of correction, see Zephaniah 3:7. But here it clearly means correction.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA63</span> The beginning of this verse literally is “The age, ye,” that is, “Ye of this age,” or generation. He was speaking before more especially of the preceding age. He now appeals to the people of that generation,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Ye of this age, see, spoken hath Jehovah,- Have I been a</span> <span style="color:#000080">wilderness to Israel, Or a land of darkness? Why have they</span> <span style="color:#000080">said, even my people, “We have ruled, we will no more come to</span> <span style="color:#000080">thee?”</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1100 -->
The above rendering of the latter part of the first line is favored by the *Septuagint, “*Hear ye the word of the Lord; thus saith the Lord.” The *Arabic* is the same. The *Vulgate* has, “See the word of the Lord,”-and the *Syriac, “*Hear the word of the Lord.’ *Blayney* renders thus, “Behold ye the cause of Jehovah.” *Gataker* takes “see” in the sense of considering, “See, “or seriously consider, “the word of the Lord.” The particle <span style="color:#008080">µa</span> after <span style="color:#008080">h</span>, may be rendered “or,” as in the *Syriac*. See Joshua 5:13. The word <span style="color:#008080">hylpam</span> is found in two MSS., <span style="color:#008080">hlypam</span>, which seems to be the true reading, countenanced by the *Targum,* and all the early versions, except the *Vulgate,* which has “serotina-lateward.” Darkness is a common metaphor for wretchedness and misery “We have ruled” is the literal rendering of <span style="color:#008080">wndr</span>, and there is no other reading. The *Septuagint* gives the same meaning, though the form is different, “We shall not be lorded over-<span style="color:#0000d4">ouj κυριευθησόμεθα</span>.” The *Arabic* is the same. It is the language of proud independence. The *Targum,* the *Vulgate,* and the *Syriac* have mistaken the verb for <span style="color:#008080">wndry</span>, which means, to descend, to come down, to bring down. *Blayney* gives the correct idea, “We are our own masters, “which *Horsley* approves. The preterite in Hebrew often includes the present; so the full meaning is, “We have ruled and do rule.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA64</span> The second word, <span style="color:#008080">hlk</span>, is rendered “sponsa-a bride,” in our version, *by Calvin* and *Blayney,* and so by the *Vulgate, Syriac,* and the *Targum,* but by the *Septuagint,* “<span style="color:#0000d4">παρθένος</span>-a virgin:” and *Parkhurst* says that it never means a bride. The version then ought to be, <span style="color:#000080">Can a maid forget her ornaments, A virgin her bands?</span>

That the word <span style="color:#008080">µyrçq</span> means bands of some kind is evident, as the verb signifies to bind, to join closely. Bands or bandage for the breast<span style="color:#0000d4">στηθοδεσμίδα</span>, is the version of the *Septuagint;* the *Arabic* and the *Vulgate* are the same. *Parkhurst* considers that “head-bands” are meant. The word is found also in Isaiah 3:20; where the *Septuagint* render it “<span style="color:#0000d4">δακτυλίοις</span>-rings,” and the *Targum,* “murenulas-chains,” which were of gold, and worn around the neck. For any practical purpose it is only necessary to know that they were embellishments which young women delighted in: and women in every age are too fond of such things, and men too; but the case is introduced here only for the sake of illustration.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA65</span> The exposition of this verse is no doubt materially correct. The words have been variously rendered, On the first clause there is a general agreement, The verb “taught” in the second, is in the first person in the received text; and to this reading *Blayney* gives the preference, and thus renders the line,<span style="color:#000080">Therefore also have I taught calamities thy ways.</span>

That is, “that God had directed calamities where to find them.” But this, is rather a remote idea. In favor of the second person, “thou hast taught, “are several MSS., all the early versions and the *Targum;* and it is what has been by most adopted. “The wicked ones” of our version is a rendering not countenanced by any of the ancient versions, nor by the *Targum;* all render it evil or evils or wickednesses.-*Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1101 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftA66</span> Our version of this text seems on the whole the best. “Blood,” <span style="color:#008080">µd</span>, is to be taken here in a collective sense, as the verb to which it belongs is plural. Instead of “poor innocents, “it ought rather to be “the innocent poor, “as the noun in Hebrew generally precedes its adjective. “Found” is in the first person, and there is no different reading, and it is so in the *Septuagint,* and the *Vulgate,* though the *Syriac* and *Arabic* give the second person, and the *Targum* the third person plural, as *Calvin* does. The last word is rendered “these” in the *Vulgate* and the *Targum;* but “oak” in the *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* and the *Arabic,* and adopted by *Blayney,* but disapproved by *Houbigant* and *Horsley*. As to the word, rendered in our version, “secret search,” the early versions have pit, pits, or ditches, and so the *Targum*. *Blayney* renders it “a digged hole, “of which *Horsley* approves; and he refers, as an illustration, to Leviticus 17:13, and to Ezekiel 24:7. The word means digging, and seems to be used here metaphorically for searching; there is no need of adding “secret” to it,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Also in thy skirts has been found The blood of the souls of the</span> <span style="color:#000080">innocent poor: Not by searching have I found it, But upon all</span> <span style="color:#000080">these (i.e., skirts.)</span></p>

The reference is to what is said in Jeremiah 2:30, where the Jews are charged with the killing the prophets. As to “the blood, “we find a similar passage in Ezekiel 24:7, 8.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA67</span> The literal rendering of this verse is as follows:-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">35. And thou hast said, “Verily I have been innocent; Surely</span> <span style="color:#000080">turned away has he his anger from me: “ Behold I will contend</span> <span style="color:#000080">in judgment with thee, On account of thy saying, “I have not</span> <span style="color:#000080">sinned.”</span></p>

The *Septuagint* have rendered the second line, “Let his anger be turned away from me;” the *Vulgate* and the *Arabic* are the same. The *Syriac* is, “therefore he turns away his anger from me.” “Turned away is his anger, “is the *Targum, Piscator, Jun*. and *Trem*. *Blayney* renders it,<span style="color:#000080">Surely his wrath shall turn from me.</span>

There is no reason for construing the verb in the future tense, or in the imperative mood. It is in the past tense, and there is no other reading. The claim of innocency is made on the supposition that God had turned away his displeasure. Hence the declaration that follows-that <!-- PAGE 1102 --> God would contest the matter-would bring it as it were into trial, as the verb here when in Niphal means.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA68</span> The idea of gadding, or of running here and there, is not countenanced by any of the early versions. The notion of vileness or degradation is what the versions convey. The *Vulgate* is,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Quinn vilis factus es nimis, iterans vias tuas! How extremely</span> <span style="color:#000080">worthless art thou become, iterating thy ways!</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The other versions are nearly of the same general import. *Blayney’s* version is,-</p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Why wilt thou make thyself exceedingly vile, In repeating over</span> <span style="color:#000080">again thy ways?</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Modern critics have considered the verb to be <span style="color:#008080">lza</span>, and not <span style="color:#008080">lz</span>. It no doubt may be either. As shame is threatened at the end of the verse, the latter verb is the most suitable,<span style="color:#000080">Why shouldest thou become wholly degraded By repeating thy</span> <span style="color:#000080">course? Even by Egypt shalt thou be put to shame, As thou hast</span> <span style="color:#000080">been put to shame by Assyria.</span></p>

“Course,” or way, means here a proceeding, and to repeat it is to pursue a course similar to what had been previously adopted.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA69</span> There are three other expositions of the words rendered by *Calvin,* “on this account.” One is that of our version, “from him;” the second is, “from hence,” i.e., from Egypt, adopted by *Piscator, Grotius,* and *Blayney;* and the third is, “from here,” i.e., from this place, their own land; which, as *Gataker* says, is probably “the genuine sense:” it is a threatening, that they were to be led into captivity. The rendering of the *Septuagint* is, “<span style="color:#0000d4">ἐντεῦθεν</span>-from hence,” or from this place; of the *Vulgate, “*ab ista-from that,” meaning, evidently Egypt; of the *Syriac* and *Targum, “*ex hoc-from this;” and of *Arabic, “*illinc-from thence.” The particle <span style="color:#008080">hz</span> is “this,” and not “that.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA70</span> “The gesture” mentioned here, a striking example of, we find in 2 Samuel 13:19. Many consider the <span style="color:#008080">w</span> here as having the meaning of “with,” and render the line as *Blayney* does,<span style="color:#000080">With thy hands upon thy head.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1103 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">But more consistent with the genius of the language is to regard the auxiliary verb to be understood,<span style="color:#000080">And thy hands shall be on thy head.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">There is a similar phrase in Isaiah 35:10, which ought to be rendered thus,<span style="color:#000080">And everlasting joy shall be upon their heads.-Ed.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA71</span> The verb for “abhor” is <span style="color:#008080">µam</span>, which means to reject, that is, with disdain and contempt; and the same when followed by <span style="color:#008080">b</span>, though often rendered “despise” in our version. It is rendered “reject, “without the <span style="color:#008080">b</span>, in 1 Samuel 15:23; Jeremiah 7:29; and “despise” being followed by <span style="color:#008080">b</span> in Judges 9:38; Jeremiah 4:30. The early versions and the *Targum* mostly differ, and none of them give the specific meaning of the verb, except that the *Septuagint* give its meaning when not followed by <span style="color:#008080">b</span>, “<span style="color:#0000d4">ἀπώσατο</span>-has rejected.” The whole verse may be thus rendered,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">37.Also from this place shalt thou go forth, And thy hands</span> <span style="color:#000080">shall be on thy head: For rejected has Jehovah those in whom</span> <span style="color:#000080">thou trustest, And thou shalt not prosper by them.</span></p>

It is not correct to render <span style="color:#008080">˚yjfbm</span>, “thy confidences;” for the word means “thy confided ones, “it being a Huphal participle. The *Syriac* renders it, “those who afford thee confidence-fiduciam tibi praebentibus.” *Blayney’s* version is, “the objects of thy trust;” and he translates the verb, “reprobated.” That this is its meaning when followed by <span style="color:#008080">b</span> is evident from Jeremiah 6:30.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA72</span> The word at the beginning of this verse has puzzled most, the form being so unusual. It is left out by the *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* and the *Arabic*. The *Vulgate* has “vulgo dieitur-it is commonly said.” But <span style="color:#008080">l</span> means at times “according to;” and it may be so rendered here,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">According to what is said, If a man sends away his wife, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">she goes from him and becomes another man’s, Is he to return</span> <span style="color:#000080">to her again? Polluted, shall it not be polluted, even that land?</span> <span style="color:#000080">But thou hast played the harlot with many friends, Yet return</span> <span style="color:#000080">to me, saith Jehovah.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1104 -->

The particle <span style="color:#008080">ˆh</span> in the first line is Chaldee for <span style="color:#008080">µa</span>; it is so rendered by the *Targun* and the early versions. The pronoun <span style="color:#008080">ayhh</span> after “land” cannot be rendered as *Calvin* proposes; it agrees in gender with “land.” It is singular that the *Septuagint,* the *Vulgate,* and the *Arabic,* have “woman” instead of “land;” yet *the Syriac* and *Targum* retain “land: “but in them all this pronoun is construed with the noun. *Gataker* takes “land” here, and in Deuteronomy 24:4, as meaning “the state, “the community, and refers to Numbers 35:33; <span class="ages-code">&lt;19A638&gt;</span>Psalm 106:38; Isaiah 24:5.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA73</span> The *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* and the *Arabic,* have by a mistake rendered the word “pastors” or shepherds; but the *Vulgate* has “lovers, “which our version and *Blayney* have adopted. But the word means companions, friends, intimates, neighbors. *Gataker* renders it “mates.”*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA74</span> *Gataker* suggests another idea,-that the reference is made to the Arabian traders, who fix their tents in the wilderness to wait for the merchants. *Blayney* renders the lines differently,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Lift up thine eyes upon the open plains, and see; Where hast</span> <span style="color:#000080">thou not been defiled in the highways? Thou hast sat waiting in</span> <span style="color:#000080">them like an Arabian in the desert.</span></p>

To render <span style="color:#008080">µypç</span>, “open plains,” is without authority; it means “craggy eminences, “or elevated places. See Numbers 23:3; Isaiah 41:18; Jeremiah 14:6. The division, too, is arbitrary. “The ways,” or highways, connects better with the following verb; and <span style="color:#008080">µhl</span> is not “in them, “but to or for them, that is, her lovers, mentioned in the preceding verse. Our version is the most suitable, with which that of *Calvin* corresponds.

“Arabian” is rendered “crow” by the *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* and the *Arabic; “*robber” by the *Vulgate,* but “Arabian” by the *Targum*. It is true that the word for a crow is from the same root, but the *iod* attached to it shews it to be a proper name. Where the *Vulgate* got the word “robber, “it is hard to know.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA75</span> It is usual to render the <span style="color:#008080">w</span> before “restrained,” “therefore;” but the sentence will read better, connected as it is with the latter part of the previous verse, by giving it its most common meaning,-

<!-- PAGE 1105 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">And restrained have been the showers, And the latter rain has</span> <span style="color:#000080">not been; Yet the front of a wanton woman hast thou had,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Thou hast refused to be made ashamed.</span></p>

This last verb is in the Infinitive Huphal. It means in Hiphil, to make ashamed; and then in Huphal, to be made ashamed. The *Targum* expresses thus the general sense of the last line, “Thou hast been unwilling to humble thyself.” The rest of the verse is rendered almost literally. The *Septuagint* and the *Arabic* wander very far from the Hebrew. The *Vulgate* is a literal version, and the *Syriac* is nearly so, only it connects “wickedness, “in the last verse, with restrained, thus,<span style="color:#000080">And for thy wickedness have been restrained the dews.</span>

And it is not improbable but that this was the original reading.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA76</span> This and the preceding verse have been variously explained. The view given by *Calvin* has been most commonly adopted; but it is hardly consistent with a literal rendering of the original, which I consider to be as follows,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">4.Hast thou not from this time called to me, “ My Father, the</span> <span style="color:#000080">guide of my youth art thou:</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">5.Will he reserve wrath for ever, Or keep it to the end?”</span> <span style="color:#000080">Behold, thou hast so spoken, And hast done evils and</span> <span style="color:#000080">persevered.</span></p>

“From this time,” that is, the time spoken of before, when the people followed idolatry. During this time, they called God their Father, and promised themselves the remittance of his displeasure. They said this, and yet followed their superstitions. This is the view which *Gataker* seemed most disposed to take. *Horsley* thus paraphrases the last line,<span style="color:#000080">“ Thou hast persisted incorrigibly in doing evil.”</span>

<!-- PAGE 1106 -->
The *Septuagint* give “called,” in the past tense; the *Vulgate,* in the imperative, “voca-call;” the *Syriac,* the *Arabic,* and the *Targum,* in the future tense, “Wilt thou not call,” etc. The received text has <span style="color:#008080">ytarq</span>, which is no doubt wrong; the *iod* is not found in very many MSS., and all the early versions agree in giving the verb in the second person. The same is to be said of <span style="color:#008080">ytrbd</span>, it ought to be <span style="color:#008080">trbd</span>, though *Horsley* prefers the former; but neither the early versions nor the context favor it. The phrase <span style="color:#008080">ht[m</span> is rendered by the *Septuagint,* “<span style="color:#0000d4">wJs οἰκόν</span>-as a house,” and by the *Arabic, “*ut filia-as a daughter.” How such mistakes could have been made, it is difficult to say. The *Syriac* has “hereafter;” and the *Targum, “*from this time.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA77</span> It is correctly rendered as a noun, for had it been an adjective or a participle, it would have followed the word Israel. Literally it is, “the apostates,”<span style="color:#000080">Hast thou seen what she did, the apostates Israel?</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Or, it may be rendered, “the backslider Israel,” though the word is deficient, having no feminine termination.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA78</span> The difficulty at the beginning of the eighth verse may be removed either by adopting <span style="color:#008080">haraw</span>, as in two MSS., and taking the verb to be in Hiphil, or <span style="color:#008080">artw</span>, as in one MS., as a repetition of the former verb, according to the *Syriac*. It is left out in the *Vulgate*. But it is most suitable to the context to take the verb to be in Hiphil. Then the passage would read thus,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">6.Hast thou not seen what she did, the apostate Israel? Go did</span> <span style="color:#000080">she on every high hill and under every green tree, And play</span> <span style="color:#000080">there the harlot:</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">7.And I said, after she had done all these things, “To me</span> <span style="color:#000080">return;” but she returned not: And see this did the hypocrite,</span> <span style="color:#000080">her sister Judah:</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">8.And I caused her to see, that on all these accounts, As</span> <span style="color:#000080">adultery the apostate Israel would commit, I dismissed her, and</span> <span style="color:#000080">gave to her The bill of her divorcement; Yet fear not, did the</span> <span style="color:#000080">hypocrite Judah, her sister, But went and played the harlot,</span> <span style="color:#000080">even herself.</span></p>

“On all these accounts,” or, for all these reasons, refers to several things-the first apostasy-God’s invitation-and Israel’s refusal. God caused Judah to see these things by his prophets, but Judah feared not. The word <span style="color:#008080">hdgb</span>, hypocrite, or the perfidious one, is a feminine participle, used as a noun. It is explained in the tenth verse by <!-- PAGE 1107 --> “feignedly” or falsely. Hypocrites would be the correct rendering. It is rendered by the *Septuagint, “*faithless-<span style="color:#0000d4">ασύνθετος</span>,”-by the *Vulgate,* “prevaricatress-praevaricatrix, “and by the *Targum, “*falsifier” or cheat.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA79</span> This verse may be thus rendered,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And it was, that through the report of her fornication, She</span> <span style="color:#000080">polluted the land; And she committed adultery with stone and</span> <span style="color:#000080">wood.</span></p>

There is no instance of <span style="color:#008080">lq</span>, in the sense of swiftness, etc., being used as a noun. It is the Chaldee for <span style="color:#008080">lwq</span>, voice, fame, report. *Gataker* paraphrases the words thus, “by her notorious fornication.” The early versions and the *Targum* all differ. Excessive addiction to idolatry is evidently what is spoken of.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA80</span> This verse stands connected, not with the preceding, but with the eighth,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Yea, even for all this, Return to me did not the hypocrite, Her</span> <span style="color:#000080">sister Judah, with all her heart, But in falsehood, saith Jehovah.</span></p>

“In falsehood,” or, by dealing falsely, as it may be taken by a participle preceded by a preposition.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA81</span> This is the literal expression, but the word <span style="color:#008080">çpn</span> is often taken for oneself, and ought often to be so rendered. See Numbers 30:5; Job 18:4; Psalm 7:2; God is said to swear by his soul, that is, by himself, Amos 6:8-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Then said Jehovah to me,- Justified herself hath apostate Israel,</span> <span style="color:#000080">More than the hypocrite Judah.</span></p>

Manifest and open apostasy is more honest than the double dealing of hypocrites, who combine God’s worship with idolatry; nor is it so hateful to God.-*Ed*.

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#800000">ftA82</span> <span style="color:#000080">12.Go and proclaim these words towards the north, and</span> <span style="color:#000080">say,- Return, apostate Israel, saith Jehovah; I will not cause my</span> <span style="color:#000080">wrath to fall on you, For merciful am I, saith Jehovah; I will not</span> <span style="color:#000080">reserve it for ever.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1108 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">That <span style="color:#008080">ynp</span>, commonly rendered “face,” means sometimes wrath or anger, is evident, see Psalm 21:9; Lamentations 4:16. God is said to have his face against the wicked, Psalm 34:16, and to make his face to shine on his people, Psalm 80:3. This accounts for the word being taken sometimes, as it were, in a bad sense: He has an angry as well as a smiling face.</p>

The rendering of the *Septuagint* is, “I will not set firm (<span style="color:#0000d4">στηριῶ</span>) my face upon you,” of the *Vulgate, “*I will not turn away my face from you,” of the *Syriac* and *Arabic, “*I will not harden my face against you,” and of the *Targum, “*I will not send my wrath upon you.” The last comes nearest to the Hebrew.

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">*Blayney’s* version is a paraphrase,-</p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">I will not look down upon you with a lowering brow; and so is</span> <span style="color:#000080">his version of the last line,- I will not keep displeasure in view</span> <span style="color:#000080">for ever.</span></p>

Our version in both instances is much to be preferred.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA83</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">But yet know thine iniquity, That against Jehovah thy God</span> <span style="color:#000080">hast thou rebelled; For thou hast diversified thy ways for</span> <span style="color:#000080">strangers, Under every green tree; And to my voice ye</span> <span style="color:#000080">hearkened not, saith Jehovah.</span></p>

The word <span style="color:#008080">˚a</span> is rendered by the early versions and the *Targum,* But, or But yet, or Nevertheless, “Verum,” “Verumtamen.” The third line is thus explained by *Parkhurst, “*Thou hast run after *various* heathen nations in their *several* idolatries.” And this they did, while they refused to attend to the voice of God. To attend to, rather than to obey, is what is meant. So the *Vulgate* and the *Syriac, “*My voice ye heard not,” or, as the former, “thou didst not hear.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA84</span> Nor is there an instance of such a meaning. Literally it is, “For I have been married with (or to) thee.” When this verb is followed by <span style="color:#008080">k</span>, as in Jeremiah 31:32, this is its meaning; but when followed by <span style="color:#008080">l</span>, as in 1 Chronicles 4:22, it means to rule, to exercise dominion. The *Vulgate* is, For I am thy husband.” The *Targum* gives the meaning,

<!-- PAGE 1109 -->

“For I have chosen you.” The *Septuagint* went astray, “For I will rule over you.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA85</span> The word is taken sometimes in a limited sense, and means what we understand by family: but it has here evidently a more extended meaning, and signifies a tribe, a community; for it includes more than a city. Such is its meaning in Jeremiah 8:3; and in Amos 3:l, it comprehends the whole community of Israel. It is rendered “<span style="color:#0000d4">ἐκ</span> <span style="color:#0000d4">πατριᾶς</span>,-from a tribe,” by the *Septuagint,* but improperly; “kindred, “by the *Vulgate* and the *Targum*. It no doubt means sometimes kindred, but not evidently in this place.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA86</span> Rather, “itself;” for the word often rendered soul, has sometimes this meaning. See note on Jeremaih 3:11.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA87</span> *Blayney,* following the *Targurn,* renders pastors “rulers,” and feed, “rule:” but this is to interpret and not to translate, as the words have never strictly these meanings, though what are sometimes to be understood by pastors are rulers, and by feeding, ruling or governing. But the interpretation in this instance seems not to be correct, and for the reason here assigned by *Calvin*. It is indeed the opinion of *Henry, Scott, Adam Clarke,* and others, that both civil and ecclesiastical pastors are intended; and if so, “knowledge” may be applied to the latter, and “wisdom” to the former. The *Septuagint* have omitted “wisdom,” and retain only “knowledge.” The *Targum* has “knowledge and wisdom;” the *Vulgate, “*knowledge and doctrine;” the *Syriac,* “knowledge and prudence;” and *Blayney, “*knowledge and discretion.” The verb <span style="color:#008080">h[r</span> means “to feed on, “as in Isaiah 44:20, or, as here, to “feed with.” It means also to “feed itself” as a beast does, Isaiah 11:7.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA88</span> The literal rendering of this verse I conceive to be the following,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">16.And it shall be, that ye shall multiply And be fruitful in the</span> <span style="color:#000080">land. In those days, saith Jehovah, they shall no more say,- “</span> <span style="color:#000080">The Ark of the Covenant of Jehovah;” And it shall not come to</span> <span style="color:#000080">their mind, Nor shall they mention it, nor visit it; And not made</span> <span style="color:#000080">shall it be any more.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The two first lines seem connected with the former verse. There is a gradation in the three last lines,-It shall not be thought of, much less mentioned or named, as given by the *Septuagint*,-still less be visited,-</p>

<!-- PAGE 1110 -->

and much less still, be made. This gradation is destroyed by *Blayney by* rendering the first line, <span style="color:#000080">Nor shall it be the delight of their heart;</span>

literally it is, “And it shall not ascend on the heart.” The “heart” means often in Hebrew the mind; and “to come to mind” is the idea; it would not be thought of. The phrase occurs in this book in two other places, Jeremiah 7:31; Jeremiah 44:21; and, not to think, or, not to come to mind, is the most suitable meaning, as it is given in our version. The purpose of this kind of gradation is to render the thing more certain and indisputable, so that there might be no room for doubt.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA89</span> This seems to be the best rendering. *Blayney* leaves out the word “Jerusalem;” but for no sufficient reason. The whole verse is as follows:-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">In that day call shall they Jerusalem, “ The throne of Jehovah;”</span> <span style="color:#000080">And gathered into it shall be all the nations, For the name of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah, even to Jerusalem; Anti they shall no more walk</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">After the resolutions of their wicked heart.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA90</span> “Evil” is connected with “hardness;” but it belongs properly to “their heart.” The word rendered “hardness” is <span style="color:#008080">twrrç</span> a plural noun, found eight times in Jeremiah; here, and in Jeremiah 7:24; Jeremiah 9:14; Jeremiah 11:8; Jeremiah 13:10; Jeremiah 16:12; Jeremiah 18:12; Jeremiah 23:17;

<!-- PAGE 1111 -->
and twice elsewhere,  Deuteronomy 29:19; Psalm 81:12. It is so variously rendered by the *Septuagint,* that they evidently did not understand its specific meaning; <span style="color:#0000d4">ἐνθυμήματα</span>, thoughts, devices; <span style="color:#0000d4">τὰ άρεστα</span>, things pleasing or agreeable; <span style="color:#0000d4">πλάνη</span>, wandering. These words are used for it in Jeremiah. The *Vulgate* ever renders it “pravitas-pravity-wickedness.” The *Targum* uniformly renders it “cogitatio-thought or imagination.” The latter word is used in our version, (except in Psalm 81:12, “lust,”) with a marginal reading “stubbornness.” The *Syriac* and *Arabic* vary as much as the *Septuagint,* “appetites, lusts, will,” etc. To “walk after” precedes it in most instances except in Jeremiah 18:12, where it is preceded by “doing.” Now to “do the hardness of the heart,” is no suitable expression; nor is “imagination” or “stubbornness” anything better. It can be derived from no verb which means to think or to imagine, or which has any connection with depravity or wickedness, or with appetites or lusts. Nor can we derive it from <span style="color:#008080">hrç</span>, which those do who render it “stubbornness;” for that will suit the passage referred to in Jeremiah 18:12. It must then come from <span style="color:#008080">rç</span>, to direct, to regulate, to rule, to exercise authority. It occurs three times in a reduplicate form; as a participial noun in Esther 1:22, and as a verb and a participle in Hithpael in Numbers 16:13, where it is applied in the sense of making oneself a prince or a tyrant. The literal meaning of the noun then is, in the plural number, predominances, domineerings, arbitraments; and it may be rendered determinations, resolutions, predominant influences or inclinations. “Walk shall they no more after the resolutions of their wicked heart.” It is used in Jeremiah 7:24, in apposition with “counsels,” being evidently a stronger word: it seems to mean resoluteness or resolvedness, a full determination, a willful decision. *Parkhurst* renders it, “the ruling principles.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA91</span> *Calvin* uses the verb “venient, “shall come, twice: but the first verb is to walk, and expresses the associating of Judah with Israel, or their union. The words are,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">In those days walk will the house of Judah with the house of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Israel, And come shall they together from the land of the north,</span> <span style="color:#000080">To the land which I made their fathers to inherit.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">They would be first united, and then advance together to their own land.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA92</span> *Calvin* takes the word in its Chaldee meaning; <span style="color:#008080">ybx</span> means in Hebrew, elation, splendor, glory; but in Chaldee, desire, what is desired; and this suits the passage best, “The inheritance of desire to hosts of nations;” that is, The inheritance desired by hosts of nations;” This is the meaning preferred by *Gataker*. I would render the whole verse thus,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">19.But I, said have I, “How shall I put thee among the children,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And give thee the land of delight, The inheritance desired by</span> <span style="color:#000080">hosts of nations?” Said have I also, “My Father shalt thou call</span> <span style="color:#000080">me, And from me thou wilt not turn away.”</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1112 -->

When the pronouns are given before verbs in Hebrew, as <span style="color:#008080">ykna</span> I, here, they are ever emphatical. “But I,” or, “as for me,” or, “even I have said.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA93</span> *Blayney,* contrary to all the early versions, construes the word <span style="color:#008080">h[rm</span> with woman, “a wicked woman.” “Friend,” or associate, or partner, is here evidently put for husband; a thing commonly done in other languages. Our common version of this verse is by no means correct: the preposition <span style="color:#008080">m</span> is not always “from,” but sometimes “with.” So here,<span style="color:#000080">Surely, as a woman deals perfidiously with her partner, So</span> <span style="color:#000080">perfidiously against me have ye dealt,</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">O house of Israel, saith Jehovah.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA94</span> The verse may be thus rendered,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">21.A voice on the high places! Heard is the weeping, the</span> <span style="color:#000080">supplications Of the people of Israel; Because they had</span> <span style="color:#000080">perverted their way, Had forgotten Jehovah their God.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Instead of “high places,” *Blayney* has “plains;” but there is no satisfactory reason for the change. As the verb in Hebrew commonly precedes its nominative, the construction adopted above is the most suitable to the character of the language.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA95</span> The literal rendering of these two verses is the following:-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">22.Return, ye apostate children, I will heal your apostasies.-</span> <span style="color:#000080">Behold us! We come to thee; For thou art Jehovah our God:</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">23.Surely, in vain are the hills, The multitude of mountains;</span> <span style="color:#000080">Surely, in (or through) Jehovah our God Is the salvation of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Israel.</span></p>

The word rendered “apostate,” does not mean “rebellious,” but such as turn away, *i.e.*, from God; and the word for “apostasies” means the same, being from the same root. The <span style="color:#008080">m</span> before the word for “hills,” is not a preposition, as it is commonly taken, but a formative: so it appears from all the versions. *Blayney* conjectures that it belongs to the former word, and makes it <span style="color:#008080">µyrqçl</span>; but then he does not account for the <span style="color:#008080">l</span> prefixed to it. There is no different reading. The *Septuagint* is,

<!-- PAGE 1113 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#0000d4">eijv ψεῦδος h=san οἱ ζουνοὶ</span>-for a lie were the hills. The *Vulgate, Syriac,* and *Arabic,* are materially the same.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA96</span> Rather, “And the shame,” i.e., the idol-worship, referred to in the preceding verse; the article <span style="color:#008080">h</span> is prefixed to the noun. This is the view taken by *Gataker* and *Blayney*. See Jeremiah 11:13; Hosea 9:10.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA97</span> *Calvin* seems to have followed the *Septuagint* in rendering the verb in the past tense. The *Vulgate* and *Syriac* retain the future of the original; but the *Targum* gives the present, and rightly so, as the future in Hebrew is often to be so taken. It is the same in *Welsh,* the future conveys the meaning of the present. This distich might in that language be rendered exactly according to the Hebrew, and the future would be understood as expressing what the present state of things is,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Gorweddwn yn ein cywilydd, A gorchuddia ni ein gwarth.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">But in English the present must be used, as it is the confession of the penitent when returning to God,-</p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">We lie in our shame, And cover us does our disgrace, Because</span> <span style="color:#000080">against Jehovah our God Have we sinned, we and our fathers,</span> <span style="color:#000080">From our childhood even to this day; And we have not</span> <span style="color:#000080">hearkened To the voice of Jehovah our God.-Ed.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA98</span> The best rendering is that which connects “to me” with the former clause: the end of the verse, as *Grotius* observes, proves this. If they returned to God, they were to return from captivity; and if they cast away their abominations, they were not to be vagabonds or to wander any more. This seems to be the meaning. The <span style="color:#008080">w</span> before <span style="color:#008080">al</span> in the last clause is left out in ten MSS., and in the *Vulgate, Targum,* and *Syriac.* The verse then would be as follows,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">1. If thou wilt return, Israel, saith Jehovah, to me, Thou shalt</span> <span style="color:#000080">be restored, (that is, from captivity:) If thou wilt remove thy</span> <span style="color:#000080">abominations from my sight, Thou shalt not be a wanderer.-Ed.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA99</span> This is a very lucid and satisfactory exposition. The import of the passage is very clearly given. A simpler version may be made, and such as will exhibit the meaning more plainly. When two *vaus* occur, they may often be rendered, *when* and *then*: so *here,-*

<!-- PAGE 1114 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">2. When thou shalt swear, “Live does Jehovah,” In truth, in</span> <span style="color:#000080">judgment, and in righteousness; Then call him blessed shall</span> <span style="color:#000080">nations, And in him shall they glory.</span></p>

To swear is to avow Jehovah as our God. The verbs “bless” and “glory” are both in Hithpael, which has commonly a reciprocal sense, but not always. See Psalm 72:17; <span class="ages-code">&lt;19A503&gt;</span>Psalm 105:3. This and the preceding verse belong to the last chapter.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA100</span> Literally, “Plough for yourselves the ploughing, “or, the plough-land; or, “Fallow for yourselves the fallow.” They were not to sow a land once ploughed; but they were to plough again.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA101</span> Rather, “On account of the evil of your doings.” Their doings were evil or wrong, both as to God and man. Impiety seems to be the special evil intended, as their defection from God had been more particularly referred to.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA102</span> These two verses contain a very spirited address, in a style truly poetical,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">5. Announce ye in Judah, And in Jerusalem publish, and say,-</span> <span style="color:#000080">Yea, sound the trumpet in the land, Proclaim, do it fully, and</span> <span style="color:#000080">say,- “ Be assembled, and let us enter into fortified cities;</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">6. Raise a banner towards Sion; Hasten ye, stay not: “ For an</span> <span style="color:#000080">evil am I bringing from the north, And a great destruction.</span></p>

The people of Judah were summoned to enter into fortified cities, and Mount Sion was to be the resort of the inhabitants of Jerusalem: “Hasten ye, “<span style="color:#0000d4">σπεύσατε</span>-hasten, *Septuagint.* This is the meaning of <span style="color:#008080">z[</span> in Hiphil. See Exodus 9:19; Isaiah 10:31. In Jeremiah 6:1, it is translated “Gather yourselves to flee;” but

“hasten,” or remove vigorously or quickly, would be the best rendering.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA103</span> The word “thicket, “in our version, correctly expresses it; a tangled wood, where trees cross and entwine with each other.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA104</span> “Laid waste” is the Chaldee sense; but the verb means in Hebrew to germinate, to produce grass, to grow over with grass as ruined cities do. The words which follow, “without an inhabitant, “shew that this meaning suits here,-

<!-- PAGE 1115 -->

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Thy cities shall grow over with grass, without an inhabitant.</span></p>

The *Targum* is, <span style="color:#000080">Thy cities shall be desolate without an inhabitant.-Ed.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA105</span> Though the most common meaning of <span style="color:#008080">bl</span>, heart, is what is here stated, yet it means also strength, firmness, courage. See Deuteronomy 20:2, 3; 2 Samuel 17:10; Psalm 22:14; Psalm 73:26. And this meaning is most suitable to this passage.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA106</span> The verse is as follows,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And it shall be in that day, saith Jehovah, That perish shall the</span> <span style="color:#000080">heart of the king And the heart of the princes, And confounded</span> <span style="color:#000080">shall be the priests, And the prophets shall be astonished.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">“Confounded,” that is, like persons at their wit’s end, not knowing what to do, or what course to take. “Astonished,” or amazed, that is, at witnessing the reverse of what they had prophesied; being filled with stunning and stupefying amazement.-*Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA107</span> There are various expositions of this verse: but the simpler and the plainer mode would be to take <span style="color:#008080">rma</span> as a noun, word, speech, saying, with an auxiliary verb, which is commonly omitted in Hebrew. The connection with the foregoing would be obvious and natural,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And the saying will be, “Alas! Lord Jehovah, Surely, deceiving</span> <span style="color:#000080">thou hast deceived This people and Jerusalem, By saying,</span> <span style="color:#000080">‘Peace shall be to you;’ And reach does the sword even to the</span> <span style="color:#000080">soul.”</span></p>

This would be the language of such as believed the false prophets, and considered them as sent by God.

<!-- PAGE 1116 -->
But *Lowth, Henry, Venema, Scott,* and others, take this view,-that God had permitted or suffered the people to be deceived by the false prophets. It is said that this verb in Hiphil, as the case is here, has sometimes this meaning, and *Lowth* refers, as instances, to Isaiah 63:17, and also to <span class="ages-code">&lt;19B910&gt;</span>Psalm 119:10; Proverbs 10:3. But the *sentiment* of the passage in this case would not be very suitable: for, according to this view, the cause of the Prophet’s grief is, that God had suffered the people to be deceived.

“It shall be said,” in the next verse, seems to be put in contrast with this “saying.” Instead of what would be commonly said of the people, God reminds them of what he would cause to be said and effected.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA108</span> The *Septuagint* version of these two verses is as foreign to the original as it can well be; and the *Syriac* and *Arabic* are nearly the same. The *Vulgate* gives a fair version; and the meaning, as given by the *Targum,* is nearly the same. The latter part of the 11th and 12th are thus rendered by *Blayney,-*

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">A wind that scorcheth the plains in the wilderness, [Shall χομέ</span> <span style="color:#000080">toward the daughter of my people, Not to winnow, nor to</span> <span style="color:#000080">cleanse;</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">12. A full wind for a curse shall come at my bidding; Now even</span> <span style="color:#000080">I will proceed judicially with them.</span></p>

*Horsley* differs as to the 11th verse, and renders it thus,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">The wind that scorcheth the craggy rocks of the wilderness</span> <span style="color:#000080">Taketh its course against the daughter of my people, Not for</span> <span style="color:#000080">winnowing or cleansing.</span></p>

The reason assigned for rendering <span style="color:#008080">hlam</span> for a curse, “and not “from those *places*, “as in our version, is, because the enemy did not come from that quarter. But this may be avoided, if we consider “as” or “like” to be understood before wind, which is no uncommon thing in Hebrew. To refer “those” or these to the winds implied in winnowing and cleansing, as *Calvin* does, and also *Gataker* and others, is not satisfactory, I would propose the following version,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">The dry wind of the cliffs in the wilderness Is advancing against</span> <span style="color:#000080">the daughter of my people, Not to winnow, nor to cleanse;</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">12.</span> <span style="color:#000080">*As*</span> <span style="color:#000080">a full wind from these, it shall come for me: Then will I</span> <span style="color:#000080">myself pronounce judgments on them.</span></p>

The word <span style="color:#008080">˚rd</span>, as *Horsley* takes it, is a verb, or rather a participle; and it is usual in Hebrew to put a participle in the first clause, and in the second a verb, as here, in the future tense. The verb means to come

<!-- PAGE 1117 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">upon, so as to tread down or subdue, Judges 5:21;</p>

Judges 20:43; Psalm 91:13. “The effect of this wind is

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">not only to render the air extremely hot and scorching, but to fill it with poisonous and suffocating vapors.”-*Blayney.-Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA109</span> Rather, “We have been wholly wasted, “or desolated. The verb is in a reduplicate form, and signifies an entire waste or desolation,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">13. Behold, like clouds will he ascend, And like a whirlwind</span> <span style="color:#000080">will be his chariots, Swifter than eagles his horses: “ Woe to us!</span> <span style="color:#000080">for we have been wholly wasted.”</span></p>

The mixture of the tenses is intended to shew the certainty of the event. Or we may consider the last line as containing what would be said after the coming of the enemy. What they would have to say was to acknowledge their entire desolation.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA110</span> The word means also iniquity, wickedness: and this is the sense in which the *Vulgate* and the *Targum* have taken it, and also *Blayney,* “the devices of thine iniquity: “and this corresponds more with the former part of the verse. The whole is as follows,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">14. Wash from evil thine heart, O Jerusalem, that thou mayest</span> <span style="color:#000080">be saved: How long shall lodge within thee The thoughts of thy</span> <span style="color:#000080">wickedness,</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">Or,</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Thy wicked thoughts.</span></p>

The word for “wash” here, according to *Parkhurst,* is ever applied to express a thorough washing, the washing away of what is inherent, such as the dirt of linen and of clothes: and he says, that there is another word, <span style="color:#008080">≈jr</span>, which is used when the washing of the surface of anything is intended, such as the washing of hands. “Shall lodge,”-it is no objection that this is singular, and the “thoughts” plural. It is an idiom: the same exists in *Welsh:* and in no other form would this sentence be rendered in that language. The present translation is incorrect, as the verb is taken to be in the second person, and applied to Jerusalem; which cannot be, as in that case it must have been in the feminine gender. The correct rendering would be,<span style="color:#000080">Pa hyd y hetya o’th fewn Dy feddyliau drygionus!</span>

<!-- PAGE 1118 -->

If the verb had *followed* its nominative case, it would have been in the same number; but as it precedes it, it is singular while the noun is plural.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA111</span> The first meaning of the word is iniquity, wickedness; and as the fruit or the effect of wickedness is affliction, distress, misery, it is sometimes taken to express the latter idea. It may be rendered here, distress.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA112</span> The verb in the first sentence followed by <span style="color:#008080">l</span> is found in Amos 6:10; where it clearly means “to make mention of, “or simply, to mention. So it may be rendered here, “Make ye mention of the nations, “or, Mention the nations, that is, for the sake of frightening the Jews. He had before referred to the voice from Dan, etc.; he now commands the invading nations to be proclaimed as approaching. The meaning is not, as *Blayney*, as well as *Calvin*, renders the phrase, “Proclaim ye unto the nations, “but, “Proclaim the nations, “as approaching, according to what is afterwards stated.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA113</span> To make this verse consistent with the context, I render it as follows,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Mention ye the nations, (and say,) “Behold</span> <span style="color:#000080">*them*</span><span style="color:#000080">!” Repeat at</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jerusalem, “ The watchers are coming from a distant land, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">shall raise against the cities of Judah their voice.”</span></p>

It is not improbable that <span style="color:#008080">l[</span> here means “over,” and that the “voice” means a triumphant shout, as *Calvin* seems to have thought. Then we may give this rendering,<span style="color:#000080">And shall raise over the cities of Judah their shout.-</span><span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA114</span> Calvin has followed the *Vulgate* and the *Syriac*. The *Septuagint* and *Arabic* have, “thou hast neglected me, “which is very wide from the original. “Rebel” is the rendering of the *Targum*, which is the Hebrew, and there is no other reading. Literally it is, <span style="color:#000080">For against me hath she rebelled, saith Jehovah.</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">And this is the rendering of *Blayney*.-*Ed*.</p>

<!-- PAGE 1119 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftA115</span> *Blayney*, contrary to all the early versions, renders <span style="color:#008080">hla</span>, “a curse, “instead of “these, “but there is no sufficient reason for the change. It is difficult to see what is the precise idea intended in our version as to the latter part of the verse. The meaning given by Calvin seems to be this,-that though the visitation was bitter and reached to the heart, it was yet to be ascribed to their wickedness. *Blayney’s* version is this,<span style="color:#000080">Such is thy calamity; for it is bitterness;</span> <span style="color:#000080">for it is a plague even unto thy heart.</span>

The latter words are taken as explanatory of the calamity. The word <span style="color:#008080">h[r</span> does indeed mean sometimes a calamity; but all the early versions, as well as the Targum, render it here “wickedness.” Hence the most suitable rendering would be,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Such is thy wickedness!</span> <span style="color:#000080">Though bitter, though reaching to thy heart.</span></p>

That <span style="color:#008080">yk</span> may be rendered “though” is evident from  Joshua 17:18; and it ought to be so rendered in Exodus 34:9; and in other places. But we may take the first <span style="color:#008080">yk</span> in its primary sense, surely, certainly, truly, and the second as a causative, for, because; an instance of a similar kind we meet in Exodus 13:17: the first <span style="color:#008080">yk</span> precedes an adjective, and is rendered “Although;” and the second <span style="color:#008080">yk</span>, a verb, and is rendered “for.” Then our version would be,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Such is thy wickedness (that is, its effect)! Surely, bitter; for it</span> <span style="color:#000080">reaches to thy heart.-</span><span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA116</span> Remarkably concise and striking are the words of this verse,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">My bowels! my bowels! I am in pain! O the enclosures of my</span> <span style="color:#000080">heart! Turbulent is my heart within me; I will not be silent; for</span> <span style="color:#000080">the sound of the trumpet Have I heard; my soul, the shout of</span> <span style="color:#000080">battle.</span></p>

To change the person of the verb, “I am in pain,” or in labor, as it literally means, as *Blayney* does, destroys the force and the vehemence of the passage; and all the early versions retain the first person. “The enclosures,” literally “the walls,” that is, what encloses or surrounds the heart, he mentions first the bowels, then what surrounds the heart, and afterwards the heart itself: and his pain was like that of a woman in

<!-- PAGE 1120 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">travail. Being in this state, he resolved not to be silent but to declare their danger to the people.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA117</span> The literal reading may be thus,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Breach upon breach has happened; For laid waste has been the</span> <span style="color:#000080">whole land; Suddenly laid waste have been my tents, In a</span> <span style="color:#000080">moment my curtains.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">He relates what he had seen in a vision, and therefore represents the whole as past. The verb <span style="color:#008080">arq</span> in Niphal as here, as well as in Kal, means sometimes to happen, to befall, to take place. The *Syriac* and the *Targum* give it here this meaning; and *Blayney* has adopted the same.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA118</span> The *specific* meaning of the terms used in this verse is not given in our version, nor by *Calvin*, nor by *Blayney*. The following, as I apprehend, is a literal version,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">For stupid</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">my people, Me they do not know; Foolish</span> <span style="color:#000080">children</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">they, And undiscerning</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">they; Wise</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">they to</span> <span style="color:#000080">do evil, But how to do good they know not.</span></p>

“Stupid,” <span style="color:#008080">lywa</span>, is one grossly ignorant, so as to be without knowledge, and not capable of knowing how to do good, or what is the good to be done. The last line explains the two first. Then “foolish,” <span style="color:#008080">µylks</span>, are the perverse, or the perverted, who are foolish through a perverted mind, who are said in the next line to be undiscerning, and who, as in the line which follows, had wisdom enough to do evil. They were stupidly ignorant, and perversely foolish. They were ignorant as to good, and wise as to evil; but this their wisdom was folly.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA119</span> These two words are viewed as synonymous by some, and the versions render them often by the same terms.
