As to the first, <span style="color:#008080">wht</span>, there can be no doubt as to its meaning, for it occurs about twenty times, and in all these places the idea of emptiness is chiefly conveyed: hence it is most commonly rendered in our version, vain, vanity, in vain, nought, etc., 1 Samuel 12:21; Isaiah 40:17; Isaiah 45:18;  Isaiah 49:4. It is improperly rendered <!-- PAGE 1121 --> “without form, “in Genesis 1:2, and “confusion” in Isaiah 34:11. When applied to the earth, as in Genesis 1:2, it imports emptiness, as it was then unfurnished either with productions or with any inhabitants. This appears evident from Isaiah 45:18, “He created it not in vain, “rather, “not empty did he create it-<span style="color:#008080">harb whtAal</span>;” “he formed it to be inhabited, “or more literally, “for a habitation he formed it.” As to the other word, <span style="color:#008080">wwb</span>, it only occurs three times, Genesis 1:2; Isaiah 34:11; and here. As the former evidently means emptiness, this may be taken to mean confusion or chaos, according to *Symmachus*, “<span style="color:#0000d4">συγκεχυμένη</span>,confused.” Then the right rendering here would be,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">23. I looked on the land, And behold emptiness and confusion;</span> <span style="color:#000080">And towards the heavens, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">*they were*</span> <span style="color:#000080">without their light.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">It is not the earth, but the land of Judea is what is meant. The whole passage being so striking, shall be here given,-</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">24. I looked at the mountains, And, behold, they were shaking,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And all the hills made quick motions:</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">25. I looked, and, behold, there was no man; And every bird of</span> <span style="color:#000080">heaven had fled away:</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">26. I looked, and, behold, Carmel a desert; And all its cities had</span> <span style="color:#000080">been demolished By the presence of Jehovah, By the</span> <span style="color:#000080">indignation of his wrath.</span></p>

The whole is represented as already done. The Prophet speaks of what he had seen in the vision.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA120</span> All the early versions, as well as the *Targum*, retain the word “Carmel.” *Blayney* renders it “the fruitful field.”-*Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1122 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftA121</span> All the early versions and the *Targum* favor the former view, as they all render the sentence, “Yet a consummation I will not make.” *Gataker* mentions another explanation, “I will not yet make a full end” with you; that is, I will punish you yet farther: and reference is made to Jeremiah 5:18. This view is adopted by *Blayney* and *Scott*. But the former view is no doubt the right one; for this is the meaning of the phrase as found in other places; see Jeremiah 30:11; where it is clear that <span style="color:#008080">hç[ hlk</span> is wholly to destroy. See also Nehemiah 9:31; Ezekiel 11:13; Ezekiel 20:17; Nahum 1:9. The meaning then is, “Yet I will not make an entire destruction.” *Henry* takes this view, and *Lowth* seems to prefer it. Indeed the phrase has no other meaning wherever it is used.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA122</span> The latter part is very concise,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Because I have said, I have purposed, And have not repented,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And I will not turn from it.</span></p>

The turning refers to what he had said, and repentance to the purpose. *Blayney* followed the *Septuagint*, and changed the order of the words, and thus destroyed the right connection of the passage, and the common parallelism of the language. We may also notice this passage as an instance of what is often found both in the Old Testament, and also in the New,-that when two or more things are consecutively stated, the most obvious, the most apparent, is mentioned first, and then the most hidden, or what is in order previous. Purpose is first in order, but speaking is first mentioned.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA123</span> The verbs in this are all in the past tense, as in some former instances. The Prophet had already seen in a vision what he here states,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">At the voice (or sound) of the rider and of the handler of the bow,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Flee did every city; They went into thickets, and into cliffs</span> <span style="color:#000080">they climbed; Every city was forsaken, And dwell in them did</span> <span style="color:#000080">no man.</span></p>

The word for “thickets” means sometimes “clouds.” The verb signifies to be dense, thick, gross, bulky: but the plural noun means a thick wood, as well as a thick or dense mass of vapors, which form clouds. It is rendered “<span style="color:#0000d4">άλση</span>,-forests, “by the *Septuagint* and *Syriac*; and “sylvas-woods,” by the *Targum*.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA124</span> The words “thou wretched, “or, more commonly, “thou spoiled, “are left out in the *Septuagint* and *Arabic*, and are retained in this sense by the *Vulgate*, *Syriac*, and the *Targum*. But, as *Blayney* justly says, it is a rendering that is not correct. “Thou, “as in the received text, is feminine, and “spoiled” is masculine. The *Keri* and many MSS. have <span style="color:#008080">ta</span> instead of <span style="color:#008080">yta</span>; and <span style="color:#008080">dwdç</span>, as *Blayney* supposes, is not a passive participle, but a verb in the infinitive mood, used as a noun. So he gives this version,<span style="color:#000080">And against spoiling what wilt thou do?</span>

<!-- PAGE 1123 -->

The word “spoiled,” or wasted, may indeed refer to “every city,” mentioned in the former verse, and the word for city is masculine. We may then render thus,<span style="color:#000080">And the city being wasted, what wilt thou do?</span>

“The *city*” may be deemed as the poetical singular for the plural.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA125</span> The *Septuagint*, the *Vulgate*, and the *Targum* give this rendering,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">“Though thou paintest with stibium thine eyes.”</span></p>

The Hebrew literally is, as it is rendered by *Blayney*,<span style="color:#000080">Though thou distendest with paint thy eyes.</span>

The verb <span style="color:#008080">[rq</span>, means first to rend, to divide, and then to divide in the sense of distending or enlarging. Large eyes were considered a beauty, and women used a sort of paint, or rather powder, for the purpose of enlarging them. See *Lowth’s* note on Isaiah 3:16, and *Parkhurst* under the word <span style="color:#008080">˚p</span>.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA126</span> Rather,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Rejected thee have paramours.</span></p>

This is the meaning of the verb when followed as here by <span style="color:#008080">b</span>. See note on Jeremiah 2:37. The word for paramours means not lovers, but lewd or mad lovers. The verb is rendered “to dote upon,” Ezekiel 23:12.*-Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA127</span> This latter part is differently taken by most. It is considered to be the confession of the daughter of Sion. The whole verse is remarkably striking,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">For the voice as of one in travail have I heard, The distress as of</span> <span style="color:#000080">one giving birth to a first-born, The voice of the daughter of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Sion;</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Who*</span> <span style="color:#000080">pants for breath,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*who*</span> <span style="color:#000080">spreads her hands,- “Wo now</span> <span style="color:#000080">to me, For melted has my soul because of murderers.”</span></p>

It is a common thing in Hebrew to omit the relative “*who, “*before a verb in a future tense, especially when it means the present time. The scene is described as present. The passage might be expressed in *Welsh* without the relative. “*Who* pants for breath,” is rendered by *Horsley,*

<!-- PAGE 1124 -->

“that draweth her breath short;” and he adds, “The passage is a most affecting picture of the last struggles of a woman expiring in labor.”-Ed.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA128</span> Our version is, “Run ye to and fro,” which has been taken from the *Septuagint-*<span style="color:#0000d4">περιδρύμετε</span>; but this is a more correct rendering. The *Vulgate* is “circuite-go round;” the *Syriac* is the same. “Streets” were the narrow ones, the lanes; and what *Calvin* renders “the cross-ways, “and our version “broad places, “were the wide streets, or the squares. In the former the poor people lived, and in the latter the great people, the chief men of the city. The examination was to extend to all the inhabitants. First, it takes place as to the poor in the lanes, and afterwards among the higher orders in the wide streets. The whole verse might be thus rendered,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">1. Go ye round through the narrow streets of Jerusalem, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">see, I pray, and know; Yea, seek in the broad streets; If ye can</span> <span style="color:#000080">find a man, if there be</span> <span style="color:#000080">*any,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">Who doeth justice, who seeks</span> <span style="color:#000080">faithfulness, Then will I spare it.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The <span style="color:#008080">w</span> after <span style="color:#008080">µa</span> may be often rendered “Then;” and this passage requires it to be so rendered. “That I may pardon her” is *Blayney’s* version; but this hardly corresponds with the former part; “If,” and “that,” form no connection.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA129</span> There are many MSS. which have <span style="color:#008080">ˆka</span>, “surely, “but <span style="color:#008080">ˆkl</span> may also be so rendered; yet, as “nevertheless” is its meaning, in Jeremiah 16:14, and other places, it may be so taken here. The *Septuagint* must have read <span style="color:#008080">ˆk al</span>, and the sentence is a question, “Do they not thus swear falsely?” But the early versions favor the present reading; and it gives a suitable meaning,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And though “Live does Jehovah, “they say, Nevertheless</span> <span style="color:#000080">falsely do they swear.</span></p>

The verbs are in the future tense, but used to express present acts, as is the case often in Hebrew, and also very commonly in *Welsh.* The words in the latter language might be expressed exactly as in the former, and be understood as speaking of what is present,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Ac er “Byw yw Jehova” a ddywedant,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Etto yn gelwyddog y tyngant.-</span><span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1125 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftA130</span> The literal rendering of this verse is as follows,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Jehovah! thine eyes, are</span> <span style="color:#000080">*they*</span> <span style="color:#000080">not on faithfulness? Smitten them</span> <span style="color:#000080">hast thou, but they have not grieved; Thou hast consumed</span> <span style="color:#000080">them,-they have refused to receive correction; Harder have they</span> <span style="color:#000080">made their faces than a rock; They have refused to return.</span></p>

The “truth” here, and in the first verse, is regarded by *Calvin* and most commentators, as faithfulness towards men. But a right view of the context will shew that it refers to fidelity towards God. Of what does the preceding verse speak? Of unfaithfulness towards God-swearing falsely in his name; that is, making a false and hypocritical profession of him; and in this verse they are described as refusing to return to him. In the fifth and sixth verses they are represented as having “broken the yoke, “and as having apostatized from him; and in the seventh their going after other gods is expressly mentioned.

The word “judgment” has been taken in the same way, but not, in my view, agreeably to the context. To do judgment, is to do what is just and right; and “the way of Jehovah, “and “the judgment of God, “in the next verse, are the same, and hence put in apposition; the word “nor, “in our version, being improperly introduced. The way of the Lord is the way he has prescribed in his word; and it is called his judgment, because it is what he has determined and ordained, or what is just and right. God had not only revealed his law, but had also appointed and ordained it for the people of Israel. His law is called a way, because it points out the course which we are to take; and it is his judgment, because it is what God has determined, fixed, and appointed. Hence in the fifth verse they are said to have broken the yoke and burst the bonds. The yoke was the law, and the bonds were those of loyalty and obedience; or they were the bonds of justice, such as were justly ordained and imposed on them.-*Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1126 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftA131</span> It is better to take <span style="color:#008080">˚a</span> here and in the next verse as an affirmative, Truly, surely, doubtless. *Blayney,* as well as *Calvin,* render <span style="color:#008080">wlawn</span>, “have acted foolishly.” The verb occurs in three other places, Numbers 12:11; Isaiah 19:13; Jeremiah 50:36. To be, or to become, foolish, or rather stupid, sottish, or stupidly ignorant, seems to be its meaning. It is here opposed to knowledge; and evidently refers to the state of the mind, and not to the conduct. Their sottishness was their idolatry. This is the special sin referred to throughout the passage,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Then I said, Doubtless, the poor</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">these, they have become</span> <span style="color:#000080">stupid, For they have not known the way of Jehovah, The</span> <span style="color:#000080">judgment of their God.-</span><span style="color:#000080">*Ed.*</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1127 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftA132</span> Literally it is, “I will go for myself,”-an idiomatic form of speech. The *Welsh* is exactly the same, *af rhagof;* which means, I will go forth; but it cannot be literally expressed in another language. After the verb, as in Hebrew, there is a preposition prefixed to “me.”-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA133</span> The word, as found here, is never used for the evening; it ever means the desert, or uncultivated plains. The plural termination of the word, when it means the evening, is <span style="color:#008080">µyA</span>-, and not <span style="color:#008080">twA</span>-, as here. See Numbers 22:1; Joshua 5:10; Jeremiah 39:5. In these verses it is rendered “plains;” they were evidently uncultivated, and might properly be called deserts. The *Vulgate* and the *Targum* have led commentators astray as to this word. The *Septuagint* have completely misunderstood it, and have rendered the sentence, “The wolf even to the houses (<span style="color:#0000d4">e[wv τῶν οἰκιῶν</span>) has destroyed them.” The version of *Blayney* is, “The wolf of the plains: “and he says in a note, that they were “unenclosed commons.” used for sheepwalks, which were commonly “infested with wolves.”-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA134</span> This illative, “wherefore,” or therefore, or, for this cause, is both retrospective and anticipative. It is a reason given for what is contained in the latter part of the last verse, and for what is contained in the last words of this verse; it anticipates the particle “because” before “multiplied.”-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA135</span> It is rendered “<span style="color:#0000d4">ἀσεβείας</span>*-impieties, “*by the *Septuagint*; “prevarications” by the *Vulgate; “*rebellions” by the *Targum.* It does not mean “iniquities, “but willful violations of the law in matters connected with God’s worship and service. The other word means apostasies, defections from God, rendered by the *Septuagint,* “<span style="color:#0000d4">ἀποστρόφαις</span>-turnings away, “and so by the *Vulgate* and the *Targum.* They were defections to various forms of idolatry. Their idols increased in number. The *Septuagint* render the last sentence thus, “They have become strong (<span style="color:#0000d4">ίσχυσας</span>) in their turnings away.” The *Vulgate* and the *Targum* are the same. The Verb <span style="color:#008080">µx[</span> means an increase in quality or in quantity. But both verbs may be rendered here as transitives,<span style="color:#000080">Because they have multiplied their transgressions, They have</span> <span style="color:#000080">strengthened (or increased) their apostasies.-Ed.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA136</span> This has been done by the Punctuists, and is no part of the language.- *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA137</span> The last line may be rendered thus,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">And the house of the harlot they crowd.</span></p>

The verb for “crowd” seems here to be transitive, though it be intransitive in Micah 5:1.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA138</span> The literal rendering of this verse, and countenanced by ancient versions, is as follows,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Horses well fed! libidinous have they become; They neigh,</span> <span style="color:#000080">every one at the wife of his neighbor. Both</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Venema*</span> <span style="color:#000080">and</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Blayney*</span> <span style="color:#000080">agree in giving this meaning.</span></p>

It does not seem, when the whole context is viewed, that adultery here is to be taken in its literal sense. It is spiritual adultery, that is, idolatry, that is referred to throughout the chapter. Besides, the comparison in this verse is such, that its application is more suitable to idolatrous acts than to those which are adulterous. The same may be said of what is found in the preceding verse,-that they crowded the house of the harlot. This is not so much the case in adultery as in idolatry, when people fill their idolatrous temples. A simile is sometimes carried beyond what is actually the case, in order to convey a right idea of what it is intended to illustrate. When they are said to be like well-fed stallions, and neighing at the wives of their neighbors, the purpose was to shew with what intense ardor they were devoted to idolatry: and the degrading comparison was no doubt made in order to pour contempt on their mad propensity: it was like the impetuous instinct of an animal, uncontrolled by any reason, persuasion, or remonstrance.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA139</span> See Note on Jeremiah 4:27.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA140</span> It is true the word means shoots or branches; but as the root means to spread, it evidently signifies here battlements, bulwarks, or ramparts.

<!-- PAGE 1128 -->

It is rendered “<span style="color:#0000d4">ὑποστηρίγματα</span>-props, pillars,” by the *Septuagint;* “propagines-shoots,” by the *Vulgate; “*foundations,” by the *Syriac* and *Arabic;* and “palaces, or towers, by the *Targum.* Our version has the most suitable word-”battlements.” *Blayney* has “branches, “and thinks that the cities of Judea are meant; but this is not suitable to the context.*-Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA141</span> The verb <span style="color:#008080">dgb</span>, when followed by <span style="color:#008080">b</span> as here, means to deal deceitfully, perfidiously, or treacherously, with one. See Exodus 21:8; Judges 9:23; Lamentations 1:2. It may be rendered here to dissemble,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">For they dissembling have dissembled with me, The house of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Israel and the house of Judah, saith Jehovah.</span></p>

As the verb is repeated, if we render it “to act perfidiously, “instead of repeating the words, to give them their force and meaning, we must say, <span style="color:#000080">For they have dealt most perfidiously with me.</span>

To “deal unfaithfully,” as rendered by *Blayney*, is too feeble an expression. To “prevaricate” is the word used by the *Vulgate,* and the same by the *Septuagint* and the *Targum.-Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA142</span> According to all the instances in which the verb occurs, followed by <span style="color:#008080">b</span>, its meaning is what *Calvin* states, though not so rendered uniformly in our version. The verb, followed by <span style="color:#008080">b</span>, occurs elsewhere five times,Leviticus, 6:2, 3;  Joshua 24:27; Job 8:18; Isaiah 59:13; Hosea 9:2. In Leviticus 6:3, <span style="color:#008080">hb çjkw</span>, ought to be rendered, “and denieth it;” and so the verb ought to be, rendered in the previous verse, “and deny to his neighbor his deposit, etc. It is rendered here “lied to the Lord” by the Septuagint and the *Targum;* and “denied the Lord” by the *Vulgate* and the *Syriac,* and so also by *Piscator, Venema,* and *Blayney.* The denial seems to have been as to the Lord speaking in his prophets. *Calvin* appears to have gone too far in saying that they denied his existence. The expression which follows, “Not he, “means that he was not in the prophets. The import is correctly given in our version, “It is not he, “that is, who speaks in the prophets. The verse might be literally given thus,-

<!-- PAGE 1129 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">12. And they deny Jehovah, And say, “</span><span style="color:#000080">*It is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">not he, And come</span> <span style="color:#000080">upon us shall not evil, And the sword and the famine we shall</span> <span style="color:#000080">not see.”</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Then the following verse, which is a continuation of what they said, proves clearly what the meaning of this is,-</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">13. “And the prophets shall be wind, For the word</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">not in</span> <span style="color:#000080">them: Thus shall it happen to them,”</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">or,</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Thus shall it be done to them.</span></p>

That is, they shall be found out to be like the wind, having spoken nothing real, such as shall be accomplished. Indeed the last line may be translated thus,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Thus shall he do to (or, deal with) them.</span></p>

The reference in this case is to God, who, they thought, would render abortive, or turn as it were into wind, what the prophets had threatened. Their blindness and presumption appear to us to have been extreme.*-Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA143</span> This sentence is left out in the *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* and the *Arabic,* but retained by the *Vulgate*,-” Haec ergo evenient illis-These things shall therefore come to them.” This meaning the original will hardly bear. The reference seems to be to the Prophets becoming wind, being so proved by the event.-*Ed* <span style="color:#800000">ftA144</span> The verb <span style="color:#008080">[mç</span> here is not merely to hear, but to hear effectually, that is, so as to understand. It has this meaning in other places; see Deuteronomy 38:49; 2 Kings 18:26. The whole verse may be thus rendered,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">15. Behold, I am bringing upon you a nation from far, O house</span> <span style="color:#000080">of Israel, saith Jehovah,- A nation, strong it</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">A nation, from</span> <span style="color:#000080">antiquity it</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">A nation, thou wilt not know its language, Nor</span> <span style="color:#000080">understand what it speaks.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The third, fourth, and fifth lines, as well as the first of the next verse, are left out in the *Septuagint,* but retained by the *Vulgate, Syriac,* and</p>

<!-- PAGE 1130 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">the *Targum.* The two first render the word for “strong, “ “robustam, “and the last by “fortis-brave.” *Blayney* renders it “strong, “which is no doubt its meaning.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA145</span> The idea intended is capaciousness. The grave is represented as never satisfied, ready to receive any number. See Proverbs 27:20; 30:16; Habakkuk. 2:5. The Chaldean quiver would be so large as to contain a vast quantity of arrows, as though it was an open grave.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA146</span> According to the Hebrew, the verbs, except the second, are all in the singular number. The *Septuagint* have pluralized them, but the singular is retained by the *Vulgate,* the *Targum,* and the *Syriac.* It is the “nation” described in the 15th verse. The second verb may be rendered in a passive sense, and the meaning will be more appropriate,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And it will devour thy harvest and thy food, Devoured shall be</span> <span style="color:#000080">thy sons and thy daughters; It will devour thy sheep and thy</span> <span style="color:#000080">ox, It will devour thy wine and thy fig-tree; It will wholly</span> <span style="color:#000080">desolate thy fortified cities, In which thou trustest, by the</span> <span style="color:#000080">sword.</span></p>

The language used here, and in the 15th verse, is remarkably like that of Moses in Deuteronomy 28:48-52. The second line may be deemed parenthetic. It is better to preserve the poetic singular in sheep, ox, vine, and fig-tree. As it is a reduplicate verb, entire desolation is intended, and that by the “sword” in destroying all the occupants of fortified cities. *Venema,* and others, as well as *Calvin,* connect the “sword” with all the preceding clauses; but this is not necessary, nor is it indeed suitable.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA147</span> See note on Jeremiah 4:27. Even if the design pleaded for by *Calvin* be admitted, there is no necessity to give the expression a meaning different from what it has in other places,<span style="color:#000080">Yet even in those days, saith Jehovah,</span> <span style="color:#000080">I will not make with you a completion,</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">*that is,* <span style="color:#000080">I will not wholly destroy you.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">It depends on the context what the bearing of this may be, while the sentence itself retains the same meaning. “I will not wholly destroy</p>

<!-- PAGE 1131 -->

you, for I intend to preserve a Church for myself, he might also say, “I will not wholly destroy you, for I have other punishments in reserve for you: “and the latter, as *Calvin* maintains, seems to be the purport of the expression in this passage. Still the words themselves have the same meaning.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA148</span> The last clause has been improperly omitted in the *Arabic:* it is found in the other versions. The word for “strangers” is different from that connected with “gods.” They served “the gods of the alien, “or, of the heathen: they would have to serve “strangers, “or, foreigners, in a land not their own. As they had adopted the *religion* of heathens, they would have to submit to the *dominion* and tyranny of heathens: and as they did the former in their own land, they would have to do the latter in a foreign land. Thus their idolatry would expel them from their own country, and subject them to the tyranny of those from whom they derived their idolatry. Thus God often makes the tempters of his people (if they succeed) to be their tormentors.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA149</span> It is better to retain the future tense, as their obstinacy is thereby more fully expressed; the whole verse may be thus literally rendered,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Hear, I pray, this, Ye perverse people and without</span> <span style="color:#000080">understanding,- Eyes they have, but they will not see, Ears</span> <span style="color:#000080">they have, but they will not hear.</span></p>

The “this” which they were to hear is contained in the next verse. The two last lines are only explanatory of the preceding. They were “without understanding, “for they would not see, though they had eyes; and they were “perverse, “or perversely foolish, for they would not hear, though they had ears. When two things are mentioned and afterwards referred to, the prophets usually explain the last, and then the first, as the case is here. The two last lines may be included in a parenthesis.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA150</span> The reference seems to be to the decree or ordinance, and not to the boundary: therefore “transgress” is the most suitable word. I would render the verse thus,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">22. Should you not fear me? saith Jehovah; Ought you not at</span> <span style="color:#000080">my presence to tremble? Who have set the sand a boundary to</span> <span style="color:#000080">the sea.- An ordinance perpetual, and it transgresses it not;</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1132 -->

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Though toss themselves and prevail not, and roar do its waves,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Yet it does not transgress it.</span></p>

The future tense in Hebrew may be often rendered subjunctively or potentially, and especially in questions. The non-transgressor is the sea-the non-transgressor of the decree, notwithstanding the tumult of its waves. “An ordinance perpetual” is rendered “<span style="color:#0000d4">πρόσταγμα</span> <span style="color:#0000d4">αἰώνιον</span>-an everlasting command, by the *Septuagint,-*” praeceptum sempiternum-a sempiternal precept, “by the *Vulgate* and the *Targum*,and “lege perpetua-by a perpetual law, “by the *Syriac.* A perpetual ordinance is the version of *Blayney.-Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA151</span> It is true that the idea of perverseness is conveyed by this word; but it means one bent on turning away from God. To turn away, or to apostatize, is its primary meaning, as in the latter clause of this verse; but here it is a participle in a reduplicate form, which has ever an expansive meaning. It means here either a heart continually turning away from God, or a heart resolutely determined to turn away from him. The last seems to be the meaning, as it appears to correspond with the next line,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">But in this people has been a heart, Thoroughly revolting and</span> <span style="color:#000080">disobedient; They have revolted and gone away.</span></p>

When they turned away from God or revolted, they were remonstrated with and exhorted to return; but they disobeyed; hence their thorough revolt and their going away.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA152</span> *Blayney,* following the *Septuagint* and the *Vulgate,* has rendered the latter clause thus,<span style="color:#000080">A sufficiency of the appointed things of harvest he secureth to us.</span>

But the *Targum* agrees with our version; and *Gataker, Grotius, Venema,* and others, take the same view, which is more expressive and more accordant with the passage,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">The weeks, the appointed seasons of harvest,</span> <span style="color:#000080">He preserves for us.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1133 -->

The word <span style="color:#008080">twqj</span>, means what is established, fixed, ordained, or appointed, as to time, place, course, portion, or law; and it is here, not in regimine, but in apposition with “weeks.”-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA153</span> There is a correspondence between the terms here used, and those in the last verse. Their iniquities had *turned aside,* or diverted from their right course, the *appointed* seasons; and their sins had *restrained* the good, the *seasonable rain* which had been given them. As it is commonly the case in the prophets, the last thing mentioned in the previous verse, is the first thing referred to here, and then what is antecedently mentioned.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA154</span> There is a grammatical difficulty connected with the word <span style="color:#008080">rwçy</span> The *Septuagint,* and the *Syriac* and the *Arabic,* omit the word, and the *Vulgate* renders it in the plural number, as if it was <span style="color:#008080">wrwçy</span>, which *Blayney* adopts. *Venema* renders it “upright” or just, and considers the root to be <span style="color:#008080">rçy</span> and refers to  Micah 7:4, when the upright is said to be” sharper than a thorn hedge, “that is, such as were counted or ought to have been “upright.” The reference here is evidently to the judge, who assumed the office of an upright one. See verse 28. The literal version would then be as follows,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">For found among my people are the unjust; The upright</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">like</span> <span style="color:#000080">the setter of snares; They have set up entrapping, Men they</span> <span style="color:#000080">catch.</span></p>

Thus all the parts correspond, and what is said corresponds with Jeremiah 5:28. The verb rendered “set up, “means to settle, to constitute, to establish; the office of the upright, that is, of the judge, was set up as an office for entrapping, he being like a setter of snares. The “unjust” among the people, as stated here, were the judges; the word, <span style="color:#008080">[çr</span> is the perverter of justice, and stands in contrast with <span style="color:#008080">qdx</span>, who acts justly.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA155</span> It is so rendered in Amos 8:1, 2. This was no doubt a wickerbasket or cage for birds, to keep them, and not a trap-cage, as suggested by the *Septuagint* and *Vulgate* versions. The *Targum* is, “the house of feeding.” The comparison is between a cage full of birds, which had been caught by snares, nets, or traps, and houses filled with spoils, which had been procured by frauds. And were “full” rendered “filled,

<!-- PAGE 1134 -->

“as it might be, there would be no need of the metonymy supposed to be in the word “fraud, “-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">As the cage is filled with birds, So their houses</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">filled by</span> <span style="color:#000080">means of fraud: Hence they have become great and grown rich.-</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed.*</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA156</span> Expounders differ as to the meaning of these words. They are partly omitted by the *Septuagint* and *Syriac.* The *Vulgate* is, “et praeterierunt sermones meos pessime-and they have passed by my words very haughtily.” The *Targum* is a loose version, “They have also transgressed the words of the law, they have done what is evil.” Such meanings do not correspond with the context. The words literally are, “They have passed over (or, by) the words of wrong;” but as the term for “words” often means things, affairs, matters, the version may be, “matters of wrong, “or wrong things. These “matters of wrong” are afterwards specified, as will be seen in the following version,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">28. They have become fat, they have shined: Moreover, they</span> <span style="color:#000080">have passed by matters of wrong; The cause they have not</span> <span style="color:#000080">defended- The cause of the orphan, yet have they prospered;</span> <span style="color:#000080">And the right of the meek have they not pleaded.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The word “moreover, “may be rendered “though, “as *Blayney* does, (see Nehemiah 6:1:) but the rest of the sentence is not so well rendered,<span style="color:#000080">Though they have gone beyond the claims of the wicked.</span></p>

He conceived that the meaning is, that they granted to the wicked man more than he claimed, while they denied justice to the orphan and the poor. But what is more accordant with the words is, that he states here what he afterward specifies. It is not properly the “poor” who are meant, but the quiet, the humble: for the poor, strictly speaking, had not much to lose; hence the judges were not bribed to allow them to become a prey to dishonest men.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA157</span> The words literally are,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Amazement and horribleness has been done in the land.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">That is, what occasioned both had been done, or what ought to have filled all with the feeling of amazement and horror.-*Ed.*</p>

<!-- PAGE 1135 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftA158</span> The *Septuagint* and the *Vulgate* have, “And the priests have applauded with their own hands;” and the *Targum, “*And the priests have helped their hands.” Both mean the same thing, though the words are different: and *Blayney* gives the same meaning, “And the priests have concurred with them.” *Horsley* says that the words literally are, “And the priests go down according to their hands;” that is, he adds, “the priests go which way their bands permit; *i.e.,* the priests are directed by them.” Though the points lead us to regard <span style="color:#008080">wdry</span> as future from <span style="color:#008080">hdr</span>, to bear rule; yet the context requires it to be in the past tense, as the previous verb is so, and that which follows: and therefore it must be <span style="color:#008080">dry</span>, to come down, to descend. When followed by <span style="color:#008080">l[</span>, as here, the preposition never means “according to, “as *Horsley* renders it, but ever, upon, toward or against, and mostly “upon.” See Exodus 9:19; Numbers 11:9; Psalm 7:16; Psalm 72:6. Therefore the literal rendering is this,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And the priests have descended upon their hands.</span></p>

An idiomatic expression, which seems to mean, that the priests assisted the prophets, according to what is expressed by the *Targum.* “Hand” signify labor, efforts; the priests joined their efforts to those of the prophets. To “concur with them” is too feeble: the line may be rendered,<span style="color:#000080">And the priests have aided them.-</span><span style="color:#000080">*Ed.*</span> <span style="color:#800000">ftA159</span> The “it” refers rather to the “strange and horrible thing” which had been done in the land,<span style="color:#000080">But what will ye do at the end of it?</span>

<!-- PAGE 1136 -->
That is, when this dreadful thing shall come to an end, when the prophets, encouraged by the priests and approved by the people, shall be found liars, what then shall you do? The *Septuagint* render the last words by “<span style="color:#0000d4">μετὰ ταῦτα</span>-after these things, “referring evidently to the particulars just mentioned, the acts of the prophets, priests, and people: but the same thing is meant. Then in the next chapter he reminds them of the approaching destruction, which the false prophets denied.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA160</span> See note on Jeremiah 4:6. The meaning of the verb is, no doubt, to haste, or to hasten. It is singular that the *Septuagint* render it in Jeremiah 4:6, “Haste ye,” and here, “Be ye strong.” The *Targum* renders it “migrate,” or, remove ye. The idea of assembling it never has. The line rightly rendered is,<span style="color:#000080">Hasten, ye sons of Benjamin, from the midst of Jerusalem.</span>

Where *Blayney* got the phrase, “Retire in a body, “it is difficult to say.*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA161</span> “Raise ye a sign (<span style="color:#0000d4">σήμειον</span>)” is the *Septuagint* and the *Targum*; “Raise ye a banner (vexillum)” is the *Vulgate* and the *Syriac*. The word has no connection with “fire, “as mentioned in our version, which has been derived from the Rabbins. *Blayney’s* rendering is, “light up a firebeacon;” but the words admit of no such meaning. It is a general expression, and may be rendered, “Raise ye a signal;” there is no definition as to what the signal was to be.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA162</span> Literally, “For evil is seen from the north.” So the *Vulgate* and the *Targum*. The verb in Kal, Niphal, and Hiphil, is rendered “look” in our version. See Genesis 19:28; Judges 5:28; Deuteronomy 26:15. But in Niphal, as it is found here, it may be rendered passively, “is seen;” and also in Psalm 85:12; and in Cant. 6:10, and in most other places. *Blayney* renders it, “is seen coming onwards, “which is a paraphrase.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA163</span> *Calvin*, with our version, has followed the *Vulgate* and the *Syriac* in this verse. Both the *Septuagint* and the *Targum* are widely different. The former have, “And taken away shall be thy height, daughter of Sion;” the latter, “Fair and delicate, how hast thou corrupted thy ways? Therefore confounded is the assembly of Sion.” The *Arabic* is the same with the *Septuagint*, only it has “pride” instead of “height” (<span style="color:#0000d4">tou[yov</span>) Some have viewed the two first words as substantives, and have rendered the verse thus,- <!-- PAGE 1137 --> To a pasture and a delightful habitation Have I likened the daughter of Sion. Disposed to this view were *Gataker* and *Lowth*. But what *Blayney* has said is true, that whenever the verb here used has the sense of likeness, it is followed by a preposition. Besides, the two first words are not substantives but adjectives, as the form, especially of the last, clearly shews. The verb <span style="color:#008080">ytymd</span> has in various passages the sense of thinking, counting, esteeming, regarding; as the result of comparing things together. See Judges 20:5; Esther 4:13; Psalm 48:9. There is a passage in Ezekiel 32:2, which is like the present, only the verb there is in Niphal; its literal rendering I consider to be the following: “The young lion of the nations art thou deemed, “or, thought to be. The literal rendering of this verse is as follows,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Home-resident and delicate,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Have I deemed the daughter of Sion.</span></p>

She was so regarded by God. Not like other nations, migratory, she had a home allotted to her by God himself; and she was nursed and sustained with all tenderness, like a delicate person. But owing to her sins, foreigners, as stated in the next verse, would come and take possession of her house, and deprive her of her enjoyments.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA164</span> There is evidently a <span style="color:#008080">w</span> or a <span style="color:#008080">y</span> wanted before the second verb in this verse. The *Septuagint* and the *Syriac* read with <span style="color:#008080">w</span>, and the *Targum* with <span style="color:#008080">y</span>. The same is the case with the third verb, <span style="color:#008080">w[r</span>; but there are two MSS. which have the <span style="color:#008080">w</span> here, with which the *Septuagint*, the *Syriac*, and the *Targum* agree. Then the version would be,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">To her shall come shepherds and their flocks, And pitch by her</span> <span style="color:#000080">their tents around, And they shall feed, every one in his border.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1138 -->
“To pitch against her” seems improper: the proposition <span style="color:#008080">l[</span> means *by* or *near*, as well as *against*. And <span style="color:#008080">dy</span> does not mean properly *place*, but *side* or *border*. It is indeed rendered *place* often in our version. See Numbers 2:17; Deuteronomy 23:12; Isaiah 56:5, 11; and in Isaiah 57:8, “quarter.” The ancient versions differ; the word seems not. to have been understood. It is rendered by the *Septuagint*, “by his hand;” by the *Vulgate*, “those under his hand; and by the *Targum*, “his neighbor.”-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA165</span> These two verses seem to have been the language of the enemies on their march towards Jerusalem. When men go to a chief city from any quarter, it is always ascending. When on their march, they encouraged one another to ascend by mid-day, as <span style="color:#008080">b</span> may be rendered, but the day declined before they reached the city; then they encouraged each other to continue their march in the night,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Proclaim ye against her war: Rise, and let us ascend by mid-</span> <span style="color:#000080">day.- Alas for us! for declined has the day, For extended have</span> <span style="color:#000080">become the shadows of the evening: Arise, and let us ascend by</span> <span style="color:#000080">night, And destroy her palaces.</span></p>

The last word is rendered “foundations” by the *Septuagint*,-” houses” by the *Vulgate*,-and “palaces” by the *Targum*. This is an instance of the loose way in which the versions were often made.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">To “sanctify war, “is not to prepare it, but to proclaim it, as *Calvin* says, by a solemn ceremony.-*Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA166</span> The two last lines may be thus rendered,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">She, the city, to be visited</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the whole of it:</span> <span style="color:#000080">Oppression is in the midst of it.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The verb <span style="color:#008080">dqph</span> is an infinite Niphal. Some, not perhaps without reason, have rendered the first line, “For thus has Jehovah of hosts said.”-*Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA167</span> The verse, literally rendered, is as follows:-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">7. As cast forth does a spring its waters, So cast forth is her</span> <span style="color:#000080">wickedness: Violence and plunder are heard of in her; Before me</span> <span style="color:#000080">continually</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">wounding and smiting.</span></p>

The first verb is in Hiphil, the second is in Huphal. “Violence” was the visible act; “plunder” or spoiling was the object or the motive; “wounding” was the effect; “smiting” was the cause. Such is often found to be the way of stating things observed by the Prophets.

*Blayney* renders the two last words “sickness and smiting, “and adds, that the two words are a Hendiadis, and signify “sickness occasioned by blows.” The true reason for the order is what has been stated: it is according to what is commonly done in Scripture; what is found often is not the progressive, but the retrogressive order.

<!-- PAGE 1139 -->
The *Septuagint* and the *Targum* have strangely rendered this verse in a manner wholly inconsistent with the context; nor are the other versions much better. The Hebrew is plain enough.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA168</span> Or, “Be warned, “or, “Be reformed.” The verb in Niphal is found in four other places, Leviticus 26:23; Psalm 2:10; Proverbs 29:19; Jeremiah 31:18, and rendered reformed, instructed, corrected, and chastised. It is the same as to receive correction and to become reformed.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA169</span> Or, “Lest forced shall be my soul from thee.” The verb means to drive, to thrust, to force. To “depart, “as rendered by the ancient versions, and by our version, is too weak, and is not the idea; and still worse is “alienated, “as rendered by *Blayney*. It intimates God’s unwillingness, as it were, to give up his chosen people, according to what *Calvin* observes.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA170</span> *Blayney’s* version is,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Turn again thine hand, like a grape-gatherer, unto the baskets.</span></p>

“That is, Take thou again into thine hand, and begin the work of gathering or gleaning anew.” He takes it as God’s address to the Chaldeans, in which they are exhorted repeatedly to return and to carry away captives the remaining inhabitants. But this does not comport with the simile of the vintager returning the hand to the baskets. It seems to be a command to put in safe custody those whom they took or gleaned, as a vintager, who, when he plucks a grape or a cluster, puts it safely in a basket to be carried away. The “hand” is put here for what the hand holds-the grapes or clusters. It is then the same as though he had said, “Lay up, as a vintager, what you glean, in baskets.” The Jews were gathered, not to be destroyed, but to be carried away into captivity. This seems to have been the intimation here,<span style="color:#000080">Return thine hand, like a vintager, unto the baskets.</span>

That is, Throw not away what you gather, but let the hand, that is stretched forth to reach the grapes, bring back what it gleans into the baskets. The *Vulgate* is, “Turn (converte) thine hand as a vintager to his basket.” The *Septuagint*. Turn ye (<span style="color:#0000d4">ἐπιστρέψατε</span>) as a vintager to his basket.” The *Syriac* is the same with the *Vulgate*, except that it has “gleaning” instead of “basket.” The *Arabic* corresponds with the *Septuagint*. The *Targum* has an unintelligible paraphrase.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA171</span> This is a remarkable verse, and shews, as *Calvin* explains, the degraded and corrupt state of the nation in a very striking manner,-

<!-- PAGE 1140 -->

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">To whom shall I speak, And protest, so that they will hear?</span> <span style="color:#000080">Behold, uncircumcised is their ear, So that they cannot hearken;</span> <span style="color:#000080">Behold, the word of Jehovah Has become to them a reproach,</span> <span style="color:#000080">They delight not in it.</span></p>

“A reproach” is to be the subject of reproach: the word of God by his prophets was despised and treated with contempt. This was the visible and palpable effect, but the cause was, that they had no delight in it or love for it.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA172</span> There are two or three points in this verse differently explained. The fury or indignation of Jehovah has been viewed as the message which the Prophet had to deliver, which strongly expressed God’s displeasure. See Jeremiah 1:9, and Jeremiah 20:9. The verb for pouring forth is either in the imperative or in the infinitive mood. The *Vulgate* and the *Syriac* render it as an imperative; but the *Septuagint,* the *Targum,* and the *Arabic* give it, as in our version, in the future indicative, the first person. *Venema* follows the *Vulgate*: but *Blayney* takes it to be in the imperative mood; which seems most consistent with the whole of the passage. The view of most as to “the old” and “the full of years” is, that the first is mature old age, and that the second is the last stage of life, the age of decrepitude. The full of days is “one” as *Blayney* says, “who has arrived at the full period of human life;” and hence “Abraham, Isaac, David, and Job are said to have died *full of years,* or of days.” See Isaiah 65:20. Though the general meaning is given in our version, yet the more literal I conceive to be the following,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">But with the wrath of Jehovah have I been filled; I am weary of</span> <span style="color:#000080">restraining to pour</span> <span style="color:#000080">*it*</span> <span style="color:#000080">forth On the child in the street, And on</span> <span style="color:#000080">the assembly of young men also; Yea, both man and woman</span> <span style="color:#000080">shall be taken, The aged and the full of days.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">It is unusual to have two infinitives following one another: but the *Welsh* is capable of expressing the Hebrew literally,<span style="color:#000080">Blinais ymattal dywallt.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1141 -->
Nothing can express the original more exactly. It is better to say “man and woman, “as *Gataker* proposes, than “husband and wife;” for the object is to shew, that all, including every age and both sexes, were to be visited with judgment.-*Ed* <span style="color:#800000">ftA173</span> “From the small of them even to the great, “*Septuagint; “*From the less to the greater, “*Vulgate; “*From the least of them even to the greatest of them, “*Targum, Syriac,* and *Arabic.* The last is the best. The positive degree is often put in Hebrew for the superlative. See Jonah 3:5.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA174</span> The words literally are “gaining gain, “rendered in Proverbs 1:19, and Proverbs 15:27, “greedy of gain.” The *Septuagint* give only a general idea, “performed unlawful things;” the *Vulgate* has, “given to avarice;” the *Targum*, “gape after riches.” The prevailing sin of all ranks was covetousness; and the special sin of the priests and prophets was falsehood: they taught falsely. The verse may be thus rendered,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">For from the least of them to the greatest of them, His all</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">to</span> <span style="color:#000080">gain; And from the prophet to the priest, His all</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">to act</span> <span style="color:#000080">falsely.</span></p>

“His all” means all his object, or all that he did.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA175</span> The words <span style="color:#008080">hlqn l[</span>-” with what is worthless, “or base, or contemptible, are rendered, “<span style="color:#0000d4">ἐξουθενοῦντες</span>-regarding as nothing,” or despising, by the *Septuagint; “*cum ignominia-with reproach” or contempt, by the *Vulgate* and *Arabic; “*illusione-by illusion, by the *Syriac;* and “with false words, “by the *Targum.* The same phrase occurs in Jeremiah 8:11. The whole verse is there omitted by the *Septuagint;* the *Vulgate* has ad ignominiam-to reproach; the *Arabic,* “in jocos-for sport; the *Syriac, “*nugis-with trifles;” but the *Targum* is the same as here. None give the same version but the last. In the Complutensian Edition, which has this verse in Jeremiah 8:11, the Greek version is evidently a version of the *Vulgate.*

<!-- PAGE 1142 -->
The idea of “slightly, “or “superficially, “as rendered by *Blayney,* is not countenanced by any of the foregoing versions, nor can the original words bear this meaning. The word <span style="color:#008080">hlqn</span>, is found as a Niphal participle, and applied to man, as a despised, contemptible, or worthless being,-1 Samuel 18:23; Proverbs 12:9; Isaiah 3:5; Isaiah 16:14. But here it refers to the means used for healing, which, according to all the versions, was something contemptible, worthless, useless, and which is afterwards named, being no more than saying, Peace, peace, when in fact there was no peace.

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And healed have they the bruise Of the daughter of my people</span> <span style="color:#000080">with what is worthless, Saying, “Peace, peace;” and there was</span> <span style="color:#000080">no peace.-</span><span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA176</span> The *Syriac* is the only version that puts the first verb in an interrogatory form. “They have been confounded, “is the *Septuagint* and *Vulgate;* and similar is the rendering of the *Arabic* and the *Targum*. The verb, taken literally, it being in Huphal, may be rendered, “They have been put to shame, “or have been made to be ashamed; that is, they had been exposed to shame; but this shame they felt not, according to what follows. Their previous evils were enough to make them feel ashamed; but they had not that effect: hence entire ruin is denounced on them at the end of the verse. The rendering of the whole is as follows,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">15. Exposed to shame have they been, Because abomination</span> <span style="color:#000080">have they wrought: Neither with shame are they ashamed, Nor</span> <span style="color:#000080">how to be abashed do they know; Therefore fall shall they with</span> <span style="color:#000080">the fallen; At the time when I shall visit them, They shall</span> <span style="color:#000080">perish, saith Jehovah.</span></p>

There is no necessity to make this verse and the 12th of chap. 8 (Jeremiah 8:12) the same in every particular, as *Blayney* attempts to do. Both passages are the same in meaning, with a little variety in some of the words. The particle <span style="color:#008080">µg</span>, repeated, may be rendered by*, either* and *nor.* See Numbers 23:5. The verb <span style="color:#008080">µylkh</span> is an infinitive Huphal. It is rendered as an infinitive by the *Vulgate. “*They shall perish, “which is according to the *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* and the *Arabic,* is literally, “They shall be made to stumble.”*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA177</span> *Blayney* renders the word for “rest,” <span style="color:#008080">[wgrm</span>, “restoration;” but his long note is not satisfactory. It is rendered, strangely enough, by the *Septuagint, “*purification-<span style="color:#0000d4">ἁγνισμὸν</span>;” but by the *Vulgate,* “refreshment-refrigerium;” and by the *Syriac* and *Targum, “*restrequiem;” which seems to be its meaning, especially here, as it stands in contrast with the false peace promised by the false prophets.

<!-- PAGE 1143 -->

The representation is that of travelers, who, when doubtful as to the right road, are to *stand,* that is, to stop, to *look,* and also to *inquire.* There were several old paths before them, but they were to inquire which was the *good way,* and to walk in it. This was what Jehovah by his prophets had exhorted the people to do, who had false prophets among them; but they refused to do so. It is a relation of what God *had* done,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Thus has Jehovah said,- “Stand ye by the ways and look, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">ask, as to the paths of old, Where that</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the good way; And</span> <span style="color:#000080">walk ye in it, And ye shall find rest to your souls:” But they</span> <span style="color:#000080">said, “We will not walk</span> <span style="color:#000080">*in it.”*</span></p>

There were many paths of old, or of antiquity, as there are still; but there was one good way, the way of God’s word. That the way is old is no proof that it is good. Error’s ways are as old as the way of truth.*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA178</span> There are several MSS. which have w before this verb, and such no doubt was the copy of *Calvin.* But all the versions render the verb as an imperative, as no doubt it is-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA179</span> The version of the *Septuagint* is wholly inconsistent with the drift of the passage. The other ancient versions are materially according to our version. Several MSS. read <span style="color:#008080">w[dw</span> for <span style="color:#008080">y[dw</span>; and this renders the meaning much better. Then <span style="color:#008080">hd[</span> is “testimony” as well as “assembly, “which appears here very much without meaning. The two verses would then be as follows,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">18. Therefore hear, ye nations, And know the testimony which</span> <span style="color:#000080">is against them;</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">19.</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Yea,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">hear thou earth,- Behold, I am bringing an evil on this</span> <span style="color:#000080">people, The fruit of their own devices, Because to my words</span> <span style="color:#000080">they have not hearkened; And my law, they have ever rejected</span> <span style="color:#000080">it.</span></p>

The preposition <span style="color:#008080">b</span> is found after the verb, to testify, and is even rendered *to* or *against;* and coming after the substantive, testimony, it ought to be rendered the same.-*Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1144 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftA180</span> It is rendered “cinnamon” by the *Septuagint* and *Arabic*, “a sweet smelling reed” by the *Vulgate,* and “an aromatic reed” or cane by the *Syriac* and Targum. The literal rendering of the verse is as follows,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">20. For what purpose is this</span> <span style="color:#000080">*done*</span> <span style="color:#000080">to me? Incense, from Sheba</span> <span style="color:#000080">it comes, And the precious reed, from a distant land: Your</span> <span style="color:#000080">burnt-offerings,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*they are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">not acceptable, And your sacrifices,</span> <span style="color:#000080">they are not pleasing to me.</span></p>

The reed or cane was dried and powdered, and formed a part of the incense. The latter is mentioned first, and then one of its ingredients. Sheba and the distant land are the same. The same order is to be observed in the burnt-offerings and sacrifices; the finished act first, and then the previous act of presenting a sacrifice.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA181</span> The ancient versions render it, “from the end, or ends, or extremities, of the earth.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA182</span> It is rendered “a spear, “or a lance, by the *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* and the *Arabic;* but improperly “a shield” by the *Vulgate* and the *Targum.* It is not true that it ever means a shield. It was a short spear or javelin. “It is evident, “says *Parkhurst, “*that this word signifies neither the larger spear nor the shield, because it is distinguished from both. See 1 Samuel 17:6; 41:45; Job 39:23.”-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA183</span> Literally it is, “And on horses shall they ride.” Then the following line is, referring to the nation in verse 21,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Set in order</span> <span style="color:#000080">*it shall be,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">like a man for war, Against thee,</span> <span style="color:#000080">daughter of Sion. Then the next verse refers to the same, the</span> <span style="color:#000080">nation,- Heard have we the report of it; Relaxed have become</span> <span style="color:#000080">our hands, Distress has laid hold on us, The pain like that of</span> <span style="color:#000080">one in travail.</span></p>

The effect is first stated, the relaxation of the hands; then the cause, the distress and anguish they felt.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA184</span> This is addressed to the daughter of Sion: hence the verbs are in the singular number. There is no need for the change which *Calvin*, and also *Blayney*, adopt, though countenanced by the *Keri*, and some MSS., for the same is addressed in the next verse,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Go thou not forth to the field, And in the way walk not; For</span> <span style="color:#000080">the enemy has a sword, Terror</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">on every side.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1145 -->

“For the enemy, “etc., literally, “For sword *is* to the enemy.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA185</span> “Their impudence resembles brass, and their obstinacy may be compared to iron.”-*Lowth*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA186</span> This verse and the preceding have been amended, and for the most part conjecturally, by *Blayney*, and though with the approbation of *Horsley*, yet with no satisfactory reasons. That the Prophet was made as it were a fortress, appears from Jeremiah 1:18: and there is here an evident allusion to that, though his being made a watchtower, or a watchman occupying such a place, was for a different purpose. The two verses I thus render,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">27. A watchtower have I given thee among my people, A</span> <span style="color:#000080">fortress, that thou mightest know and try their way; Then we</span> <span style="color:#000080">are told what he had found them to be,- All of them</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the</span> <span style="color:#000080">apostates of apostates, Companions of the slanderer; Brass and</span> <span style="color:#000080">iron</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">all of them, Corrupted</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">they.</span></p>

“The apostates of apostates,” mean thorough, confirmed apostates, as “servant of servants” means the basest: “companions,” etc., is literally, “Walkers with, “etc. “All of them, “clearly belong to “Brass and iron, “as “they” follows “corrupted.” The ancient versions are not satisfactory, and the *Targum* is paraphrastic; but they give the general meaning. “Prover” or “examiner” is what the versions give for “watchtower.” “Fortress” is omitted in the *Septuagint*, the *Arabic*, and the *Targum*, and is rendered “strong” by the *Vulgate*. “The apostates” is left out by the *Septuagint* and the *Arabic*, and is rendered “princes” by the *Vulgate*, *Syriac*, and the *Targum*. For “companions of the slanderer, “the *Septuagint* and *Arabic* have “walking perversely<span style="color:#0000d4">σκολιῶς</span>;” the *Syriac* and *Targum*, “walking with guile-cum dolo;” and the *Vulgate*, “walking fraudulently-fraudulenter.” The word <span style="color:#008080">lykr</span>, “slanderer” is found in five other places,  Leviticus 19:16; Proverbs 11:13; 20:19; Jeremiah 9:4; Ezekiel 22:9.

In the first three passages it is rendered in our version “a talebearer, “but more correctly, a slanderbearer, or, as *Parkhurst* renders it, “a trader in slander.” It does not mean “a sharper, “as *Blayney* thinks. The passages in Proverbs are inconsistent with such an idea. There is no passage where it may not be rendered “a slanderer, “except Ezekiel 22:9; where it evidently means “slander.”-*Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1146 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftA187</span> The true reading of the third word in this verse is <span style="color:#008080">µt çam</span>, according to the *Keri*, many MSS., the *Septuagint* and the *Vulgate;* and <span style="color:#008080">µt</span> sometimes means “consumed.” *Pliny* says that they formerly used lead to separate the dross from the silver, as they use quicksilver now. Then the verse is to be thus rendered,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Burnt has been the bellows by the fire, Consumed has been the</span> <span style="color:#000080">lead; In vain</span> <span style="color:#000080">*has been*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the melting of the melted, For their evils</span> <span style="color:#000080">have not been separated.</span></p>

They had been in the furnace, but the lead intended to separate the dross from the silver, was consumed, and the melting did not succeed, for their evils, or their vices, were not separated from them. Hence in the next verse they are called reprobate silver.*-Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA188</span> The ancient versions, except the *Syriac*, render this in the imperative mood, “vocate-call ye them.” So also the *Targum*, and *Blayney* has adopted the same,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Reprobate silver call ye them, For reprobated (or, rejected)</span> <span style="color:#000080">them has Jehovah.</span><span style="color:#000080">*-Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA189</span> Though the ancient versions, except the *Vulgate*, render the verb to dwell, as an Hiphil, “cause to dwell, “as in our version, yet *Blayney*, as well as *Calvin*, follows the *Vulgate*, “And I will dwell with you in this place: “which seems more accordant with the context. Their boast was that God was dwelling with them, as the temple was his temple. Then when Shiloh, in Jeremiah 7:12, is referred to, God says that he set his name there: and no doubt the same thing is meant here.*-Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA190</span> The difficulty in the construction is removed by *Blayney*, who renders <span style="color:#008080">yrbd</span> as a participle, as it is in some other places, Psalm 5:6: Psalm 38:3; Psalm 63:11. His version is,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Trust ye not in those who speak falsehood, saying,- The</span> <span style="color:#000080">Temple of Jehovah, the Temple of Jehovah, The Temple of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah, are these.</span></p>

The *Septuagint*, the *Syriac*, and the *Arabic*, have “the Temple of the Lord” only twice, and the verb is in the singular number, “The Temple of the Lord, the Temple of the Lord it is.” The verb is the same in the

<!-- PAGE 1147 -->

*Vulgate*, only the words, as in Hebrew, and also in the *Targum*, are repeated thrice. The paraphrase of the latter is rather singular,-” Trust not in the words of the prophets of falsehood, who say, Before the Temple of the Lord ye worship, before the Temple of the Lord ye sacrifice, before the Temple of the Lord ye offer praise; three times a year ye appear before him.”

“These” mean, as *Gataker* thinks, these places or buildings; and *Lowth* and *Blayney* think the same. The repetition seems to denote the frequency with which the Jews used the words: they continually boasted of having God’s Temple among them. “The Prophet, “says *Henry*, “repeats it, because they repeated it upon all occasions. It was the cant of the times. If they heard an awakening sermon, they lulled themselves asleep again with this, ‘We cannot but do well, for we have the Temple of the Lord among us.’ It is common for those that are farthest from God to boast themselves most of their being near to the Church.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA191</span> What is to be understood by innocent blood in not only murder, nor principally; but the offering of innocent infants to Moloch, referred to in the 31st verse of this chapter.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA192</span> *Calvin* departs here from his former rendering in verse 3d. The words mean the same, “Then will I dwell with you.” So the *Vulgate.-Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA193</span> The three foregoing verses admit of a different construction, though the general meaning continues the same. It is usual to consider the words in the ninth verse as verbs in the infinitive mood; but they are participles, and stand connected with the previous verse. The *Targum* render them as personal nouns, “thieves, murderers, “etc.: but they are in Hebrew in the singular number, the collective singular being often employed in that language. The passage may be thus rendered,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">8. Behold, ye trust in words of falsehood to no profit,-</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">9. The thief, murderer, and adulterer, And the false swearer and</span> <span style="color:#000080">incense-burner to Baal, And the walker after foreign gods,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Whom ye have not known;</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">10. And ye come and stand before me In this house, on which</span> <span style="color:#000080">is called my name, And ye say, “We are freed To do all these</span> <span style="color:#000080">abominations,”</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1148 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">Or,</p>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And ye say, “He has made us free To do all these</span> <span style="color:#000080">abominations.”</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Blayney,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">following the</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Syriac,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">has rendered the</span> <span style="color:#000080">words,- And say, Deliver us, that we may practice all these</span> <span style="color:#000080">abominations.</span></p>

But what is most consistent with the passage is to consider the sentence as declarative, and not as a prayer. They considered themselves freed from guilt when they had offered their sacrifices. They thought themselves then at liberty to be immoral and also to be idolatrous. We might think such a state of blindness and infatuation impossible; but it has existed among those calling themselves Christians, and it exists now. *Gataker* mentions a common saying among ignorant Papists of the same import with what is said here, “We must sin to be shriven, and shriven to sin.” The turning of the grace of God into lasciviousness is the same thing.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA194</span> It is to be observed that one only of the vices mentioned in verse 9 is here referred to, the first in the catalogue. But as the Temple was the den of thieves, so it was also made the asylum of murderers, adulterers, and of idolaters. It seems then, that the Jews thought that by sacrifices they purchased immunity not only for theft, murder, adultery, false swearing, but also for idolatry, and that having sacrificed they were free to commit all these evils. How unaccountably strange is the conduct of deluded man!

The words “Which is called by my name, “are literally, “Which called is my name upon it, “an idiomatic mode of speaking, with which the *Welsh* exactly corresponds,<span style="color:#000080">Yr hwn y gelwir fy enw arno.</span>

The pronoun relative without a preposition is afterwards followed by a pronoun substantive with a preposition prefixed.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA195</span> The verb is in the past tense,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">I also, behold, seen have I, saith Jehovah.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">That is, He had seen all they did. If anything be put after “seen, “it should be “these things, “and not “it;” for the reference is to the</p>

<!-- PAGE 1149 -->

particulars before mentioned. See Psalm 10:14; Ezekiel 8:12.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA196</span> This was in the tribe of Ephraim, between Bethel and Shechem, Judges 21:19, about twenty-five miles north of Jerusalem. The Ark had been there more than 300 years. It did not return there after it was brought forth in the war against the Philistines. The place afterwards declined, and its ruinous state became a proverb. See Joshua 18:1; Psalm 78:60; Jeremiah 26:6, 9.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA197</span> *Blayney* thinks that the reference is not to the ruinous condition of Shiloh, immediately subsequent to the time when the Ark was removed thence, but to the devastation occasioned by the captivity of the ten tribes, which was a recent occurrence, this reason is, because they were then directed to go and see the place. But if the place had ever continued in a ruinous state, and was so at that time, there was every propriety in saying, “Go now and see it.” Besides, the argument is not so complete, as when its dilapidated state, occasioned by the sins of the people, when the Ark was thence removed, is referred to. “It is probable, “says *Henry*, “that the ruins of that once flourishing city were yet remaining: however, they might read the history of it, which ought to affect them as if they saw the place.”-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA198</span> The literal rendering of the two verses is as follows:-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">13. And now, as ye have done all these doings, saith Jehovah,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And</span> <span style="color:#000080">*as*</span> <span style="color:#000080">I have spoken to you, rising early and speaking, And ye</span> <span style="color:#000080">have not hearkened, And I have called you, and ye have not</span> <span style="color:#000080">answered;</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">14. I will also do to the house, On which my name is called, In</span> <span style="color:#000080">which ye trust, and to the place, Which I gave to you and to</span> <span style="color:#000080">your fathers, According to what I did to Shiloh:</span></p>

“The house” was the Temple, “the place” was the city: both are threatened with destruction. Then he says in the next verse, “And I will cast you from my presence.” The Temple and the city were to be destroyed like Shiloh; and *they* (“ you”) were to be dealt with as their brethren, the ten tribes, who had been driven into exile.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA199</span> This is hardly correct. The reference is to the state of the Jews at that time. The Jews were formed of two tribes, Judah and Benjamin. They

<!-- PAGE 1150 -->
