<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">were joined, after the captivity of Israel, by several of the remnants both of Ephraim and Manasseh, and of the other tribes. (2 Chronicles 34:9.) There is indeed mention made of some from the tribes of Ephraim, Manasseh, and Simeon, coming to worship at Jerusalem in the reign of Asa; but we do not read that they settled there.-*Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA200</span> There are here *three* things forbidden; their distinctive character is not correctly given in our version nor by *Calvin.* I render the verse thus,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And thou, be not an intercessor for this people, Nor raise for</span> <span style="color:#000080">them a cry and a supplication, Nor make an entreaty to me, For</span> <span style="color:#000080">I will not hear thee.</span></p>

That is, “Undertake not their cause as one who intercedes or mediates between a judge and a criminal, nor cry suppliantly for mercy, nor entreat me to be favorable to them.” He was not to be for them an intercessor, nor a deprecator of evils, nor a solicitor of favors. All the versions render the passage loosely.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA201</span> The ancient versions (the *Arabic* excepted) and the *Targum* render the word, cakes-*placentas.* It is only found elsewhere in Jeremiah 44:19.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA202</span> The *Septuagint* render the words here, “the host of heaven, “and in Jeremiah 44:17 and 19, “the queen of heaven.” The *Vulgate* in the three places, renders them “the queen of heaven, “-the *Targum,* “the star of heaven, “-and the *Syriac*, “the army of heaven, “in the two first places, but in the last, “the queen of heaven.” There are several MSS., in the three places, which insert the <span style="color:#008080">a</span>, so as to make the word “work, “or workmanship: but this change has evidently arisen from the *Septuagint.* But this word is never used to designate the work of the visible heavens: the word used in that case is <span style="color:#008080">hç[m</span>. See Psalm 8:3; Psalm 19:1; <span class="ages-code">&lt;19A225&gt;</span>Psalm 102:25; Psalm 163:5. Our version and the *Vulgate* are no doubt right. But what is intended by the queen of heaven is not the moon; for the word commonly used for the moon is always masculine, and the word generally used for the sun is commonly feminine. This may appear strange; but so it is. In South Wales the word for sun is always feminine, but in North Wales, masculine.

<!-- PAGE 1151 -->

In Deuteronomy 4:19, the sun, the moon, and the stars, as constituting the host of heaven, are mentioned together: these the first, as including all the rest, seems to be intended. Instead of “queen, “we should say in our language, “the king of the heavens.” We do not read that the Jews worshipped the moon; but the worship of the sun among them is specifically referred to and mentioned. See 2 Kings 23:11; Ezekiel 8:16. The Israelites adored the sun under the name of Baal, which was the Chemosh of the Moabites, and the Moloch of the Ammonites.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA203</span> The verb rendered “provoke, “means to disturb, to disquiet, to cause an annoyance, to irritate,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Is it I they are annoying, saith Jehovah? Is it not themselves, to</span> <span style="color:#000080">the confusion of their own faces?</span></p>

They were not disturbing, as it were, the repose of God, but their own. They could do no hurt or annoyance to God, but they were annoying and injuring themselves; and this would turn out to their own shame and confusion.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA204</span> The meaning is not so plain as it might have been made: the burntofferings were all consumed by fire; but a part of the peace-offerings and of other offerings was eaten. See Leviticus 1:9; and Leviticus 7:11-16. Then God says, by way of contempt, “Add your burnt-offerings to your other offerings, and thus you will have your appetites gratified.” Some derive the verb rendered “Add, “from <span style="color:#008080">hps</span>, which means to sweep together; and “collect together<span style="color:#0000d4">συναγάγετε</span>,” is the *Septuagint; “*heap together” is the *Syriac.* This comports better with the contemptuous strain of the passage,<span style="color:#000080">Your burnt-offerings sweep together To your sacrifices, and eat</span> <span style="color:#000080">flesh.-</span><span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA205</span> This exposition is ingenious and plausible, and all that is said is perfectly true: but it may be doubted whether it be the way to account for the mode of speaking here adopted. It is usual in Scripture, when two things are mentioned, and the one is of little or no importance in comparison with the other, to state the first negatively.

<!-- PAGE 1152 -->

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">“Labor</span> <span style="color:#000080">*not*</span><span style="color:#000080">, “says our Savior, “for the meat which perisheth, but</span> <span style="color:#000080">for that meat which endureth unto everlasting life.”</span> <span style="color:#000080">John 6:27.</span></p>

But it may be, that the reference here is specifically to the *day* in which the Israelites were delivered; for on that day, or at that particular time, (for the word *day* is not to be taken in its strict meaning,) obedience to his voice was the only thing which God required. See Exodus 15:26.

*Venema* thinks that reference is here made, not to the institution of sacrifices, but to the ground of the covenant. Sacrifices were not the condition of the covenant, but obedience. God did not say, “If you sacrifice to me, I will be your God;” but, “If you obey my voice, I will be your God, and you shall be my people.” When the law was delivered on Mount Sinai, there was no mention of sacrifices.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA206</span> The words are literally,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">And they walked in the counsels,-</span> <span style="color:#000080">In the resolutions of their evil heart.</span></p>

They not only *devised* their own ways, but *resolved* to walk in them. They formed their own counsels, and made resolutions to follow them, and they were the counsels and resolutions of a disordered and perverted heart. In rendering the last word “wickedness, “*Calvin* has followed the *Vulgate;* and our version, “imagination, “is the *Targum.* It is omitted in the *Septuagint,* and “desires” in *Syriac.* See note on Jeremiah 3:17.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA207</span> The words are,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And they were for behind, and not for before them;</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1153 -->
which seem to mean, that they were bent on turning back to their own ways rather than to go on in the ways of God. The version of the *Septuagint* is, They were for things behind, and not for things before; the *Syriac* and *Arabic, “*They retrograded and did not advance, “or go forward. The allusion seems not to be, as *Blayney* thinks, to refractory oxen under the yoke; but to those travelers who, when shewn the right way, go back instead of going forward. And this was especially true of the Israelites, who, after having left Egypt, wished often to return, instead of going forward to Canaan. Hence it is said, that they were going back to their old ways, and not going forward in the way which God bad pointed out to them. The phrase in Jeremiah 2:27, is of another kind, and ought not to be confounded with this.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA208</span> The former part of this verse would better connect with the former verse, than with this sentence. There is the copulative <span style="color:#008080">w</span>, “and,” before the verb “sent.” The sending of the prophets is mentioned in addition to the first command given to them. The passage may be thus rendered,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">And they went backward and not forward,</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">25. From the day in which your fathers came forth From the</span> <span style="color:#000080">land of Egypt, to this day: And I sent to you all my servants</span> <span style="color:#000080">the prophets, Every day rising early and sending;</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">26. Yet they hearkened not to me, Nor inclined their ear, But</span> <span style="color:#000080">hardened their neck; They have been more wicked than their</span> <span style="color:#000080">fathers.</span></p>

Such is the connection in all the ancient versions and in the *Targum.* The verb, rendered “they have been more wicked, “or “done worse, “is omitted by the *Septuagint* and the *Syriac;* but retained by the *Vulgate* and the *Targum,* and is found wanting in no MS.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA209</span> Our version is wrong, “obeyeth not;” for the charge against them is, that they did not *attend* or give ear to God’s voice: they would not hear it any more than the Papists at this day. This is evident from the previous verse.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA210</span> All the ancient versions and the *Targum* render <span style="color:#008080">hgwma</span> by <span style="color:#0000d4">πίστις</span>, fides-faith, fidelity or faithfulness. The first verb, “perished” or lost, is omitted by the *Septuagint* and *Arabic,* but is retained by the *Vulgate, Syriac,* and the *Targum.* The second verb is stronger than the first; faithfulness was lost from the mouth, yea, it was completely separated from it. <span style="color:#000080">Lost is faithfulness, yea, wholly separated from the mouth.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1154 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftA211</span> The emendations of *Houbigant,* adopted by *Blayney,* are by no means to be approved; for without the authority of any MSS. or versions, he changes the gender of these verbs in succession. It is a common thing in the prophets to call the people the daughter of Sion: and probably they are here so addressed, because the hair is an ornament to a female, and to cut it off is a token of deep distress.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA212</span> The word means what is unclean and filthy, “their filthy things.” They were the idols which were introduced into the Temple. They did this evil “before his eyes, “or in his presence, as the Temple was his habitation. They brought idols as it were into his very presence, and thereby literally violated the first commandment, which expressly forbad them to have other gods before him or in his presence. By what means did they evade this explicit command? In the same way as the Church of Rome justifies idolatry,-that idols are helps to devotion: but God regards them as abominations.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA213</span> This is the meaning according to all the ancient versions and the *Targum*. The *Syriac* is, “for want of place.” *Gataker* gives the same meaning. Our version follows *Pagninus,* and is substantially adopted by *Blayney* both here and in Jeremiah 19:11. Literally it is, “From no place, “that is, “From there being no place, “as *Calvin* says elsewhere.*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA214</span> The poetical singular is used throughout the verse,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And the carcase of this people shall be for meat To the bird of</span> <span style="color:#000080">heaven and to the beast of the earth, And there will be no</span> <span style="color:#000080">terrifier.-Ed.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA215</span> Though it be improper to render the word “consult;” yet the meaning is the same: they sought them, that is, their counsel and direction. They sought them in all emergencies. “To inquire of the Lord, “in Genesis 25:22, is literally “to seek the Lord;” and “to inquire of

God,” in Exodus 18:15, is literally “to seek God.” Indeed, to inquire of or to ask, that is, counsel, seems to be the most common meaning of the word. “Sought, “is the *Vulgate* and *Targum,-*” consulted, “is the *Syriac*,-and “cleaved to, “is the *Septuagint* and *Arabic;* and this is the idea of *Calvin.-Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA216</span> It would be better to render <span style="color:#008080">hnwma</span> before the verbs here as an adverb, *because, as,* or *inasmuch as.* There is a pronoun following every verb,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">2. And they shall expose them to the sun, And to the moon,</span> <span style="color:#000080">and to all the host of heaven: As they had loved them, and as</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1155 -->

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">they had served them, And as they had walked after them, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">as they had sought them, And as they had bowed down to</span> <span style="color:#000080">them, They shall not be gathered, nor buried; For dung on the</span> <span style="color:#000080">face of the land shall they be.</span></p>

The order here is from the principle to the action, and not the contrary, as is often the case: love-service-walking according to the rules prescribed-seeking counsel-and worshipping.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA217</span> The literal rendering of this verse is as follows,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And chosen shall be death rather than life by all the remnant,-</span> <span style="color:#000080">Who</span> <span style="color:#000080">*shall*</span> <span style="color:#000080">remain of this wicked family, In all the places of</span> <span style="color:#000080">such as</span> <span style="color:#000080">*shall*</span> <span style="color:#000080">remain, Whither I shall have driven them, Saith</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah of hosts.</span></p>

*Blayney* justly observes, that the participle in the second line is in apposition with “remnant, “as explanatory of it, and is not to be put in the genitive case, as in our version. A similar construction is found in Jeremiah 24:8. But there is no sufficient authority or reason for omitting the same participle after “places, “as is done by *Blayney.* Such repetitions are common in the prophets.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA218</span> Most agree in this view,-*Gataker, Venema, Henry, Lowth, Blayney,* and *Scott.* All the versions favor this view, giving two different meanings to <span style="color:#008080">bwç</span>, repeated in the last clause, except the *Syriac,* which gives this version, “Though they ought to repent, they yet do not repent.”-*Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1156 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftA219</span> The idea of revolt or apostasy is given by the ancient versions to the verb used at the beginning of the verse, and also to the noun which follows, and not that of rebellion, as by *Calvin.* The same meaning is given by *Gataker, Venema,* and *Blayney*; and they consider that Jerusalem is in apposition with “this people, “in this manner,-Why has this people, Jerusalem, Revolted with perpetual revolt? As it has been already observed, the verb <span style="color:#008080">bwç</span>, with all its derivations, means strictly to *turn,* but is used in the sense of turning to or from, that is, of returning or of departing. The context is our guide. It can hardly be supposed to have these two meanings in the same passage. All agree in giving it the idea of returning, at the end of the fourth verse, and at the end of this verse, the fifth; and in the three other instances in which it occurs here, they give it the idea of turning away or departing. I am disposed to think that it has the first meaning throughout the passage. I would render these two verses thus,-

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">4 Thou shalt also say to them, Thus saith Jehovah,- Do men</span> <span style="color:#000080">fall and not rise again? Does</span> <span style="color:#000080">*any one*</span> <span style="color:#000080">return and not return?-</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">5. Why,-often have this people returned, Jerusalem</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">returning</span> <span style="color:#000080">continually!- They hold fast deceit, they have refused to return.</span></p>

The hypocrisy of the people is the subject: they pretended to return, but did not really return; they were deceitful. It is a sort of a dialogue. The beginning of the next verse is an answer to the end of this,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">6. I hearkened and heard, “No: “thus they say:</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Yet*</span> <span style="color:#000080">no man has</span> <span style="color:#000080">repented of his evil,- Saying, What have I done? Every one</span> <span style="color:#000080">returns to his own course, Like a horse rushing into battle. The</span> <span style="color:#000080">charge of refusing to return was negatived,-</span><span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA220</span> It is curious the variety as to the names of birds in this verse, as given in the ancient versions: *Vulgate;* kite-turtle-swallow-stork; *Septuagint,* stork-turtle-swallow-sparrows; *Syriac,* stork-turtle-crane-swallow; *Arabic,* crane-turtle-swallow-birds; and the *Targum* is, stork-turtlecrane-swallow. The names in our versions seem to be the most correct, and are adopted by *Venema* and *Blayney*,-stork-turtle-crane-swallow; the same with the *Syriac* and the *Targum.-Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA221</span> The latter part of this verse has another meaning according to the ancient versions. They are substantially to this purport,<span style="color:#000080">Behold, surely to deceive</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is what*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the false pen of the scribes has done.</span>

The *Vulgate,* with which the rest materially agree, is as follows,<span style="color:#000080">Verily, falsehood has the false pen of the scribes wrought.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">As a proof of this it is added in the next verse, that those who pretended to be wise were made ashamed, etc. That the reference is made to the false glosses of the scribes, the expounders of the law, is confirmed by verse 11. I render the whole verse thus,-</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">8. How can ye say, “Wise</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">we, And the law of Jehovah</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">with us?” Indeed!-Behold, to deceive Has the deceptive pen of</span> <span style="color:#000080">the scribes served.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1157 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">He ironically admits that they had the law; but he refers to the false interpretation of the teachers; and in the next verse he mentions the *effect* on the pretended wise, and *the fact* as to God’s law,-</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">9. Ashamed have become the wise, They have been dismayed</span> <span style="color:#000080">and ensnared: Behold, the word of Jehovah have they despised;</span> <span style="color:#000080">And wisdom, what have they!</span><span style="color:#000080">*-Ed.*</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA222</span> It would be better to consider the *shame* in this verse as referring to the people, and the *want* of shame in Jeremiah 8:12, as applied to the teachers, the scribes, the false interpreters of the law, who promised peace, while there was no peace.-*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA223</span> The verb is here followed by <span style="color:#008080">b</span>: see note on Jeremiah 2:37.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA224</span> It would be more suitable to render “for” because, as it is explanatory of <span style="color:#008080">ˆkl</span>, “for this, “or, for this reason, at the beginning of the verse. This illative, and others too, are often used anticipatively,-

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">For this reason, give will I Their wives to strangers, their fields</span> <span style="color:#000080">to inheritors; Because from the least even to the greatest, Ever</span> <span style="color:#000080">one covets gain; From the prophet even to the priest, Every</span> <span style="color:#000080">one practices deception.-</span><span style="color:#000080">*Ed.*</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA225</span> See note on Jeremiah 6:14.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA226</span> This may be differently understood. There are here throughout the passage two parties spoken of,-the people, and the priests together with the prophets. The “wise” men, in Jeremiah 8:9, made ashamed were the people deluded by the priests and prophets. Those who felt no shame, mentioned in this verse, though their words proved false, were the priests and prophets: and hence we see the import of this expression here, that they were appointed to fall with the fallen, that punishment would reach them as well as the people.

This verse is somewhat different from the fifteenth of the sixth chapter, and may be thus rendered,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">12. Have they been ashamed, Because they have done</span> <span style="color:#000080">abomination? Even with shame they are not ashamed, And how</span> <span style="color:#000080">to blush they know not: Therefore fall shall they with the</span> <span style="color:#000080">fallen; At the time of their visitation They shall perish, saith</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1158 -->

The “abomination’ was the perversion of the law so as to justify idolatry and all kinds of wickedness.-*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftA227</span> The verse may be thus rendered,-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">13. I will gather their ingathering, saith Jehovah: No grapes</span> <span style="color:#000080">*shall be*</span> <span style="color:#000080">on the vine, And no figs on the fig-tree, Even the leaf</span> <span style="color:#000080">*shalt*</span> <span style="color:#000080">fade away; For I will give</span> <span style="color:#000080">*these*</span> <span style="color:#000080">to those</span> <span style="color:#000080">*who*</span> <span style="color:#000080">shall pass</span> <span style="color:#000080">through them.</span></p>

God threatens the Jews with the deprivation of the fruits of the earth. He would gather their fruits by means of their enemies, such as would pass through them; and there would be no limits for them. As to the last line, it is a common thing in Hebrew to leave out the relative “who” before a verb in the future tense. So it is in *Welsh-* <span style="color:#000080">Canys rhoddav y</span> <span style="color:#000080">*rhain*</span> <span style="color:#000080">iddynt hwy a dramwyant trwyddynt.</span>

The most literal and the most suitable to the context is this rendering.*Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftA228</span> The verb <span style="color:#008080">hmd</span> means, to be silent, to be reduced to silence; and the silence is that of inactivity, or of weakness, or of death. The silence of inactivity seems to be the meaning in the first instance, and the silence of weakness in the second: “Let us be silent, “or, let us rest; “God hath made us silent, “or, made us feeble, or, reduced to nothing our strength:-

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">14. Why do we sit still? be ye assembled, And let us go into</span> <span style="color:#000080">fortified cities, And let us be silent there; For Jehovah our God</span> <span style="color:#000080">has reduced us to silence; And he has given us to drink the</span> <span style="color:#000080">water of hemlock, Because we have sinned against Jehovah.</span></p>

That <span style="color:#008080">çar</span> is “hemlock, “or some poisonous herb, is evident from Hosea 10:4. “The water” seems to be the juice in this instance-”

the juice of hemlock.” It is rendered “the water of gall, <span style="color:#0000d4">u[dwr χολῆς</span>,” by the *Septuagint,* and the same by the *Vulgate; “*bitter waters, “by the *Syriac; “*water of bitterness, “by the *Arabic; “*the cup of malediction, “by the *Targum*. “Water of hemlock” is the rendering of *Blayney.*

*Horsley* renders the third and the fourth line as follows:-

<!-- PAGE 1159 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">And let us there sit in despair, Since the Lord our God hath</span> <span style="color:#000080">brought us to despair.-</span><span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA229</span> The word is <span style="color:#008080">hwq</span>, hoping, or longing, rendered by all the ancient versions, as though it were in the first person plural. It may be taken as a noun,<span style="color:#000080">The hope was for peace, but there was no good! For a time of</span> <span style="color:#000080">healing, but behold terror!-Ed.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftA230</span> The ancient versions and the *Targum* all differ as to the meaning of this word; and it is difficult to make the original to agree with any of them. The word, as in the received text, is a verbal noun from Hiphil, with a *iod* affixed to it, and is either a personal noun in the feminine gender, “my consoler, “or “strengthener, “meaning his own soul,-or a common noun, “my consolation, “or “strength, “meaning God. But *Schultens,* regarding the verb as signifying to smile or to laugh, and thinking that it means here the laugh of misery or of contempt, renders it “O thou (*i.e.,* the daughter of Sion) that grinnest at me for pain, “and sayest, “within me the heart is sick.” The *Targum* seems to favor this view, as it mentions the division of the people. *Blayney,* according to several copies, divides the word thus, <span style="color:#008080">ytyg ylbm</span>, and considers the one as a negative, and the other a verbal noun from <span style="color:#008080">hhg</span>, to heal, and renders the verse thus:<span style="color:#000080">Sorrow is upon me past my remedying,</span> <span style="color:#000080">My heart within me is faint.</span>

Still the simplest way, and the most suitable to the passage, is to take the word as a common noun, signifying consolation, comfort or strength, and to consider the words as addressed to God,<span style="color:#000080">My strength! within me</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">sorrow, Within me</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">my heart faint.</span>

“Faint, “that is, through grief. It is rendered “grieve, “or “is sorrowful, “by all the ancient versions and the *Targum.-Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">fta231</span> Literally it is, “*The* voice of the shout of the daughter of my people,” four words in succession, and three in regimine by juxtaposition. The *Welsh* is exactly the same, “Llev gwaedd merch vy mhobl” — Voice shout daughter my people. — Ed.

<!-- PAGE 1160 -->

<span style="color:#800000">fta232</span> The meaning of this verse is viewed by some differently. Their exile is considered as referred to at the beginning of the verse, “from a distant land,” — or literally, “from the land of the remote ones.” All the versions render the preposition “from,” and not “because of,” as in our version. The Prophet contemplates them as in banishment, and relates what they would say, and what answer God had for them: and they seem to have been thus contemplated to the end of the chapter, — <span style="color:#000080">19.Behold the voice of the cry of the daughter of my people From</span> <span style="color:#000080">the land of the remote ones, — “</span><span style="color:#000080">*Was*</span> <span style="color:#000080">not Jehovah in Sion?</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Was*</span> <span style="color:#000080">not</span> <span style="color:#000080">her king within her?” “Why! they provoked me with their carved</span> <span style="color:#000080">images, With the vanities of the foreigner.”</span>

Then follows the continuation of the cry in exile, — <span style="color:#000080">20. “Passed has the harvest, Ended has the summer, And we have</span> <span style="color:#000080">not been saved!”</span>

The “King,” in verse 19, is “Jehovah” in the former line. “The vanities of the foreigner” were idols: they were vanities, because they could do nothing, neither good nor evil. What made them gods were the imaginations of the infatuated and superstitious. The gods of many now are nothing better. Every notion of God is false but what is consistent with his word. The Socinian god is not the true God; it is the fiction of a perverted mind. Nor is the god of the thorough Papists anything better, nor the god of the Pharisee. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta233</span> To keep throughout the metaphorical character of this verse, it ought to be rendered thus, — <span style="color:#000080">For the bruising of the daughter of my people I was bruised, I</span> <span style="color:#000080">became black; Desolation possessed me.</span>

But taking the words as applied to the mind, divested of metaphor, we must render them thus, — <span style="color:#000080">For the sorrow of the daughter of my people I sorrowed, I</span> <span style="color:#000080">mourned; Astonishment possessed me.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">And this “astonishment” he explains in the next verse: there were means of restoration, and yet the people were not restored; at this he was astonished. — *Ed.*</p>

<!-- PAGE 1161 -->

<span style="color:#800000">fta234</span> As the whole passage, from the 19th verse, is anticipative, and represents the ease of the Jews in captivity, this verse is to be viewed in the same light, and rendered in the past tense, — <span style="color:#000080">22.Was there not balm in Gilead? Was there not a healer there?</span> <span style="color:#000080">Why then has not succeeded The recovery of the daughter of my</span> <span style="color:#000080">people?</span>

Whether balm or rosin be meant, it makes no great difference; its healing virtues had become proverbial; and in this sense it is to be taken here. *Kimchi* held that it was balm or balsam, which *Josephus* reports was first brought to Judea by the Queen of Sheba. But the tree which produced <span style="color:#008080">yrx</span>, was not an exotic, but indigenous in Judea, as it appears from Genesis 37:25, and 43:10; and it grew especially in Gilead, as it appears from this passage and from chap. 46:11. *Bochart* maintained that rosin is meant by the word, the gum drawn from the Terebinthus or the turpentine tree, which possesses strong healing virtues. It is rendered, “<span style="color:#0000d4">ρητίνη</span> — rosin,” by the *Septuagint,* the *Vulgate,* and the *Arabic;* and “cera — wax,” by the *Syriac.* “Healer,” or physician, is rendered “<span style="color:#0000d4">ἰατρὸς</span> — healer,” by the *Septuagint,* and “*medicus,*” by the *Vulgate, Syriac,* and *Arabic.* It appears that Gilead was not only celebrated for its healing gum, but also for its medical men.

The balm was the word of God, and the healer who applied it was the prophet or the teacher.

Perhaps the most literal rendering of the first two lines is the following, and the most suitable to express astonishment, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*The balm, not in Gilead!:*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Verily, a healer, not there! — Ed.*</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftB1</span> This verse is connected by some with the last chapter: and it seems to belong to it. It forms in all the Hebrew MSS. the 23d verse of the preceding chapter (Jeremiah 8:23). The phrase, <span style="color:#008080">ˆtyAym</span>, “who will give,” means a wish, “O that my head,” etc., or “May my head,” etc. The *Septuagint,* the *Vulgate,* the *Arabic,* and the *Targum* express it literally, “Who will give;” but the *Syriac* has, “O, I wish my head were turned into water.” — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftB2</span> This verse may be rendered thus, —

<!-- PAGE 1162 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">O that I, had in the desert the lodging of travellers, Then I would go</span> <span style="color:#000080">away from them; For all of them</span> <span style="color:#000080">*are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">adulterers, A company of</span> <span style="color:#000080">hypocrites.</span></p>

He preferred living in the temporary sheds of travellers, erected in the desert, rather than to live among his own people. How intolerably wicked they must have been! A company, or an assembly, a multitude: the word need not be deemed as retaining its primary idea. The meaning is, that the whole community, the whole people, were hypocrites; they pretended to worship and serve God, and at the same time were idolaters and treacherous and immoral in their conduct. The word for “hypocrites” is derived from one that means a garment, a cloak, a covering; and the verb means to act under a cover, to act deceitfully, or falsely, or hypocritically, or perfidiously. It is rendered “deceivers” by the *Septuagint,* “prevaricators” by the *Vulgate,* “liars” by the *Syriac,* “falsifiers” by the *Targum,* and “perfidious dealers” by *Blayney. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftB3</span> The ancient versions differ in rendering the first clauses of this verse: “They have bent their tongue like a bow; falsehood and not truth has prevailed over the land,” *Septuagint; —* “And they have stretched their tongue like a bow of falsehood, and not of truth; they have become strong in the land,” *Vulgate; —* “Their tongue as with their own bow have they shot; by falsity and perfidiousness they have become great in the land,” *Syriac;* — “Their tongues for falselhood have they bent, as a bow; and prevailed has perfidy over the land,” *Arabic. Blayney* makes a conjectural emendation, and *Houbigant* and *Horsley* make another; but neither is necessary. The literal version is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">And they bend their tongue, their lying bow; And not for truth are</span> <span style="color:#000080">they strong in the land.</span>

“Their lying bow,” or “their bow of falsehood;” it was a bow by which they shot lies; they employed their tongues for this purpose. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB4</span> The whole verse may he thus rendered, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And they deceive, every one his neighbor, And the truth they</span> <span style="color:#000080">speak not; They have taught their tongue the word of falsehood;</span> <span style="color:#000080">*With*</span> <span style="color:#000080">perverting have they wearied themselves.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1163 -->

The verb for “deceive” means to mock, to trifle with, to play the fool with. Their object was to befool their neighbors by cheating and deceiving them. “The word,” or the matter, “of falsehood,” is falsehood itself, or sheer falsehood. The *Vulgate* and the *Syriac’s* version is, “They have taught their tongue to speak falsehood.” To teach the tongue false-hood, was to habituate it to tell lies. The last line is differently rendered. The *Septuagint* deviates far from the original. The version of the *Vulgate* is, “They have labored to act unjustly;” and this comes near the meaning; only “to act unjustly” is rather to act pervertingly: they wrested and turned everything from its right course and meaning; and they labored in perverting things, until they wearied themselves. Falsehood requires more labor than truth. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB5</span> *Houbigant, Horsley,* and *Venema* have suggested emendations as to this verse, derived in some measure from the *Septuagint.* A part of the first word, <span style="color:#008080">˚tbç</span>, is connected by the *Septuagint* with the former verse; for it is divided into <span style="color:#008080">bç</span>and <span style="color:#008080">˚t</span> As to this division the preceding authors agree; but they differ as to the construction, though mainly the same in sense. The most probable is that of *Venema,* for he only divides the word, and gives this version, <span style="color:#000080">Burst forth does robbery upon robbery, deceit, on deceit; They</span> <span style="color:#000080">refuse to know me, saith Jehovah.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Burst forth, or *boil — exoestuat,* is certainly not the meaning of <span style="color:#008080">bç</span> which signifies to return, to turn, to restore, etc. Then the rendering would be, <span style="color:#000080">Return does guile for guile, deceit for deceit.</span></p>

The first word is rendered “usury — <span style="color:#0000d4">τόκος</span>” by the *Septuagint,* but <span style="color:#008080">˚t</span> means guile or fraud. See Psalm 10:7; Psalm 55:12; Psalm 72:14. The meaning is, that their dishonest dealings were reciprocal: the cheats cheated one another.

Our version agrees with the *Vulgate* and the *Syriac,* and is adopted by *Blayney:* and he concludes from the end of this verse, that the speaker from the 2d verse is not the Prophet, but God; who, adopting the language of man, intimates his wish to leave a people so wicked. But this conclusion is not necessary; for the prophets often introduce sentences of this kind. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1164 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftB6</span> All the ancient versions (except the *Vulgate)* and the *Targum* read, as though <span style="color:#008080">tb</span> “daughter,” were <span style="color:#008080">t[r</span> “wickedness,” as in Jeremiah 7:12. Both *Houbigant* and *Horsley* adopt this reading; and the first gives the meaning of “thus” to <span style="color:#008080">˚ya</span>, and not “how.” Then the verse would be, <span style="color:#000080">Therefore thus saith Jehovah of hosts, — Behold I will melt them</span> <span style="color:#000080">that I may try them; For thus will I do because of the wickedness</span> <span style="color:#000080">of my people.</span>

The rendering of the last line, according; to the received text, might be this, which is nearly the *Vulgate, —* <span style="color:#000080">For how should I deal</span> <span style="color:#000080">*otherwise*</span> <span style="color:#000080">with the daughter of my people?</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The passage runs better in this way, than according to the proposed emendation. — *Ed*.</p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#800000">ftB7</span> The word, <span style="color:#008080">fjwç</span>, means “killing” or slaying; see Genesis 22:10;</p>

Genesis 37:31; Exodus 12:6. Its primary meaning, as

Parkhurst thinks, is to shed, or to *drain off*, either blood from animals, or juice from grapes, or gold from dross. But it is used in the sense of slaying. The *Septuagint* and the *Vulgate* render it here, wounding,” — <span style="color:#000080">A killing arrow</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">their tongue; Deceit it speaks; With his mouth</span> <span style="color:#000080">does</span> <span style="color:#000080">*one*</span> <span style="color:#000080">speak peace to his neighbor, But in his heart he sets an</span> <span style="color:#000080">ambush for him.</span>

Literally, “his ambush,” that is, the ambush of which he is the object. This form of speech is often in Hebrew. See Job 28:10. “Penit ei insidias” is the *Vulgate. Blayney* gives a paraphrase, not a version, — <span style="color:#000080">But inwardly will he resolve to fall upon him by surprise.</span>

The future tense here, as in many other instances, is used as a present tense, and designed to shew the habitual practice of the people. The same is done in the Welsh language: the future tense is continually used to express a present action. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB8</span> It is not from this root, but from <span style="color:#008080">hwa</span>, to desire, in Niphal, <span style="color:#008080">hwan</span> to be desirable. The noun means *desirable* or pleasant *places* or spots. See Psalm 23:2; Jeremiah 25:37; Joel 1:19. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftB9</span> The whole verse is as follows, —

<!-- PAGE 1165 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">10. For the mountains will I raise weeping and wailing, And for the</span> <span style="color:#000080">pleasant places of the desert, a lamentation; For they are desolate,</span> <span style="color:#000080">without any one passing through, And they hear not the voice of</span> <span style="color:#000080">cattle; From the bird of heaven even to the beast, They have</span> <span style="color:#000080">migrated, they have gone away.</span></p>

The “pleasant places” were “desolate;” and “in the mountains” no “voice of cattle” was heard. No one “passing through” explains the desolation. The word is improperly rendered, “burnt up,” in our version and by *Blayney.* It was used before in the sense of desolation, Jeremiah 4:7; and it ought to be so rendered in Jeremiah 2:15. In the last line, the migration refers to birds, and the going away to the beasts. In none of the ancient versions is this distinction intimated. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB10</span> Somewhat a different view may be taken of this verse, as it will appear from the following version, — <span style="color:#000080">Who is the man that is wise, And he will understand this, — And</span> <span style="color:#000080">to whom the mouth of Jehovah has spoken, And he will declare it,</span> <span style="color:#000080">—</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Even*</span> <span style="color:#000080">why destroyed is the land, Made waste like the desert,</span> <span style="color:#000080">without a traveler.</span>

The wise man is the same with him to whom God had spoken: and what he had to understand and to declare was the reason why the land was destroyed. Then in the next verse God himself, by the mouth of his prophet, makes this known. “Made waste” is rendered “burnt up” by the*Septuagint*  and the *Vulgate,* but desolated, or desolate, by the *Targum, Syriac,* and *Arabic;* and no doubt rightly, as “without a traveler,” or one passing through, explains what is meant: in like manner, “without an inhabitant,” in the preceding verse, is an explanation of “the cities of Judah” being made “desolate,” or rather, entirely desolate. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB11</span> “Voice” is for God’s word; and so the *Targum* renders it: they did not walk *in,* or according to, his word*. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftB12</span> See Note on Jeremiah 3:17, 18.

<!-- PAGE 1166 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftB13</span> It is supposed that the Israelites made a difference between this word and God: they allowed but one God, but introduced Baalim, or inferior gods, and worshipped them. They tried to evade the charge of idolatry, by alleging that Baalim were mediators. But no excuse of this kind was admitted, as God everywhere imputed idolatry to them. Notwithstanding this *example,* and the distinct declaration of Scripture, that there is but one God and one Mediator, (1 Corinthians 8:5, 6; 1 Timothy 2:5,) the error, the awful error of praying to saints, etc., as mediators, has prevailed in the Christian Church! *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftB14</span> It makes no difference as to the meaning, but the true construction of this clause is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">Which their fathers have taught them.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The verb “to teach,” in Hebrew as well as in some other languages, admits of two objective cases. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftB15</span> But the reason why this herb is mentioned is its bitterness, — and not its wholesome effects. It was hence chosen to designate what is afflictive and distressing. This appears from. Proverbs 5:4, “bitter as wormwood.” *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftB16</span> See note on Jeremiah 8:14.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB17</span> I render the verses thus, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">17.Thus saith Jehovah of hosts, bethink yourselves; And call for</span> <span style="color:#000080">mourning women, that they may come;</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Yea,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">for the skillful send,</span> <span style="color:#000080">that they may come,</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">18.And hasten, and raise for us a wailing, That our eyes may pour</span> <span style="color:#000080">forth tears, And our eyelids drop down waters. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed.*</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftB18</span> The true version is that given by *Blaney* and approved by *Horsley, —*

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">Because they have thrown down our habitations.</span></p>

The ancient versions differ, but none give the meaning of our version, which is that of *Junius* and *Tremelius.* The whole verse is in the past tense: things are represented as having already taken place: — <span style="color:#000080">For the voice of wailing has been heard from Sion,-- “How have we</span> <span style="color:#000080">been plundered! We have been put to great shame; For we have left</span> <span style="color:#000080">the land, For they have thrown down our habitations.”</span>

The people are set forth as assembled in Jerusalem, having been made to quit the land, their dwellings having been pulled down. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1167 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftB19</span> The objection, that there is an inconsisteney in saying that death entered through the windows to cut off children from the street, disappears, when we consider that the Jews thought themselves safe because their gates were dosed and their city fortified. Be it so, says the Prophet, yet death will enter, if not through the gates, yet through the windows, and through our towers, and it will destroy the children who play in our streets, and our young men assembled in the squares and the wide places of our city. That those collected at Jerusalem are here meant, is evident from the nineteenth verse. Then, in the next verse, he refers to those who still continued in the country. And this accounts for the change made in the sentence, which has puzzled some expounders, and induced them to propose emendations. The verse may be thus rendered, — <span style="color:#000080">For climbed has death through our windows, It has come through</span> <span style="color:#000080">our towers, To cut off the child from the street, The young men</span> <span style="color:#000080">from the broad streets.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Though the gates were closed, yet death came in, not only through windows, or any openings there might have been, but also through strong towers. — *Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftB20</span> *Blayney* and some others connect <span style="color:#008080">rbd</span> with the former verse, and, on the authority of the *Septuagint,* leave out “thus saith Jehovah.” The *Vulgate* and the *Targum* retain the text as we have it, and the *Syriac* omits only the first word; and there is no MS. in favor of what has been proposed; and the meaning, as here represented by *Calvin,* is so evident, that no change is at all necessary, — <span style="color:#000080">22. Speak, Thus saith Jehovah, Fall also shall the carcase of man,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Like dung on the face of the field, Or like an handful of corn after</span> <span style="color:#000080">the reaper, And without any to gather</span> <span style="color:#000080">*it.*</span>

This would be the fate of such as remained in the country, whilst the greatest part had fled into Jerusalem. It is by keeping this distinction in view that the whole passage, from verse the seventeenth, may be rightly understood. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1168 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftB21</span> The next sentence is, “the valiant in his valor:” so the *Vulate;* but by the *Septuagint*  “the strong (<span style="color:#0000d4">ἰσχυρὸς</span>) in his strength;” by the *Syriac,* “the giant in his strength.” The *Targum* gives this paraphrase of the verse, “Thus saith the Lord, Let not Solomon, the wise son of David, delight in his wisdom; nor Sampson, the valiant son of Manoah, delight in his valor; nor Ahab, the rich son of Omri, delight in his riches.” It is military valor that is probably meant. They thought that they had wisdom in counsel, valor in defense, and wealth to procure aid, or to carry on war. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB22</span> *Blayney* and *Venema* agree with *Calvin* in thinking that “these” refer to such men as knew God and trusted in him, and not to “these” things, the mercy, judgment, and justice before mentioned. The versions and the *Targum* are ambiguous, like the Hebrew, except the *Vulgate,* in which “these” is in the neuter gender, referring to things, and not to men. I would render the verse thus, — <span style="color:#000080">But in this let him glory</span> <span style="color:#000080">who glories, That he understands, and that</span> <span style="color:#000080">he knows me, — That I am Jehovah, Who doeth mercy, judgment,</span> <span style="color:#000080">and justice in the land; For in these have I delighted, saith Jehovah.</span>

“Me” is left out in the *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* and the *Arabic.* “That he knows me” is only a more clear enunciation of the previous words, “that he understands:” what he understands or knows is then stated, “That I am,” etc. “Judgment,” when connected with justice, seems to refer to what the law forbids; and “justice,” to what the law enjoins. See Isaiah 56:1, where the command is, to “keep” or observe “judgment,” and to “do” or execute “justice.” God doeth judgment in that he doeth nothing wrong, contrary to what is right and just; and he doeth justice in the defence of what is right and just, and in making good what he hath promised. Judgment regards the negative part of the law, and justice the active part. In Jeremiah 22:3, we find both words, “judgment and justice,” or righteousness. Then, as it is usual with the prophets, the last is described first, “delivered is the spoiled:” afterwards judgment is set forth, it does “no wrong,” etc. But it is only when the two words occur together that they have these specific meanings; for both, occurring separately, have a much wider import. They are used together more than twenty times. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1169 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftB23</span> The exposition of the phrase given in this section is inconsistent with all the ancient versions and the *Targum:* it is what has been given by modern rabbins. “The shaven around the face,” is the *Septuagint;* “the shaven as to the hair,” the *Vulqate* and the *Targum;* and to the same purpose is the *Syriac* and *Arabic.* The word <span style="color:#008080">hap</span> is used in Leviticus 19:27, with distinct reference to the *side* or *corner* of the head, and of the beard; and the Israelites were forbidden to imitate the nations who shaved off those parts. *Parkhurst* renders the phrase (which occurs also in Jeremiah 25:23, and in Jeremiah 49:32) “trimmed on the sides,” that is, of the head; more literally, “the shaven on the side,” that is, of the head, or, “on the corner,” that is, of the beard. It was a phrase, though defective, yet no doubt well understood, as it is the case in other languages. The design of mentioning these seems to have been to class together such as had been expressly separated. I propose the following as the version of the two verses, — <span style="color:#000080">25. Behold the days are coming, saith Jehovah, That I will visit</span> <span style="color:#000080">every one circumcised,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Who is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">in uncircumcision, —</span>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">26.The Egyptians and Judah, Edom also and the children of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Ammon and Moab, And all the shawm on the side</span> <span style="color:#000080">*of the head,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">Who dwell in the desert; For all these nations are uncircumcised;</span> <span style="color:#000080">And all the house of Israel, —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*They are*</span> <span style="color:#000080">uncircumcised in heart.</span></p>

It is justly remarked by *Horsley* that the nations here mentioned practiced circumcision. They were hence circumcised, and yet in uncircumcision; and the Jews were like them: and the last line explains this apparent contradiction: they had the outward but not the inward circumcision. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftB24</span> Here the preceding lecture ends in the original; but in order to keep the chapters distinct, this section has been transferred to the present lecture. A similar arrangement is adopted as to the last lecture in this volume. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB25</span> The *Sept.* and *Vulg.* render it “according” — <span style="color:#0000d4">κατὰ</span> — juxta. It is omitted in the *Sur. Blayney* renders the line thus: — <span style="color:#000080">“Unto the way of the heathen conform ye not.”</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">We may view it as a negative, thus: — <span style="color:#000080">“No, the way of the heathen learn not.”</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1170 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">But it is most probable a typographical error for <span style="color:#008080">ta</span> as Jeremiah so writes at least in two other instances, Jeremiah 2:23, and</p>

Jeremiah 12:16. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB26</span> *Blayney* gives a similar explanation — “The sun, moon, and stars are said indeed to have been created and set in the firmament for ‘signs.’ (Genesis 1:14) But hereby is meant, that they should serve as natural marks, serving to distinguish, by their periodical revolutions and appearances, the various times and seasons; which, however, is a very different use from that of prognosticating future events, or causing any alteration in the fortunes of men.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB27</span> This is not correct, the verb is plural, and there is no different reading. The *Vulgate* has led *Calvin* and our translators astray here. The other versions never changed the form of the sentence. The verse may be thus rendered, — <span style="color:#000080">3.Verily, the customs of the nations are very vanity; For a tree</span> <span style="color:#000080">from the forest they cut down, — The work of the hands of the</span> <span style="color:#000080">worker with the ax!</span>

Then verbs in the plural number follow in the next verse, — <span style="color:#000080">4 With silver and with gold they beautify, With nails and with</span> <span style="color:#000080">hammers they fasten them, So that none may move</span> <span style="color:#000080">*them*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

The verb for “move” is in Hiphil; it means in Kal to totter, “that none may cause them to totter.”

But the *Septuagint* have rendered the verb “cut down” as a passive participle, <span style="color:#008080">twrk</span>, transposing the <span style="color:#008080">w</span>; and *Venema* takes this as the proper reading, — “For a tree from the forest is cut down.” But this does not run well with the following verse. The nations or heathens, is the nominative to all the verbs.

*Venema* renders the last line of the fourth verse, — <span style="color:#000080">That nothing may make</span> <span style="color:#000080">*them*</span> <span style="color:#000080">to reel.</span>

He considers that <span style="color:#008080">al</span> means often *“nothing;*” but it means also sometimes, “none,” or no one. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB27A</span> Cum faber incertus scanmum faceretne Priapm, Maluit esse *Demu.” — Hor. Lib.* 1, Sat. 8.

<!-- PAGE 1171 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftB28</span> The word is, <span style="color:#008080">ˆyam</span>: the <span style="color:#008080">m</span> here is not a preposition, but a formative, and the word means *none*. So all the versions and the *Targum* render it. The proper rendering of the verse is — <span style="color:#000080">None</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">like thee, Jehovah; Great</span> <span style="color:#000080">*art*</span> <span style="color:#000080">thou, And great</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">thy name,</span> <span style="color:#000080">in strength. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftB29</span> This verse is omitted in the *Septuagint.* The sentence, “To thee it belongs, is in the *Vulgate* and *Syriac,* “Thine is the honor;” and in the*Targum* and *Arabic,* “Thine is the kingdom.” *Blayney* gives this version, — <span style="color:#000080">“When he shall approach unto thee.”</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">But this has hardly a meaning here, and far less has the rendering of *Horsley, —* <span style="color:#000080">“Surely unto thee shall be the coming;”</span></p>

*i.e.,”* The general coming, the universal resort.” The bishop saw predictions everywhere. The explanation of *Calvin* is the most satisfactory. The act mentioned in the preceding clause, “fear,” is to be understood as the nominative case. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB30</span> All the versions and the *Targum,* as in the former instance, do not regard the <span style="color:#008080">m</span> as a preposition, but render the word by *“none,”* or no one. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB31</span> The word <span style="color:#008080">tjab</span> is rendered by the Versions and the *Targum, alike, equally* or *together.* Literally, “in one,” that is, altogether.*Calvin*  rather refines here. The verse may be thus rendered, — <span style="color:#000080">But they are together brutish and stupid; The teaehing of vanities</span> <span style="color:#000080">the wood is.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Literally, “the wood it,” but as *Gataker* says, the pronoun is often used in Hebrew for the substantive verb. The phrase is elliptical, no unusual thing in Hebrew. It may be thus, rendering in full, — <span style="color:#000080">The teaching of vanities, is the teaching of the wood,</span> <span style="color:#000080">or respecting the wood.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1172 -->
What they taught respeering the wooden idols was *“vanities,”* that is, very or extremely vain; for so the plural often means. The version of*Blayney,* after *Castellio,* and approved by *Horsley,* is the following, — <span style="color:#000080">“The very wood itself being a rebuker of vanities.”</span>

But it is a sentiment not suitable to this place. The most strict meaning of <span style="color:#008080">rswm</span> is restraint, and not rebuke; it often means teaching or instruction. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB32</span> The verse is literally thus, —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">9.Silver extended, from Tarsis it is brought, And gold from Uphaz,</span> <span style="color:#000080">— The work of the artizan And of the hands of the founder; Blue</span> <span style="color:#000080">and purple their garments, — The work of the wise, all of them.</span></p>

The *Septuagint* and *Arabic* have “Mophaz;” the *Vulgate,* “ Ophaz;” the *Syriac* and the *Targum,* “Ophir.” Probably the same country is meant, and that it had two names. “Blue” is rendered “hyacinth,” violet-color, by all the versions and the *Targum.*

“Uphaz,” according to *Bochart,* was a country near the Ganges in India, and the same with Ophir. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB33</span> The verse, literally rendered, is as follows: —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">“But Jehovah, God the truth</span> <span style="color:#000080">*he,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">God the life and King eternal; At</span> <span style="color:#000080">his wrath tremble will the earth, And not bear</span> <span style="color:#000080">will the nations his</span> <span style="color:#000080">indignation.”</span></p>

It is usual in Hebrew to put nouns for adjectives; divested of this peculiarity, and the future being taken for the present, the verse would run thus: <span style="color:#000080">“But Jehovah, the true God is he, The living God and King eternal;</span> <span style="color:#000080">At his wrath tremble does the earth, And the nations cannot bear</span> <span style="color:#000080">his indignation.”</span>

<!-- PAGE 1173 -->
“The true God,” and “the living God,” is the version of the *Vulgate* and of the *Targum;* but that of the *Syriac* and *Arab.,* “the God of truth,” and “the God of the living,” but no doubt incorrect. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftB34</span> Critics have unnecessarily suggested a doubt as to the genuineness of this verse, written in Chaldee. They have nothing but conjecture, and even for that conjecture there is no solid reason. It is not omitted in any MS. but one, nor by any of the early versions, nor by the *Targum,* though paraphrased more than what is commonly done. As to the context, it seems to be wholly necessary; for the meaning of the two following verses cannot be well understood without it, provided they are rendered correctly. I shall first give the three verses, and then point out the connection: — <span style="color:#000080">11. Thus shall ye say to them, “The</span> <span style="color:#000080">gods, who have not made the</span> <span style="color:#000080">heaven and the earth, Perish from the earth, even from under</span> <span style="color:#000080">heaven, shall they:</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">12. He who made the earth through his power, Who has set in</span> <span style="color:#000080">order the world by his wisdom, And through his understanding</span> <span style="color:#000080">expanded the heavens, —</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">13. At his voice when given, Abundance of waters is in the</span> <span style="color:#000080">heavens, For he brings clouds from the extremity of the earth;</span> <span style="color:#000080">Lightnings for rain he makes, And sends forth the wind from his</span> <span style="color:#000080">treasures.”</span></p>

The Prophet’s object was to shew that the Creator of the world is its ruler. As false gods did not create the world, they do not rule it. The *name* of the true God is not given in these verses, nor are the gods of the Chaldeans specially named. The gods who were no creators are alone mentioned, and contrasted with them is he who made all things; and of him he says, that “at his voice when given,” or literally, “at the voice of his giving,” abundance of waters appear, which he brings from the extremity of the earth. He states things as they appear; clouds arise from the horizon, said here to be the extremity of the earth. Then he mentions the most terrific things in nature, thunders, lightnings, and storms, (for that is what is meant here by wind,) as being under the entire control of him who made the heaven and the earth. Thus we see that when the passage is rightly understood, the eleventh verse is necessary as a portion of the context. “He who made,” etc. *He* is put as a sort of nominative absolute, as”gods” is in the former verse. This kind of phraseology is often to be met with in Hebrew. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1174 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftB35</span> The first, clause of this verse is rendered by the *Sept.* and *Vulg.,* “Foolish has become every man by knowledge;” by the *Sp.,* “Foolish have all men become without knowledge;” the *Arab.* and the *Targ.* convey the same idea with the last. *Gataker* takes this view and gives this version, “Every man is become brutish for want of knowledge.” But as the framers of idols were called, in Jeremiah 10:9, “wise” or cunning men, it is more probable that their boasted knowledge is what is meant here. The verse may be thus rendered — <span style="color:#000080">14. Brutish has every man become by</span> <span style="color:#000080">*his*</span> <span style="color:#000080">knowledge; Disgracefully</span> <span style="color:#000080">has every founder done as to the graven image, For deception</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">his</span> <span style="color:#000080">cunning; And no spirit is in them.</span>

To render the different parts of this verse correspondent, it is necessary to take <span style="color:#008080">çybwh</span> as a Hiphil. The connection is between the first and last line, and between the two middle lines. Eyery man, both the carver and the founder, or melter, were brutish, in employing their knowledge and skill in making idols or images, because there was, after all their toil, no spirit, no life in them. Then the founder acted shamefully in taking the carved thing or image, to cover it with gold or silver, because what he melted was a mere deception.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">This verse is no prediction, but a representation of the extreme folly and stupidity of idol — makers. This is confirmed by the following terse. — *Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftB36</span> So, substantially, is the version of the *,Sept., Vulg., Syr*., and — *Arab.,* — “ridiculous — worthy of laughter — foolish — ludicrous.” But the word means no such thing. The verb <span style="color:#008080">h[t</span> means to wander, to err, to go astray; in Niphal, to be led astray, ‘to be deceived; and Hiphil, to lead astray, to seduce, to deceive; and it is a Hiphil participle in Genesis 27:12. It is here a reduplicate noun; and *Blayney* takes it as referring to persons, and not as an abstract noun — those who greatly err; and this is the best view, as the Prophet has been throughout describing the idol — makers — <span style="color:#000080">Vanity are they (</span><span style="color:#000080">*i.e.,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the idols,) The work of the grossly deluded:</span> <span style="color:#000080">At the time of their visitation they shall perish;</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">*that is,* the *grossly* deluded.</p>

He had before threatened ruin to idols; but he now threatens their makers. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB37</span> *Scott* quotes a sermon of *Mede,* in which he says, “Ye have heard the state of the times, wherein this prophecy is commanded; now let us consider the event. We have heard of the admired oracles of the <!-- PAGE 1175 --> Gentiles, of Apollo at Delphos, of Jupiter Ammon in Egypt, etc.; but all of them are long since perished. Where is now Bel, the god of Babylon, Nisroch, the god of Assyria, Baal and Astaroth, the gods of Zidonians, Milcom of the Ammonites, Chemosh of Moab, and Tammuz of the Egyptians? Even these also are perished with their names.” The partial fulfillment of this prophecy is an evidence of its complete fulfillment, when “the spirit of evil,” as *Scott* says, “whom all idolaters worship, shall be confined to the bottomless pit.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB38</span> This clause is left out in the *Septuagint,* but retained by the *Vulgate,* the *Targum,* and the *Syriac,* though “rod’ is rendered “tribe” in the last; and so it may be rendered, for <span style="color:#008080">fbç</span> means a tribe as well as a rod or scepter: and this meaning is the most suitable. God was the portion of Israel, and Israel was the tribe or nation whom God inherited or possessed as his inheritance, there being no other nation so favored. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftB39</span> As to these two verses the early versions all differ from one another, as well as from our version and that of *Calvin.*  The *Targum* comes the nighest to our version. I offer the following rendering, — <span style="color:#000080">17.Gather from the land thy gains, Thou who dwellest in a</span> <span style="color:#000080">fortress!</span>

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">18.For thus saith Jehovah, — Behold I will sling out The</span> <span style="color:#000080">inhabitants of the land at this time, And will fortress them, that</span> <span style="color:#000080">they may be taken.</span></p>

The first verse is spoken ironically, recommending what they were doing. Then the Lord says what he would do: They were gathering their goods into fortresses in order to secure them, and the Lord says that he would violently fortress (as the word means literally) or drive into fortresses all the inhabitants of the land, and would do so, that they might be found or taken, that is, captives; there would be no need of collecting the people, for they would be driven into fortified cities, where the enemies would find them. This seems to be the meaning of this verse, which *Horsley* deemed “very obscure,” and elucidated “by no expositor.” — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftB40</span> Our translation, as to this verse, is nearly the *Syriac.* The *Septuagint* and *Arabic* have wandered much from the original; and so have the

<!-- PAGE 1176 -->

*Vulgate* and the *Targum* in some degree. The most literal is the version of *Calvin.* The terms here used, *bruising, smiting,* are commonly employed to designate great trouble and affliction, or distress; and this distress he describes in the verse that follows; and in the twenty-first verse the cause of it is set forth. And the distress corresponds with what he says in the eighteenth verse, where he says that the inhabitants would be driven from the land into fortresses, so that he would have none to set up his tent. All these verses seem connected. The literal rendering of this verse is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">19.Woe is to me, because of my bruising, (distress;) Grievous is</span> <span style="color:#000080">my stroke; I have said, — Surely, this</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">grief! but I must bear it.</span>

Then he proceeds to state his distress: he had none even to assist him to pitch his tent, the people having all been driven to fortified cities. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB41</span> I should render the verse as follows —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">My tent, it is laid waste, And all my curtains, they are broken; My</span> <span style="color:#000080">sons, they have left me, and there are none of them; No one extends</span> <span style="color:#000080">any more my tent, and sets up my curtains.</span></p>

When the noun precedes its, verb in Hebrew, I consider that it ought commonly to be rendered as above. “There are none of them,” that is, with me; not that they “were not,” that is, that they were dead. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB42</span> The meaning of the verb <span style="color:#008080">lkç</span> here is determined by the verb <span style="color:#008080">r[b</span> at the beginning of the verse: it is what is the reverse of that. Now <span style="color:#008080">r[b</span> is a verb derived from the name, which means a beast. To be like the beast is to be ignorant, stupid, void of reason and understanding: then <span style="color:#008080">lkç</span> means here to act with knowledge, like one who possesses mind and reason. But then the shepherds did not act but like beasts who have no understanding. Then the verse may be thus rendered, — <span style="color:#000080">20. For stupidly-ignorant have become the shepherds, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah they have not sought; Therefore wisely have they not</span> <span style="color:#000080">acted, And every one from their pastures is scattered.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1177 -->
The “scattering” was from the land or country to the fortified towns, referred to in Jeremiah 10:18. They left the country, like sheep quiting their shepherds’ pastures, and visited towns. Then, in the next verse, the Prophet says, that even the towns also would be destroyed. In the first instance God would terrify them, and fling them, as it were, from the land, so that they would take shelter in fortresses: this would be owing to the foolish conduct or their shepherds. They would be driven, then, that their enemies might more easily find or take them: and in the following verse he announces the approach of their enemies who were coming to lay waste their towns.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">All the versions give the idea of knowledge or wisdom to <span style="color:#008080">lkç</span> here; but the *Targum*, that of prosperity. To act foolishly is what they all render the verb <span style="color:#008080">r[b</span> *— Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftB43</span> The verse may be thus rendered, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">A sound is heard! — behold it comes, Even a great commotion,</span> <span style="color:#000080">from the land of the North, To make the cities of Judah a</span> <span style="color:#000080">desolation, The habitation of dragons.</span></p>

*Blayney* is right in taking the first words by themselves, but, “Hark, a voice!” is not a true version, <span style="color:#008080">h[wmç</span> is here a passive participle. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB44</span> Literally rendered the verse is as follows: —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">I know, Jehovah, That not to a mortal</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">his way; Nor</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*it*</span> <span style="color:#000080">for man</span> <span style="color:#000080">to walk And to stablish his steps.</span></p>

Such substantially is the meaning of the *Targum,* and of all the versions, except the *Syriac,* which *Blayney*  has followed thus: <span style="color:#000080">I know Jehovah, that his way is not like that of men, Nor like a</span> <span style="color:#000080">human being doth he proceed and order his going.</span>

This construction is wholly inadmissible. Had Jehovah been in the objective case, it would have <span style="color:#008080">ta</span> before it. See 1 Samuel 3:7. Then the rest of the verse is a paraphrase and not a version; and such a paraphrase as the original will not bear. To “walk” and to “stablish” are in the same predicament, both infinitives; and so they are rendered in all the versions and the *Targum.*

<!-- PAGE 1178 -->
The design of the passage seems to be more correctly intimated by *Gataker* than by *Calvin:* — “Lord, we know well, that this army cannot come in but by thy permission; but since thou art resolved to chastise us, we beseech thee, in wrath remember mercy.” So in the next verse the Prophet says, “O Lord, correct me, but with judgment.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB45</span> Or, as the French version has it, “does not reach the burden and knot of the subject.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftB46</span> James 4:13.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB47</span> Proverbs 16:1.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB48</span> Epist. 107, ad Vitalem.

<span style="color:#800000">ftB49</span> The word judgment, though usually given as the version of the original word, does not convey its meaning here. Of the twelve senses mentioned by *Johnson* as belonging to the word judgment, not one of them is applicable to this place. There is perhaps not a word in any language which includes all the ideas conveyed by a word of a similar general import in another. The word <span style="color:#008080">fpçm</span> is rendered in our version, “judgment,” Exodus 23:6, — “manner,” 1 Samuel 27:11, — “custom,” 1 Samuel 2:13, — “ordinance,” Isaiah 58:2, — “due,” Deuteronomy 18:3, — “right,” Deuteronomy 21:17, — “measure,” Jeremiah 30:11; the last is in the sense of moderation; and this is its meaning here; or, it may be rendered, “due measure.” <span style="color:#000080">Chastise me, Jehovah, but yet in moderation; Not in thy wrath, lest</span> <span style="color:#000080">thou diminish me,</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">*or*, <span style="color:#000080">render me</span> <span style="color:#000080">small. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1179 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftB50</span> The *Septuagint* and *Arabic* render this verse as though spoken by the people, “chastise us,” etc., and the last clause, “lest thou make us few.” The *Targum* has, “chastise them,” and, “lest they be diminished.” These are interpretations and not versions. The *Vulgate* and the *Syriac* render the Hebrew literally, “chastise me,” and the last clause, “lest thou reduce me to nothing,” or, according to the *Syriac,* “to a small nmnber,” which is literally the original; and this verb clearly shews that this verse was spoken, as *Calvin* observes, in the name of the people: but diminution, and not destruction, is meant, as the verb has never the latter meaning. Hence our version is wrong, and also *Blayney’s,* “lest thou crush me to atoms.” Diminution, and not annihilation, is what the word means; and this diminution was one of the judgments that would come upon them in case of disobedience, as mentioned by Moses, Leviticus 26:22. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftB51</span> *Blayney* for no good reason has omitted the verb “consumed,” following the *Septuagint* and one MS. The *Vulgate,* the *Syriac,* and the *Targum,* retain the two verbs.
