So far is the last verb from being without meaning, as this author says, that it has an especial emphasis, it being stronger than the preceding verb, — <span style="color:#000080">24.Pour forth thine indignation on the nations, Who know not thee,</span> <span style="color:#000080">and on the families, Who on thy name have not called; For they</span> <span style="color:#000080">have devoured Jacob, Yea, they have devoured him and consumed</span> <span style="color:#000080">him, And his habitation have they made desolate. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC1</span> So the *Vulgate*  and the *Targum,* but the *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* and *Arabic,* have the verb in the singular number, “and thou shalt say.”

The <span style="color:#008080">µ</span> at the end of the verb may be rendered “them;” so *Blayney* regards it. We may consider the end of this verse and the following as parenthetic; otherwise the particle “this” seems singular. It will thus appear to be “this covenant which I commanded your fathers.” Still the whole passage seems not to run well. I am disposed to render <span style="color:#008080">tazh</span>, “even these,” and to put a part in a parenthesis, thus, — <span style="color:#000080">2. Hear ye the words of the covenant, even these, (and thou shalt</span> <span style="color:#000080">speak them to every man of Judah and to the inhabitants of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jerusalem,</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">3. and thou shalt say to them, Thus saith Jehovah, the God of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Israel,)</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">4. “Cursed is the man who hearkens not to the words of the</span> <span style="color:#000080">covenant, even these, which I commanded your fathers in the day I</span> <span style="color:#000080">brought them up from the land of Egypt, from the iron furnace,</span> <span style="color:#000080">saying, “Hearken to my voice, and do ye according to all that I</span> <span style="color:#000080">shall command thee; and ye shall be to me a people,</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">5. and I shall be to you a God; that I may confirm the oath which I</span> <span style="color:#000080">have sworn to your fathers, to give them a land flowing with milk</span> <span style="color:#000080">and honey, as it is this day.” — And I answered and said, Amen, O</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah</span> <span style="color:#000080">*— Ed.*</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1180 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftC2</span> *Gataker* says, “It is not unlikely that the Prophet held out the book or volume of the law, wherein the covenant was engrossed and recorded, then in his hand.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC3</span> There is no need of any alteration in the text, as proposed by some: the literal rendering is, “Hearken to my voice, and do ye according to all that I shall command you.” The <span style="color:#008080">µta</span> “ye,” after “do,” seems to bc placed there instead of with “heaarken.” Some MSS. have <span style="color:#008080">µtwa</span>, which is evidently wrong. It is only the *Targum* that countenances this reading: all the versions read according to the meaning given above. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC4</span> “Establish — <span style="color:#0000d4">στήσω</span>,” is the *Septuagint;* “awaken — suscitem,” is the *Vulgate;* “perform,” is the *Syriac;* “confirm,” is the *Targum.* “To make to stand” is the literal meaning of the verb. Hence the most correct word is “confirm.” The connection of this verse is not with the immediately preceding words, but with “Hearken” and “do,” etc., at the middle of the former verse. Hearken and do, that I may confirm the oath, etc. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC5</span> On the meaning of these words, see a note in vol. 1.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC6</span> There is certainly an incongruity in taking the expression, “the words of the covenant,” in two different senses. The verse is omitted in the *Septuagint,* but retained in the other versions and the *Targum.* This clause, in the *Vulgate* and *Syriac,* is thus given: *“I* have brought on them the words of this covenant.” The *Targum* is, “I have brought vengeance on them, because they undertook not the words of this covenant.” To bring words on one, seems to mean to enforce, to enjoin them. I cannot find the phrase anywhere else. Taken in this sense, the expressions will be wholly suitable to the rest of the passage, which I render thus: <span style="color:#000080">6. Then said Jehovah to me, Proclain these words in the cities of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Judah, and in the streets of Jerusalem, saying, — Hear the words of</span> <span style="color:#000080">the covenant, Even these, and do them:</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">7. Verily, testifying I testified to your fathers In the day I brought</span> <span style="color:#000080">them from the land of Egypt; And to this day, early-rising and</span> <span style="color:#000080">testifying, Saying, “Hearken to my voice:”</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1181 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">8. Yet they hearkened not, nor bent their ear, But walked, every</span> <span style="color:#000080">one, according to the resolutions Of their own wicked heart; Yea, I</span> <span style="color:#000080">urged on them all the words of the covenant., Even these, which I</span> <span style="color:#000080">commanded them to do; But they did</span> <span style="color:#000080">*them*</span> <span style="color:#000080">not.</span></p>

To “testify,” rather than to “protest,:’ is the meaning of the verb, when followed by <span style="color:#008080">b</span>, as here. To this testifying was added that of urging or pressing on them the duty of attending to all the words of the covenant; but all was to no purpose. To introduce punishment here comports not with the passage. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC7</span> Rendered “<span style="color:#0000d4">σύνδεσμος</span>, binding together,” by the *Sept., —* ““conjuratio, confederacy, *or* conspiracy,” by the *Vulg..* and *Arab., —* “rebellion,” by the *Syr.* and *Targ., —*  “combination,”  as given by *Gataker* and *Blayney,* would express better the meaning of the original word. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC8</span> There is here an oversight. “Israel” is not mentioned here, but the men of Judah and the inhabitants of Jerusalem. “Israel” is mentioned at the end of the next verse, as having with “Judah” annulled the covenant.*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC9</span> The *Sept.* have “<span style="color:#0000d4">τῶν πρότερον</span> — who were before:” the *Vulg.* improperly joins it with “iniquities,” — “ the former iniquities of the fathers;” the *Syriac* renders it “ancient,” and the *Targum,* “former,” both connecting it with “fathers.” The word means the “first,” rather than the “former.” If we take it as connected with “fathers,” then the first fathers with whom the covenant, after they came out of Egypt, was made, are meant; but it may be taken as in apposition with “fathers;” then the first who refused to hear God, are referred to. Taking this view, we may render the verse thus — <span style="color:#000080">10. They have turned to the iniquities of their fathers — The first</span> <span style="color:#000080">who refused to hearken to my words; And they have walked after</span> <span style="color:#000080">alien gods to serve them: Annulled have the house of Israel and the</span> <span style="color:#000080">house of Judah My covenant, which I made with their fathers.</span>

The word for “iniquities” means perversions, distortions, the turning of things to purposes not intended. These are the kinds of iniquities which are meant. Perverting the truth rather than denying or renouncing it, had ever been the sin of the Jews. Instead of worshipping God

<!-- PAGE 1182 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">himself, they worshipped him by means of idols, and through the mediation of inferior gods. This was the perversion. Alien gods were mediators; hence they never renounced God’s worship. But God deemed this as an annulment of his covenant, by which they were required to worship him alone. — *Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC10</span> The literal rendering is as follows: —

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">11. Therefore thus saith Jehovah,- Behold, I will cause to come on</span> <span style="color:#000080">them an evil, From which they shall not be able to go forth: And</span> <span style="color:#000080">they shall loudly cry to me, But I will not hearken to them.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The third line in Welsh is literally the Hebrew, — <span style="color:#000080">Yr hwn his gallant vyned allan ohono. Which they will not be able</span> <span style="color:#000080">to go forth from it.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The verb <span style="color:#008080">q[z</span> is not merely to cry, but to cry loudly, or vehemently, or clamorously; the effect of great distress impatiently endured.Our version and *Blayney* are wrong in rendering <span style="color:#008080">w</span> And thought It is not what *may* have been, is meant, but what *would* be. It is expressly foretold what they would do; and corresponding with this are all the versions and the *Targum. — Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC11</span> But the most obvious meaning of the passage is, that the Jews would first cry to God, and that being not heard, they would then cry to alien gods. Hence our version renders the <span style="color:#008080">w</span> at the beginning of this verse, “Then,” and rightly too: so does the *Syriac,* though the other versions render it “And,” as *Blayney* does: and if so rendered, the connection would appear the same, — <span style="color:#000080">And go shall the cities of Judah and the inhabitants of Jerusalem,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And they shall loudly cry to the gods, To whom they burn incense;</span> <span style="color:#000080">But saving they will not save them At the time of their calamity.</span> <span style="color:#000080">—</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC12</span> The word is <span style="color:#008080">tçb</span>. “*Bosheth,* shame,” says *Lowth,* “was a nickname for Baal. (See Hosea 9:10.) So Jerubbaal is called Jerubbosheth in 2 Samuel 11:21.” The word is left out in the *Septuagint;* the other versions and the *Targum* render it differently; its meaning was evidently not understood. It may be rendered here “baseness,” or a base thing; the last clause is explanatory of this, —

<!-- PAGE 1183 -->

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Ye have set up altars for a base thing —</span> <span style="color:#000080">Altars to burn incense to a Baal.</span></p>

By putting the indefinite article we avoid the contrariety which *Calvin* refers to. It is given in the singular number in all the versions except the *Vulgate,* which has Baalim. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC13</span> The connection of this verse has not been pointed out by *Calvin.* It begins with “For,” or because; so that a reason is given for what has been said previously, and that is not found in it he immediately preceding verse, but at the end of the 11th, “I will not hearken unto them;” then what is said here is given as a reason. But if we render <span style="color:#008080">yk</span> “though,” as it is often done, and not “For,” the connection is with the next preceding verse; their gods would not save them, “though” they were as many as their cities, etc. This seems to be the most natural connection. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC14</span> See a note in vol. 1. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC15</span> “Holy fleshes,” <span style="color:#0000d4">κρέα a[gia</span>, carnes sanctae, is the version of the *Septuagint* and *Vulgate,* and “holy flesh” is the *Syriac;* but the *Targum* has “the worship of my sanctuary.” *Blayney* renders it “holy flesh.” The word <span style="color:#008080">çdq</span> means holy, or holiness, and <span style="color:#008080">çdqm</span> is the sanctuary. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC16</span> This verse has been varicously rendered and explained. The versions all differ, and the *Targum* too; and none of them seem to render the original correctly. *Blayney,* following the *Septuagint,* has introduced corrections, but not authorized by any MSS. There is no different reading of any consequence. The literal rendering I consider to be as follows: — <span style="color:#000080">15.What, as to my beloved,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">in my house her doing? Is not her</span> <span style="color:#000080">plotting with many?- Yea, the holy flesh do they take away from</span> <span style="color:#000080">thee; When thou doest evil against me, then thou exultest.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1184 -->
The word for “plotting” does not mean “lewdness,” or “abomination,” as rendered by all the versions, but devising, contriving, scheming, machinating; the reference is to the scheme of uniting the worship of God with the worship of idols. The *Targum* gives the idea, “they have taken counsel to sin greatly.” All the versions agree in giving a Hiphil meaning to <span style="color:#008080">wrb[y</span>, cause to pass from — to remove or take away. The “many” who advocated the worship of idols took away the holy flesh — the sacrifices, and took them away from her, “the beloved,” as, when given to idols, they would be of no benefit. The words, <span style="color:#008080">ykt[r</span> <span style="color:#008080">yk</span>, are literally, “when thy evil *is* against me.” It is a similar mode of expression with <span style="color:#008080">ymq</span>, “those who rise up against me,” (2 Samuel 22:40.) Though it was an evil against God, yet they exulted in what they did. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC17</span> This clause is difficult. The versions give no assistance. The word <span style="color:#008080">hlwmh</span>, or rather <span style="color:#008080">hlmh</span>, is rendered *“circumcision”* by the *Septuagint, “speech’* by the *Vulgate, “decree”* by the *Syriac, “tumult”* by our version, and clamor by *Blayney.* It occurs only in one other place, Ezekiel l:24; where it stands in apposition with the *“voice* of the Almighty,” which means there, and often elsewhere, “ thunder:” and its meaning there is evidently the breaking of thunder or the thunderclap. It comes from <span style="color:#008080">lm</span>, to cut, to break, to shiver. Then the noun is literally breaking, or crashing; it is the bursting noise of thunder. The other difficulty is <span style="color:#008080">hyl[</span>, rendered “upon it” in our version as well as in the early versions: but “it” is feminine in Hebrew, and “of it” after branches is masculine, the same gender with “olive.” None have accounted for this anomaly. *Blayney* has indeed made the word a participle to agree with *fire, —*  “a fire mounting upwards;” but this can hardly be admitted. I would render the verse thus, — <span style="color:#000080">An olive, flourishing, beautiful in fruit, in form, Hath Jehovah</span> <span style="color:#000080">called thy name: At the sound of a great thunderclap, — Kindled</span> <span style="color:#000080">hath he a fire by it,; And shivered have been its branches.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The verb for “kindled” is in Hiphil, and “by it” is the “thunderclap,” which is feminine, and “its” is the “olive,” which is masculine. *Houbigant* refers this passage to thunder.</p>

The past tense is used for the future. He compares the nation to a flourishing tree, and then he speaks of its destraction by a fire kindled by the breaking of a thunder: the fire is the lightning. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC18</span> It is literally “evil.” There is here a striking instance of the same word used in two different senses — the *evil* of punishment and the *evil* of sin. The verse is thus, —

<!-- PAGE 1185 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">And Jehovah of hosts, who hath planted thee, Hath spoken against</span> <span style="color:#000080">thee an evil, For the evil of the house of Israel and of the house of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Judah · Which they have done for themselves, By provoking me in</span> <span style="color:#000080">burning incense to Baal.</span></p>

“For the evil,” etc., is unintelligibly rendered by *Blayney,* “In prosecution of the evil,” etc.; <span style="color:#008080">llgb</span> is a preposition, and is so rendered in all the early versions and the *Targum:* it is also so found in many other parts of Scripture. “Which they have done? etc., may be rendered, Which they have procured for themselves; for the verb <span style="color:#008080">hç[</span> may sometimes be thus rendered. See Genesis 12:5; Genesis 31:1. But “which” refers to the first “evil,” of which

God had spoken, the evil of punishment — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC19</span> *Calvin* connects this verse with the foregoing, but most with what follows. The first verb in the *Septuagint* is a prayer, “Lord, make known to me, and I shall know.” The *Syriac* and *Arabic* are the same. The *Vulgate* takes the verb in the second person, “O Lord, thou hast made known,” etc. *Venema* seems to agree in part with *Calvin;* he connects the first clause with the foregoing, and the second with the following verse; and this appears to be the best construction. Then the <span style="color:#008080">w</span> is “when,” as it may be rendered when followed as here by <span style="color:#008080">za</span>, “then,” — <span style="color:#000080">When Jehovah made me to know, so that I knew</span> <span style="color:#000080">*these things;*</span> <span style="color:#000080">Then</span> <span style="color:#000080">thou didst shew me their doings.</span>

That is, when Jehovah made known to him what he had previously related, he then shewed to him also the doings, or the purposes, of the men of Anathoth, which he afterwards more particularly mentions. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC20</span> All the early versions, and the *Targum* render <span style="color:#008080">ãwla</span> as a participle or an adjective, — “<span style="color:#0000d4">άκακον</span>, innocent,” by the Septuagint; “mansuetus, meek,” by the *Vulgate;* simple, by the *Syriac;* and choice or chosen by the *Targum.* The word used as a verb means to teach, to train, to guide; and it seems here to be a passive participle, taught, trained, and may be rendered here docile, meek or innocent, — <span style="color:#000080">But I — as a meek lamb led to be killed</span> <span style="color:#000080">*was I*</span> <span style="color:#000080">And I knew not, that</span> <span style="color:#000080">against me they had devised devices.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1186 -->

The *Septuagint*  render the last words “they have thought an evil thought,” and, “I knew not,” is connected with the former line thus, — <span style="color:#000080">But I, as an innocent lamb led to be slain, I knew not: Against me</span> <span style="color:#000080">have they thought an evil thought.</span>

But the construction in the other versions, and in the *Targum,* is according to the former rendering. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC21</span> But the best meaning is that given by the *Syriac,* and has been adopted in our version, and by *Gataker, Venema, Henry, Horsley, Scott,* and *Adam Clarke, —*  “Let us destroy the tree with its fruit;” that is, the Prophet and his prophecy. “In this case,” says *Horsley,* “the man is the tree; his doctrine the fruit.” But there seems to be an allusion in the words to “the olive” mentioned in Jeremiah 11:16, which was threatened with destruction: and Jeremiah’s enemies, adopting his simile, by way of irony apply it to himself: “Well, thou comparest us to an olive devoted to ruin; we shall now deal with thee accordingly: thou art a tree, and we shall cut thee down and destroy thee and all the fruit thou bearest.”

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The whole verse I would render as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">19. And I — as a meek lamb led to be killed</span> <span style="color:#000080">*was I*</span> <span style="color:#000080">And I knew not</span> <span style="color:#000080">that against me they had devised</span> <span style="color:#000080">*these*</span> <span style="color:#000080">devices: — “Let us destroy</span> <span style="color:#000080">the tree with its fruit, Yea, let us cut him down from the land of the</span> <span style="color:#000080">living; And his name, let it be remembered no more.” —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC22</span> The beginning of the verse is differently rendered: “O Lord,” in the vocative case, by the *Septuagint,* the *Vulgate,* and the *Syriac;* “The Lord,” by the *Arabic* and *Targum.* All the versions agree as to the imprecation, “May I see — <span style="color:#0000d4">ίδοιμι</span> — videam:” but the *Targum*  has, “I shall see;” and so it is rendered by *Gataker, Venema, Scott,* and *Adam Clarke.* The verb is future, but the future in Hebrew has sometimes the meaning of the optative or the subjunctive, as well as of the imperative. But the future is the most suitable here; for the <span style="color:#008080">W</span> before “Jehovah” will not allow it to be in the vocative case. The verse then would be as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">20.But Jehovah of hosts,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*who art*</span> <span style="color:#000080">a righteous judge, The trier of the</span> <span style="color:#000080">reins and of the heart, I shall see thy vengeance on them; For on</span> <span style="color:#000080">thee have I devolved my cause.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1187 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">“Jehovah of hosts” is a nominative absolute — a form of expression very common in the Prophets. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC23</span> “Emboldened,” says *Blayney,* “as it should seem, by the success of his prayers against the men of Anathoth, the Prophet ventures freely, though with professions of confidence in the divine justice, to expostulate with God concerning the prosperity of wicked men in general, whose punishment he solicits, attesting the mischiefs that were continually brought on the land by their unrestrained wickedness.”

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">I would render the verse thus, — <span style="color:#000080">Righteous art thou, Jehovah; Though I should dispute with thee;</span> <span style="color:#000080">Yet of judgments will I speak to thee, — How</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is it.?*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the way of the</span> <span style="color:#000080">wicked, it prospers; Secure are all the dissemblers of dissimulation.</span></p>

Perhaps the fourth line might be rendered thus, — <span style="color:#000080">Why; the way of the wicked, it prospers.</span>

The order of the words will not admit it to be rendered otherwise. *Blayney* renders the last line as follows: — <span style="color:#000080">At ease are all they who deal very perfidiously.</span>

The last words literally are, “all the cloakers of cloaking,” or, “all the coverers of covering.” But according to the secondary meaning of the word <span style="color:#008080">dgb</span> the phrase would be, “all the dissemblers of dissimulation.” The version of the *Septuagint* is, “all who prevaricate prevarications.” What is meant evidently is, that they were hypocrites, and that by hypocrisy they covered their hypocrisy, — a true and a striking representation. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC24</span> Ovid, Eleg. 8.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC25</span> Ovid, Eleg. 8.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC26</span> The verb is <span style="color:#008080">wkly</span> rendered “proficiunt — proeeed or advance,” by the *Vulgate* and *Syriac.* The *Septuagint* must have read <span style="color:#008080">wdly</span>, as the version is, they have brought forth children, which is wholly inconsistent with the simile of a tree. To “advance in growth,” as *Blayney* renders it, is what is clearly meant. The *Targum* is a paraphrase, and the simile is wholly left out. To “become rich” is the corresponding expression, which gives the meaning. The <span style="color:#008080">µg</span>, which

<!-- PAGE 1188 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">occurs twice, would be better rendered “yea,” as in our version, than “also,” as by *Blaney, —* <span style="color:#000080">Thou hast planted them, yea, they have taken root; They thrive,</span> <span style="color:#000080">yea, they have produced fruit: Nigh art thou to their mouth, But far</span> <span style="color:#000080">from their reins.</span></p>

“They thrive,” is literally “they go on,” that is, after having rooted, or taken root. The “reins” stand for the affections — fear, reverence, love,etc. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC27</span> This verse, according to the tenses of the verbs, is as follows: —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">But thou, Jehovah, thou hast known me; Thou seest me, and triest</span> <span style="color:#000080">my heart towards thee: Pull them out as sheep for the sacrifice,</span> <span style="color:#000080">And set them apart for the day of slaughter.</span></p>

It is evident that “seest,” which is here in the future tense, is to be taken as expressing a present act. It would be so rendered in Welsh, — <span style="color:#000080">Ond ti Jehova, adwaenaist vi; Gweli vi, a phrovi, vy nghalon tuag</span> <span style="color:#000080">atat.</span>

God had known him, he was still seeing him, and approved of his heart before him, as the *Septuagint* express the words. To prove here, or to “try,” means a trial by which a thing is found to be genuine. *Blayney* gives the meaning by a paraphrase, — <span style="color:#000080">Thou canst discern by trial my heart to be with thee. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC28</span> Both *Gataker* and *Venema* regard the meaning of the last clause differently. Here ends the expostulation of Jeremiah; and they consider that he mentions here what his persecutors said of him, that he would not see their end, or their ruin, which he had foretold. Were <span style="color:#008080">yk</span>, as in the first verse, rendered “though,” the connnection would be more natural, — <span style="color:#000080">How long shall mourn the land And the grass of every field wither?</span> <span style="color:#000080">For the evil of those who dwell in it, Swept away has been the</span> <span style="color:#000080">beast and the bird, Though they have said, “Hewill not see our</span> <span style="color:#000080">end.”</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The third line connects better with what follows than with what precedes it; and it is so rendered in the *Syriac.* The word for “beast,”</p>

<!-- PAGE 1189 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">though in a plural form, is used elsewhere as a singular, Psalm 73:22; and so it is here, and so rendered by the *Vulgate* and the *Targum. — Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC29</span> The author of the Targum — the Chaldee Paraphrase. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC30</span> Most commentators agree in the previous exposition, — that a comparison is made between the persecution which Jeremiah experienced from his countrymen at Anathoth, and the persecution he was to expect at *Jerusalem.* So thought the Jewish commentators, *Grotius, Venema, Gataker, Henry, Scott, Adam Clarke,* and *Blayney.* It must. however, be added, that *Jerome* and *Horsley* were of the same opinion with *Calvin:* but the most obvious and natural meaning seems to be the former.

The rendering of *Blayney* is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">If thou hast run with footmen, and they have wearied thee, Then</span> <span style="color:#000080">how wilt thou chafe thyself with horses?</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">More literally, — <span style="color:#000080">If with footmen thou hast run, and they have tired thee, Then how</span> <span style="color:#000080">wilt thou heat thyself with horses?</span></p>

“Horses” may indeed be rendered horsemen, as “feet” in the previous line is rendered footmen. As to the verb “heat thyself,” the versions and the *Targum* differ, but the word in Hebrew is plain enough; it is <span style="color:#008080">hrj</span> to heat, to burn, or to be warm or hot, in Hithpael. To “contend” has been taken from the *Vulgate. — Ed..* <span style="color:#800000">ftC31</span> As in the previous clause, so in this, most interpreters are opposed to *Calvin.* The contrast here is between a quiet state and great troubles. If Jeremiah complained, when among his connections at Anathoth, what could he do when troubles, like the swelling of Jordan, overflowed the land? And this view is confirmed by the verse which follows, —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">*Blayney,* following the *Vulgate,* renders the passage thus, — <span style="color:#000080">And though in the land of peace thou mayest have confidence, Yet</span> <span style="color:#000080">how wilt thou do in the swelling of Jordan?</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">But rather as follows, —</p>

<!-- PAGE 1190 -->

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">And in the land of peace thou art secure; But how wilt thou do in</span> <span style="color:#000080">the swelling of Jordan?</span></p>

That is, “Thou complainest though living secure in a land which enjoys peace and is not harassed with war: what then wilt thou do when the troubles of war shall come over the land like the overflowings of Jordan?” or, according to some, “Thou complainest though living in retirement among thine own people, where thou didst expect rest and peace, what wilt thou do when exposed to the violent persecutions of the great and powerful?” the swelling of Jordan being considered a proverbial expression, designating great and overwhelming troubles. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC32</span> It is necessary to understand <span style="color:#008080">yrja</span> here as meaning “behind,” that is, “behind his back,” as we commonly say; for his friends and relations acted perfidiously, they cried against him in his absence, while they spoke friendly to himself. The verse is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">For even thy brethren and thy father’s house, Even they have</span> <span style="color:#000080">dissembled with thee; Yea, they have cried behind thee vehemently</span> <span style="color:#000080">Believe them not when they speak to thee kind things.</span>

“Vehemently,” or more literally, *“fully;*” <span style="color:#008080">alm</span> is used here adverbially. The versions, except the *Vulgate,* which renders it, “with a full voice,” have not given its meaning, nor the *Targum.* The “multitude” of our version is evidently wrong, distantly derived from the *Septuagint. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC33</span> “My beloved soul” is the version of the *Septuagint, Vulgate,* and *Arabic,* but very improperly; the *Syriac* is “the beloved of my soul.” The three first versions betoken an ignorance of the construction of the Hebrew language. To express their idea, “beloved” must have followed “soul,” and not preceded it. Besides, the word for “beloved” is in the plural number, but used as *delitice* in Latin, to express great affection; and it ought to be rendered, the very dear, or the very beloved, of my soul. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC12</span> The most literal rendering of the verse is as follows, —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">9.Is not my heritage to me a stripped bird of prey? Is there not a</span> <span style="color:#000080">bird of prey around against it? Come, assemble, every beast of the</span> <span style="color:#000080">field; Hasten ye to devour.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1191 -->

The versions and the *Targum* all differ, and are wholly unsatisfactory. Some, as *Venema,* agreeably with our version, retain not the questionary form in the two first lines, and render them thus, — <span style="color:#000080">A stripped bird of prey</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">my heritage to me; A bird of prey</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">around against it.</span>

The meaning is the same; but the <span style="color:#008080">h</span> before “bird of prey,” or rapacious bird, seems to favor the interrogation. The <span style="color:#008080">[wbx</span>, stripped or speckled, is a participle, and not the name of a ravenous bird,” as *Blayney* thinks, is evident from its location, for it follows the word <span style="color:#008080">fy[</span>, a rapacious bird: it would have otherwise preceded it. The *Vulgate* renders it. “discolored — diversely colored,” and the *Syriac* is the same. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC35</span> The *Septuagint* and *Arabic* render the verb as passive in the singular number, “It has been set a desolation.” We may take <span style="color:#008080">hmç</span> as a passive participle, the <span style="color:#008080">w</span> being omitted, with <span style="color:#008080">h</span>, *it*, affixed. Then the verse would run thus, — <span style="color:#000080">11. Set it</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">an utter desolation; It has mourned before me (or, to</span> <span style="color:#000080">me) being utterly desolate: Desolate has been the whole land,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Though no man lays</span> <span style="color:#000080">*it*</span> <span style="color:#000080">to heart.</span>

“Utter desolation” is the meaning, for it is a reduplicate noun. Both the *Vulgate* and the *Targum* connect “being  utterly desolate” with the next line, though not rightly: but both, as well as the *Syriac,* render the first verb, as though it were <span style="color:#008080">hwmç</span> “They have set it.” *Venema* and *Houbigant* render <span style="color:#008080">yl[</span>, in the second line, a preposition, and render the line thus, — <span style="color:#000080">It has mourned on account of desolation. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC36</span> The versions and the *Targum* render the first verb in the past tense, but the second, incorrectly, in the future. The verse is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">12. On all heights in the wilderness have wasters come, For the</span> <span style="color:#000080">sword has for Jehovah devoured; From one end of the land to the</span> <span style="color:#000080">other end of the land No peace</span> <span style="color:#000080">*has been*</span> <span style="color:#000080">to any flesh.</span>

The third line reads better with the last. No doubt, the past, as *Calvin* says, is used for the future. The same is the case in the next verse. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1192 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftC37</span> The *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* and the *Arabic,* render all the verbs in the second person plural, and in the present tense, “Ye sow,” etc.; but the *Vulgate* and *Targum* retain the Hebrew third person and the past tense, except in the third line, “Ye (not they) are ashamed,” etc., which seems to be the correct reading, though not found in any MS., for it is what “your fruits,” or produce, require.

The meaning of being.”wearied,” or sick with labor, is given only by the *Syriac* to the verb <span style="color:#008080">wljn</span>; all the other versions, as well as the *Targum*, give it the idea of “inheriting,” or possessing as an heritage. So *Blayney* renders it, “They have possessed,” etc. The verse then is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">13.They have sown wheats, but thorns have they reaped; They</span> <span style="color:#000080">have got an heritage, but have not succeeded: Yea, ashamed have</span> <span style="color:#000080">you been of your produce, Through the burnlung of the wrath of</span> <span style="color:#000080">Jehovah.</span>

A conversive *vau* before “succeeded” is supplied by many MSS., and by the *Vulgate* and *Syriac.* The way in which *Calvin* accounts for the change of person in the third line is ingenious; but an instance of what he says can hardly be found in one and the same clause. All the versions and the *Targum* regard the verb as <span style="color:#008080">wçbtw</span>, the *tau* only being supplied.

*Venema* takes the verb to be an imperative in the second person plural, and gives this version, — <span style="color:#000080">Therefore be ye ashamed of your fruits, By reason of the heat of</span> <span style="color:#000080">the wrath of Jehovah.</span>

But what the early versions warrant is more consistent with the context, and gives a better meaning. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC38</span> No doubt the people of Israel were often called the heritage of God; but the word heritage means here evidently the land. The version of *Calvin* cannot be admitted; the verb is in Hiphil and must be rendered, “I have caused to inherit;” and so it is rendered in all the versions and *Targum.* The verse runs thus, — <span style="color:#000080">14. Thus saith Jehovah, — As to all my neighbors, Who have done</span> <span style="color:#000080">evil, who have touched the heritage, Which I have caused my</span>

<!-- PAGE 1193 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">people Israel to inherit, — Behold, I will root them up from their</span> <span style="color:#000080">land, And the house of Judah will I root up from the midst of them.</span></p>

There is here a promise of two removals, — that of heathens from the Iand of Canaan, — and that of the Jews from the land of heathens. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC39</span> Rather, “I will turn,” *i.e.,* from the course he had pursued. This is often the meaning of <span style="color:#008080">bwç</span> . It is rendered here adverbially by *Blayney* and others; though it may at times be so rendered, yet not suitably in this place. It means here a change in God’s proceedings: he had plucked them up; but now he will deal differently with them. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC40</span> The verb <span style="color:#008080">dml</span>, to learn, in this verse, has evidently two meanings, as “learn” has in old English. In the first instance, — “If they will learn the ways of my people,” it means what is commonly understood by the term; but, in the second instance, — “As they have learned my people,” it signifies to teaJeremiah Though in English the word is not now used in this sense, yet in Welsh the word still continues to have this double meaning; and the same word, “dysgu,” is used in these two clauses, according to what is done in Hebrew.

There is here a clear instance of <span style="color:#008080">w</span> being rendered “then,” and it cannot be rendered othersise, — “If learning they will learn, etc., then shall they be built up,” etc. In the first clause there is also a striking correspondence between the Welsh and the <span style="color:#008080">µa</span>, — “Os gan ddysgu y dysgant.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC41</span> It is rendered “<span style="color:#0000d4">περίζωμα</span> – a girdle,” by the *Septuagint*; — “lumbare – a garment for the loins,” by the *Vulgate*; — “sudarium – a napkin,” by the *Syriac*; — “cingulum – a girdle,” by the *Targum* and *Arabic*. The Hebrew word never means anything but a girdle or belt, as the verb signifies to surround, to bind.

<!-- PAGE 1194 -->
*Calvin* makes no remark on the command, not to put it in water before he wore it. Various has been the explanation. The view the Rabbins give is inconsistent with the passage, — that it was to be left dirty after wearing, that it might rot the sooner; for the Prophet is bidden, when commanded to wear it, not to wash it. *Grotius* and others think that he was to wear it as made, in its rough state, in order to shew the rude condition of the Jews when God adopted them. *Venema* is of the opinion that in order to shew that is was newly made, and had not been worn by another, nor polluted. *Gataker* says that the purpose was to shew that nothing was to be done by the Prophet to cause the girdle to rot, as wet might have done so, in order to prove that the rottenness proceeded only from the Jews themselves. *Lowth* regards it as intended to teach the Jews their corrupt state by nature, so that it was through favor or grace only that God adopted them; and he refers to Ezekiel 16:4. The last, which is nearly the same with the view of *Grotius*, seems the most suitable. – *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC42</span> Many agree with Calvin that this was a vision and not an actual transaction, such as *Gataker, Lowth, Blayney, Adara Clarke*, &c. *Henry* hesitates, but Scott seems to be strongly in favor of a real transaction. *Bochart* and *Venema* hold also the latter opinion, only they think that <span style="color:#008080">trp</span> here does not mean “Euphrates,” but Ephrata, that is, Bethlehem, in Judea; but this cannot be maintained. Lowth refers to an instance where a vision is related as a fact, without any mention being made that it was a vision, that is, Genesis 15:5: God brought Abraham forth and shewed to him the stars; and yet it appears from Jeremiah 13:12 that the sun was not set. *Blayney* remarks, that “the same supposition of a vision must be admitted in other cases, particularly Jeremiah 25:15-29.” *Gataker* refers to similar instances in Ezekiel 8:3; Ezekiel 11:24. It was most probably a vision; and the Prophet related to the people what God had in a supernatural way exhibited to him. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC43</span> It is strangely rendered “reproach — <span style="color:#0000d4">u[zrin</span>,” by the *Septuagint,* but “pride” by the *Vulgate, —* “the haughty ones,” by the *Syriac, —* “insolence” by the *Arabic,* and “strength” by the *Targum. Blayney* agrees with *Calvin* and renders it “excellency,” and *Horsley,* “glory.” — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC44</span> These words are in the *Septuagint* and the *Vulgate* put in apposition with the last words of the preceding verse; but in the *Syriac* and *Targum* they form the nominative case to the verb “shall be,” as in our version, near the end of the verse, the <span style="color:#008080">w</span> before it being omitted; but the simpler mode of construction is to consider the substantive verb, is, to be understood in the first clause; then the whole verse would run thus, —

<!-- PAGE 1195 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">This</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">a wicked people, Who refuse to hear my words, Who walk</span> <span style="color:#000080">in the resolutions of their own heart, And walk after foreign gods,</span> <span style="color:#000080">To serve them and to bow down to them; And they shall be as this</span> <span style="color:#000080">girdle, Which will not be good for anything.</span></p>

On “the resolutions,” see vol. 1. “For anything,” the <span style="color:#008080">lk</span> here evidently means “anything,” as it means in some other places “any,” or any one. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC45</span> “Name” means here renown; “praise,” celebrity or commendation; and “glory,” ornament, decoration, or beauty. The three words are found together, though not in exactly the same order, in Deuteronomy 26:19. There the order is, praise, name, and honor, which is rendered here “glory.” See Isaiah 43:21; Isaiah 61:11; Isaiah 63:12. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC46</span> It is not true that the word ever means a bladder, though so rendered by the *Septuagint* and the *Targum.* The *Vulgate* has “laguncula — a little flagon,” and *Syriac* “dolium, — a tub.” It means a jug or jar. *Blayney* has “vessel.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC47</span> With regard to this comparison, *Gataker* says, “A type taken from what they much loved, liked, and looked after; for they loved and looked after the *flagons of wine,* Hosea 3:1; and those prophets best pleased them who prophesied of *wine and strong drink,* Micah 2:11. God therefore sendeth his prophet to them with a prophecy of *wine,* but of other wine than they expected.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftC48</span>  The clause, literally rendered, would convey this meaning, —

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">And the kings who sit for David on his throne.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1196 -->
“For David,” that is, as his representatives. “In David’s stead,” is the rendering of *Gataker* and *Blayney.* The word “even” before “the kings” in our version, is improper; for what follows is not a specification of what is gone before, as “the inhabitants of Jerusalem,” at the end of the verse, is in contrast with “all the inhabitants of this land,” that is, the people of the country*. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC49</span> The word seems to mean shattering or breaking in pieces, and in a secondary sense, scattering, as the effect. The early versions give the latter meaning, scattering, but, as *Calvin* says, inconsistently with the rest of the clause. The *Targum* gives in effect the first sense, “I will cause them to rush, each on his brother.” The word “dash” is the most suitable, or dash to pieces, — <span style="color:#000080">And I will dash them to pieces, each against his brother, Both the</span> <span style="color:#000080">fathers and the sons together, saith Jehovah.</span>

The allusion is to the bottles: they would be broken like brittle vessels, when thrown one against another. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC50</span> The verbs are different, and so *Calvin* renders them in the text; but not here. There is no unanimity in the versions as to these verbs and the one which follows. The first means to be tender so as to relent; the second, to spare so as not to inflict punishment, to connive; and the third, to feel pity or compassion. They may be rendered thus, — <span style="color:#000080">I will not relent, nor will I spare; Nor will I pity, so as not to</span> <span style="color:#000080">destroy them.</span>

The two lines announce the same thing, only the last is stronger and more specific. Pitying or commiserating is stronger than relenting, and not destroying describes the act, while sparing is a general term. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC51</span> The sentence literally is, “From consuming,” or destroying, “them.” The preposition <span style="color:#008080">m</span>*, mem,* here has the force of a negative. It is a sort of an elliptic phrase, which, though understood in the original, yet requires a supplement in a translation, — “I will not pity, *so as to abstain* from consuming them.” But a literal rendering in Welsh would be understood, — <span style="color:#000080">Ae ni resynav rhag eu difetha.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The preposition *“rhag,”* which ordinarily means *from*, signifies here *from not,* which is exactly the Hebrew. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC52</span> This may be rendered more consistently with the context, “For Jehovah speaks,” or *is* speaking: for the *reference* evidently is to what was now addressed to them. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1197 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftC53</span> So all the versions and the *Targum. Gataker* renders it, “Be ye not haughty,” which is no doubt the meaning. The verb means to be high, lofty, or elevated, and so to be elevated as to be haughty, proud. See Isaiah 3:16. Men, creatures of the dust, too high and elevated to hear what God said to them! This is the case still. What a monstrous thing! — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC54</span> All the versions and the *Targum* render the first verb intransitively, “Before it grows dark :” but *Montanus, Pagninus, Piscator* and *Junius and Tremellius,* give it a transitive meaning, as *Calvin* does, and no doubt correctly, for it is in Hiphil, “Before he causes *or* brings darkness;” or it may be rendered, “Before he makes it dark.” *Blayney* follows the early versions, but *Gataker, Lowth,* and *Venema,* the latter versions; and the conclusion of the verse confirms, as *Calvin* says, this meaning. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC55</span> This is a mistake, the preposition is <span style="color:#008080">l[</span> which means *on, upon,* etc..

Our version of this sentence is in accordance with the early versions: it is indeed literally the *Septuagint* and the *Vulgate.* Yet it is not the original. The verb is in Hithpael, and means to strike or smite together, or against one another. The literal rendering is the following, — <span style="color:#000080">Before your feet smite one against the other, On the mountains of</span> <span style="color:#000080">gloominess</span> <span style="color:#000080">*(i.e.*</span> <span style="color:#000080">gloomy mountains.)</span>

It is true the word for “gloominess” means sometimes the twilight; but here it seems to signify a state somewhat dark or obscure. To wander and to stumble on gloomy mountains betokens the miserable condition of fugitives: and this is what is meant here. See Jeremiah 16:16; Ezekiel 7:16. Then what follows might be thus rendered, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">When ye shall look anxiously for light, Then will he make it the</span> <span style="color:#000080">shadow of death, He will turn it to thick darkness.</span></p>

When two *vaus* occur in a sentence, they may often be rendered *when* and *then.* The change proposed as to the last verb is not at all necessary. Literally it is, “He will set it (to be) for thick darkness.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC56</span> The whole verse may be thus rendered, —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">But if ye will not hear, weep in secret places Will my soul, on</span> <span style="color:#000080">account of your haughtiness; Yea, bewailing it will bewail, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">pour down will mine eye the tear, When taken captive is the flock</span> <span style="color:#000080">of Jehovah.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1198 -->
The word for “haughtiness,” <span style="color:#008080">hwg</span>, is rendered “insolence” by the *Septuagint* and *Arabic;* “pride” by the *Vulgate,* and “affliction” by the *Syriac.* The word is commonly derived from <span style="color:#008080">hag</span>, to swell, to be high, to be elated. It is found in this sense in two other places, Job 33:17, and Daniel 4:37; and in a good sense, elevation, in Job 22:29. It seems to be a contraction, in full <span style="color:#008080">hwag</span>. See Psalm 36:12; Proverbs 29:23. This being the meaning of the word, the view of *Calvin* cannot be admitted. There is an evident reference to what is said in Jeremiah 13:15, “Be ye not lifted up,” or, “be ye not haughty.” The cause of his weeping was their haughtiness in not hearing God speaking to them.- *Ed.*

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#800000">ftC57</span> So *Gataker* and *Lowth;* and they refer to 2 Kings 24:12, and to</p>

Jeremiah 22:26. From this circumstance it is gathered that this prophecy was delivered in the short reign of that king, which lasted only three months.

The word “queen,” in our version, is rendered “mistress *or* lady — domina,” by *Calvin,* but *“potentates”* by the *Septuagint, Syriac,* and *Arabic;* “governess — dominatrix,” by the *Vulgate;* and “queen” by the *Targum.* The word means governess; it is rendered “mistress” in Genesis 16:4, 8; “lady” in Isaiah 47:5, 7; and “queen” in 2 Kings 10:13. *— Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC58</span> There is an oversight here; the passage referred to is in Micah 6:1; nor is it a right view of it. See vol. 3 on the Minor Prophets, p. 328. — .*Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC59</span> All the early versions render the words, “Fallen from your head has the crown of your glory.” Our version is that of *Montanus.* If <span style="color:#008080">m</span> be a formative, then the word, in every instance in which it occurs, means bolsters or pillows, things for the head to rest on. The word for head has commonly a masculine termination in the plural number; but here it is feminine. The most literal rendering is the following: — <span style="color:#000080">For bring down from your heads will he the crown of your glory.</span>

The latter words mean “your glorious crown,” the expression being an Hebraism.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Our common version, as *Blayney* observes, violates grammar; for the gender of the verb <span style="color:#008080">dry</span>, (which, the same author thinks, ought to be <span style="color:#008080">drwy</span>, future in Hiphil) is masculine, while the noun made its nominative is feminine. — *Ed*.</p>

<!-- PAGE 1199 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftC60</span> The ancient versions render these last words of the verse in the same way with our version and that of *Calvin;* but the Hebrew, as *Blayney* remarks, is not rightly rendered, though he unnecessarily makes <span style="color:#008080">hlk</span> a verb, and according to his construction it ought to be <span style="color:#008080">htlk</span>; and he does not satisfactorily account for the last word, <span style="color:#008080">µymwlç</span>. The literal version I regard to be the following: — <span style="color:#000080">The transmigration of Judah h</span><span style="color:#000080">*as been*</span> <span style="color:#000080">entire, — The transmigration</span> <span style="color:#000080">of retributions.</span>

The past time, as in the beginning of the verse, is to be used, though it is used for the future. The word <span style="color:#008080">µymwlç</span>, is never found in an adverbial sense; and indeed it is found only once elsewhere as here, in the plural number, Isaiah 34:8; but thrice in this sense in the singular number, Deuteronomy 32:35; Hosea 9:7; Micah 7:3. The *Targum* favors this rendering, as it retains the idea of retribution. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC61</span> May not the queen regent, or governess, mentioned with the king in Jeremiah 13:18, be here meant? Sovereigns are called shepherds, and hence “flock” and “sheep” are here mentioned. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC62</span> The best rendering of this clause is as follows: —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">For thou hast taught them to be</span> <span style="color:#000080">over the leaders in chief.</span></p>

It is the feminine gender that is still used; and the queen or governess may be addressed as the representative of the ruling power in the land. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC63</span> The verb is here in the singular, and is followed by a nominative in the plural; the very same anomaly exists in *Welsh.* The line would be literally the same in that language, — <span style="color:#000080">Pam y digwyddodd i mi y pethau hyn?</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">But if “these things” preceded the verb, it would be in the plural. — *Ed*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC64</span> The three last lines are as follows: —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">For the number of thine iniquity Discovered have been thy skirts,</span> <span style="color:#000080">Violently stripped off have been thy heels.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1200 -->

“Skirts” here stand for the parts covered by them, and “heels” for the sandals which were worn. Both the *Septuagint* and the *Vulgate* mention the parts, and not skirts — “the hinder parts,” “the uncomely parts,” but they retain the word “heels.” The metonomy exists, no doubt, as to both. The *Syriac* has “skirts” and “ankles.” The *Targum* gives the meaning, “confusion” and “ignominy.” The past time is used for the future. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC65</span> The word in Hebrew is *“Cushite;”* and many learned men contend that the “Ethiopian” is not meant, though all the early versions so render it except the *Syriac,* which has “Indian.” *Blayney* agrees with *Bochart* and others in thinking that the Cushites were the inhabitants of Arabia, on the borders of the Red Sea, and he refers in proof of this to 2 Chronicles 21:16. The skin is not said here to be black, but it was no doubt of a particular color, different from that of the Jews. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC66</span>”Panther,” <span style="color:#0000d4">πάρδαλις</span> — pardus, is the rendering of the *Septuagint* and the other versions. The word rendered “spots,” found only here, is translated “varieties” by the *Septuagint* and *Vulgate,* but “spots” by the *Syriac* and *Targum. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC67</span> Neither this sentence nor the preceding is put interrogatively in the *Septuagint,* the *Syriac,* and the *Vulgate,* but in this way, — “ If the Ethiopian,” etc.; “Even so can ye,” etc. The *Arabic* and the *Targum* have both sentences in an interrogative form, and more consistently with the Hebrew. *Blayney* renders the first part interrogatively, as in our version, but not the second, and he gives a meaning to the second part which the original will not bear, and which is not countenanced by any of the versions. The most literal version is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">Can the Cushite change his skin, Or the panther his spots ? —</span> <span style="color:#000080">Also ye, can ye do good, Who have learned evil?</span>

The future tense in Hebrew ought often to be rendered potentially, and sometimes subjunctively. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1201 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftC68</span> Our version begins with *“therefore,”* giving this meaning to <span style="color:#008080">w</span>, *vau,* but *Gataker* considers this verse as connected with the 22d, and regards the 23d as parenthetic; and then he renders the *vau* “and.” The literal rendering of the latter part is, “Passing to the wind of the desert,” that is, the stubble which is exposed to that violent wind. The meaning may be thus given, — <span style="color:#000080">And I will scatter them like the stubble That is subject to the wind</span> <span style="color:#000080">of the desert.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">To pass over to a thing is to become within its range, or to its possession. The sense would be given by the following version, — <span style="color:#000080">That is carried away by the wind of the desert.</span></p>

The meaning is not what the *Septuagint* give, “carried by the wind to the desert;” nor what the *Vulgate* presents, “carried by the wind in the desert;” but what is meant is, “the wind of the desert,” or, as *Calvin* says, the south wind. When the stubble was exposed to that, it is carried away with the greatest violence: such would be the scattering of the Jews. — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC69</span> It may be thus rendered, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">This thy lot</span> <span style="color:#000080">*is*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the share of thy measures From me, saith Jehovah.</span></p>

The “lot” was the scattering threatened in the previous verse. “The share of thy measures,” is a Hebrew idiom for “a measured share,” or “a measured portion,” as rendered by *Blayney.* Some say that “measures” are mentioned, because the length and breadth were included. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC70</span> It is better to render <span style="color:#008080">rça</span> here “because” or for, according to all the versions and the *Targum,* than “who,” as by *Blayney. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC71</span> This is no doubt the meaning. See Nahum 3:5. The verb means to strip off, so as to make bare. The threatening is, to strip off the skirts and throw them over the face; and this is the rendering of the *Syriac.* Probably the most literal rendering would be the following, — <span style="color:#000080">And I also will strip (or roll) up thy skirts over thy face.</span>

The versions all differ, but the *Septuagint* convey this idea. *Blayney’s* uncovering “thy skirts before thee,” imparts no meaning. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC72</span> In all the versions, as well as in the *Targum,* the words in the beginning of this verse, as far as “whoredom,” are read in apposition with “shame” in the preceding verse, and what follows as connected with the verb “I have seen,” in this manner, —

<!-- PAGE 1202 -->

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">On hills in the field have I seen thy abominations.</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">Another arrangement, suggested by *Gataker,* is more consonant with the Hebrew style, by considering the substantive verb to be understood in the first clause, as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">27. Thy adulteries and thy neighings, The scheming of thy</span> <span style="color:#000080">fornication,</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Have been*</span> <span style="color:#000080">on hills in the field; I have seen thine</span> <span style="color:#000080">abominations.</span></p>

The word <span style="color:#008080">tmz</span>, which I render “scheming,” is from a verb which means to devise, to contrive, to scheme, to plot. It is rendered “wickedness” by the *Vulgate,* “alienation” by the *Septuagint,* “fornication” by the *Syriac,* and “design” or counsel by the *Targum.* It never means “lewdness.” It seems to mean here the contrivances and devices formed by those given to fornication. *Blayney* considers it a verb in the second person: he connects the first line with the preceding verse, and renders thus what follows, — <span style="color:#000080">Thou hast devised thy whoredom upon the hills, In the fields I</span> <span style="color:#000080">have seen thine abominations.</span>

The simplicity of this order recommends it, but the former seems preferable. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC73</span> The meaning seems to be right, but it is better to construe <span style="color:#008080">yrja</span>, “after,” with these words, — <span style="color:#000080">Woe to thee, Jerusalem! thou wilt not be cleansed</span> <span style="color:#000080">After what time</span> <span style="color:#000080">*wilt it*</span> <span style="color:#000080">yet</span> <span style="color:#000080">*be?*</span>

Literally it may be rendered, “After when yet ?” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC74</span> The *Septuagint* express it in one,word, “<span style="color:#0000d4">ἀζροχία</span> — the want of rain;” the *Vulgate,* by words of dryness, or drought: and the *Syriac,* by “defect of rain.” We may take “words” here in the sense of effects; so we may render the Hebrew, “concerning the effects of restraints;” and the last word is put in the plural number because there was a twofold restraint, — that of the heavens from rain, and that of the earth from producing fruit. The “effects” of these restraints are described in the following verses. — *Ed*.

<!-- PAGE 1203 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftC75</span> There is a little inadvertence here: “iron,” in this text, is applied to heaven, and “brass” to the earth, — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC76</span> The versions *connect* the two verbs with gates: and if we take “gates” metonymically for those who attended them, the meaning will be evident. We may then render the verse thus, — <span style="color:#000080">Mourned hath Judah, And her gates, they have languished; Grieved</span> <span style="color:#000080">have they for the land; And the cry of Jerusalem hath ascended.</span>

In the gates was the court of justice; there the chief men or governors assembled. The languishing belonged, not to the gates, but to those who attended them, and so the grief or lamentation. The first meaning of the verb is to be dark, to be black, but it is used to signify extreme grief or lamentation. See Psalms 35:14; 38:6; Jeremiah 8:21. As light denotes joy, so darkness is a symbol of grief or mourning. We use a similar kind of metonymy, when we say, “The court is in mourning.” The *Septuagint* render the verse thus, — <span style="color:#000080">Mourned hath Judah, And her gates have been emptied, And have</span> <span style="color:#000080">become dark for the land; And the shout of Jerusalem hath</span> <span style="color:#000080">ascended.</span>

*Blayney’s* version of the third line is as follows, — <span style="color:#000080">They are in deep mourning for the land.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The *Targum* paraphrases the verb thus, — “Their faces are covered with blackness.” *— Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC77</span> The persons here mentioned are called by the *Septuagint* “chieftains – <span style="color:#0000d4">μεγιστᾶνες</span>,” and “young men — <span style="color:#0000d4">touv νεωτέρους</span>;” by the *Vulqate,* “the elder ones — majores,” and “the younger ones — minores;” by the *Syriac,* “the chiefs,” and “the common sort;” and by the *Targum,* “chief men,” and “subjects.” The first word is well expressed in our version, “nobles,” — the illustrious; and the most suitable word for the others is “menials;” they were the servants. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC78</span> would render the verse thus, —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">3.When their nobles sent their menials for water, They came to the</span> <span style="color:#000080">reservoirs, they found no water; They brought back their vessels</span> <span style="color:#000080">empty: They were ashamed and confounded, And they covered</span> <span style="color:#000080">their heads.</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1204 -->

The word I render “reservoirs” means literally arches or vaults. They were places arched over to preserve water. *Parkhurst* thinks that the reservoirs made by King Hezekiah are intended, 2 Chronicles 32:30. That the verb <span style="color:#008080">bç</span> has the meaning of bringing back is evident from Isaiah 52:8; and this is according to the *Vulgate* and the *Septuagint* in this place. *Gataker* and *Venema* think that the shame and confusion refer to the nobles, and not to the servants. This verse speaks of Jerusalem, the last mentioned in the former verse; and what follows refers to Judea, spoken of in the former part according to the usual manner of the prophets. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC79</span> The three foregoing verses I render as follows, —

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">4.On account of the ground being cracked, As there has been no</span> <span style="color:#000080">rain in the land, Ashamed were the husbandmen, They covered</span> <span style="color:#000080">their heads:</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">5. When also the hind</span> <span style="color:#000080">*was*</span> <span style="color:#000080">in the field, It brought forth young, and</span> <span style="color:#000080">it was forsaken, Because there was no grass: 6. And the wild asses,</span> <span style="color:#000080">they stood on the cliffs; They drew in the wind like serpents; Fail</span> <span style="color:#000080">did their eyes, Because there was no herbage —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC80</span> All the versions connect “Jehovah” with the next words; and so do *Veema, Gataker,* and *Blayney.* The particle <span style="color:#008080">µa</span> if, or though, is omitted by the *Septuagint* and the *Arabic;* but is retained by the *Vulgate, Syriac,* and the *Targum.* It may be rendered verily, or truly, — <span style="color:#000080">Verily, our perversities, they have responded against us.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">The word <span style="color:#008080">ˆw[</span> means perverse or headstrong wickedness. There is an allusion in responding to a trial. “They have stood against us,” is the *Septuagint.* See Job 15:6. — *Ed.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC81</span> The latter part may be thus rendered, —

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">Jehovah! deal</span> <span style="color:#000080">*with us*</span> <span style="color:#000080">for thy name’s sake: For many have been our</span> <span style="color:#000080">defections, Against thee have we sinned.</span></p>

The *Syriac* renders fitly the first line, — <span style="color:#000080">O Lord, spare us on account of thy name. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1205 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftC82</span> The word <span style="color:#008080">µhdn</span>, rendered “astonished,” is only found here; it is evidently a Niphal participle, and rendered, by the *Septuagint* “sleeping — <span style="color:#0000d4">ὑπνῶ</span>,” — by the *Vulgate,* “wandering — vagus,” — by the *Siyriac,* “weak — imbceillis,” — by *Montanus* and *Paginius,* “astonished.” *Parkhurst,* after *Grotius,* derives it from an Arabic verb, which means to “come upon one unexpectedly,” or to overwhelm, and renders it overwhelmed, astonied. It may then be rendered, surprised. *Grotius* says, that it means a precipitant person, coming to the aid of one in danger, and not capable of delivering him.

As in the former instance, “the sojourner” and “the traveler” are the same, only what is said of the latter is more specific; so it seems to be here: the man, taken by surprise, is only farther described as one who is not able on that account to save. The two verses may be thus rendered — <span style="color:#000080">8.The hope of Israel! his Savior in time of distress! Why art thou</span> <span style="color:#000080">like a sojourner in the land? Or like a traveler turning aside to pass</span> <span style="color:#000080">the night? 9.Why art thou like one taken by surprise — Like a man</span> <span style="color:#000080">who is not able to save? Yet thou</span> <span style="color:#000080">*art*</span> <span style="color:#000080">in the midst of us, Jehovah;</span> <span style="color:#000080">And thy name, on us is it called: Do not forsake us</span><span style="color:#000080">*. — Ed.*</span> <span style="color:#800000">ftC83</span> The <span style="color:#008080">ˆk</span> *so,* before “loved,” is not well accounted for, nor is it given in any of the versions. The previous complaint was that God was like a “traveler” in the land, who made no stay: the answer given is, “so have ye been; ye have loved to wander here and there.” It is an ironical retort. The verse may be thus rendered, — <span style="color:#000080">Thus saith Jehovah of this people, — “So</span> <span style="color:#000080">have they loved to</span> <span style="color:#000080">wander, Their feet have they not restrained.” And Jehovah has not</span> <span style="color:#000080">been pleased with them; He will now remember their iniquity, And</span> <span style="color:#000080">he will visit their sin. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC84</span> As it is a participle in Hiphil, preceded by a pronoun, it ought to be rendered causatively, — <span style="color:#000080">But with the sword, and with famine, and with pestilence, Will I</span> <span style="color:#000080">cause them to be condemned. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<!-- PAGE 1206 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftC85</span> “Alas!” is commonly the meaning of this exclamation, being an expression of grief rather than of astonishment. “Ah!” is the *Vulgate,* —  “Oh!” the *Septuagint.* It is rendered “Alas!” by *Blayney. — Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC86</span> Or, “constant peace — pacem firmam.” It rather means “true or real peace,” literally, “the peace of truth.” The version of the *Septuagint* is “truth and peace,” — of the *Vulate* and *Targum,* “true peace,” and of the *Syriac,* “peace and security.” — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC87</span> I render the verse as follows, —

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">14. And Jehovah said to me, — Falsehood do the prophets</span> <span style="color:#000080">prophesy in my name; I have not sent them, nor given them a</span> <span style="color:#000080">command, Nor have I spoken to them: A vision of the fidsehood</span> <span style="color:#000080">and divination, And vanity and delusion of their own heart, Do</span> <span style="color:#000080">these of themselves prophesy to you.</span></p>

God had not *sent* them, the final act; he had given them *no command* or commission, the preceding act; he had not *spoken* to them, the first act. God first speaks, then gives a commission, and afterwards sends forth his servants. The vision the false prophets had was that of the falsehood of their own heart, of the divination, of the vanity, and of the delusion of their own heart. Such seems to be the meaning given by the *Septuagint* and the *Vulgate.* It was the lying vision of their own heart, it was the divination or the presage, the vanity, and the delusion of their own heart. The word for “prophesy” in the last line is in *Hitthpael;* and hence “of themselves” is added.

*Blayney* gives a different view; his version is, — <span style="color:#000080">A false vision, and divination, and vanity, And the guile of their</span> <span style="color:#000080">own heart, do these prophesy unto you.</span>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">He considers “a false vision” to be an imaginary revelation; “divination,” to be something discovered by that art; “vanity,” to be the oracular response of an idol; and “guile,” to be the fraudulent suggestion of their own heart.</p>

But the simplest exposition is what I have stated: The vision, being that of their own heart, was false; it was their own divination or prognostication; it was worthless, vain, and empty; it was the effect of their own delusion. This was the character of what they prophesied. We may render the words thus, —

<!-- PAGE 1207 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">The false vision and the divination And the vanity and the delusion</span> <span style="color:#000080">of their own heart, Do these of themselves prophesy to you. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed.*</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC88</span> These two verses are differently connected by some: the words, “these prophets,” at the end of the fifteenth verse, are joined with the “the people” in the next verse; and this construction is evidently the best, — <span style="color:#000080">15. Therefore, thus saith Jehovah, — As to the prophets who</span> <span style="color:#000080">prophesy in my name, (Though I have not sent them, yet they say,</span> <span style="color:#000080">— The sword and the famine shall not be in this land) By the</span> <span style="color:#000080">sword and by the famine shall they perish:</span>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#000080">16. These prophets, and the people, to whom they prophesy, Shall</span> <span style="color:#000080">be cast out into the streets of Jerusalem, On account of the famine</span> <span style="color:#000080">and the sword; And there</span> <span style="color:#000080">*will be*</span> <span style="color:#000080">none to bury them —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Neither*</span> <span style="color:#000080">them, nor their wives, nor their sons, nor their daughters; Thus will</span> <span style="color:#000080">I pour upon them their own wickedness.</span></p>

The preceding connection is favored by the *Septuagint*  and the *Arabic,* but the other versions do not join the “prophets” and the “people” together. “Their own wickedness” is “their own evils” in the *Septuagint, —*  “their own evil” in the *Vulgate, —* and “their own wickedness” in the *Syriac.* If rendered “wickedness,” then it is a metonymy for the fruit or effect of wickedness; if “evil” then the meaning is, the evil due to them. — *Ed*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftC89</span> More consistent with the character of the tiebrew is to render the verse thus, — <span style="color:#000080">17. And thou shalt say to them this word, — Pour down shall my</span> <span style="color:#000080">eyes the tear Night and day, and shall not cease; For great</span> <span style="color:#000080">*has been*</span> <span style="color:#000080">the breach, Broken has been the virgin of the daughter of my</span> <span style="color:#000080">people; The smiting has been very grievous.</span>

The event, though future, is represented as having past; for he relates a vision. The “daughter” is not in apposition but in construction with “virgin.” *Vitringa* says, that a state, or a kingdom, is often called a virgin in the prophets. It is rendered here “kingdom” by the *Targum.* See Isaiah 37:22; 47:1; Jeremiah 31:21; 46:11.

<!-- PAGE 1208 -->

“Those cities,” says *Lowth,* “are called virgins, which never came into a conqueror’s hands.” Jerusalem was in this sense a virgin. He says further, “The dissolution of the body politic is called a *breach,* in allusion to the breaking of the limbs of the human body.” The “smiting,” or the stroke, was “very grievous,” because the body politic, or the state, was shattered into pieces.” — *Ed.* <span style="color:#800000">ftC90</span> I take the words before “sword” and “famine” to be nouns substantive, — “the piercings of the sword,” and “the wastings of the famine,” — <span style="color:#000080">If I go out to the field, Then behold the piercings of the sword; And</span> <span style="color:#000080">if I enter the city, Then behold the wastings of the famine. —</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Ed*</span><span style="color:#000080">.</span>

<span style="color:#800000">ftC91</span> *Venema*  agrees with *Calvin* as to the meaning of the latter part of the verse: it is indeed the only one that comports with the context; the other explanations are quite foreign to it.
